# Open Animate — Feature Spec & Roadmap

Oct 18, 2026 · @Mary

## Overview

Open Animate is a private, Windows-only desktop app for 2D cut-out and vector animation: Moho's animation model inside Blender's interface and editing habits, written like Blender in C++ with an embedded Python API.

**What it takes from each**

| From Moho | From Blender |
| --- | --- |
| Action bones (Moho's smart bones) | Fast, lag-free viewport and rendering |
| Simple layer system: groups, bones, switch layers | Mirror modifier for armatures and drawings |
| Masking | Grease Pencil-style layer masks |
| Vector drawing and point tools | Free 3D view outside the camera; imported meshes |
| Bone binding and rigging tools | Modular editors with split views |
|  | Layout and theme; a better timeline |
|  | Modal tools and shortcuts: G move, E extrude |
|  | Custom bone shapes |

**Guiding principles**

- **Moho's data model, Blender's interaction model.** Layers, bones and action bone actions behave like Moho; tools, keymaps and editors feel like Blender.
- **Everything is an operator.** Every action is a named command that keymaps, menus, undo and Python all share. One that changes nothing leaves no undo step (a step equal to the one before is skipped), and one refused changes nothing at all; undo and redo leave the frame shown and the timeline's switches (Auto Keying, Time Together) as they are.
- **Python is first-class.** Tools, panels and add-ons can be written in Python, the way Moho tools are Lua scripts.
- **Small first.** Each milestone ships something usable before the next one starts.

**Not in scope:** public release, macOS and Linux builds, 3D modelling, sculpting, physics simulation, a node-based compositor, or Moho file compatibility.

**Sources:** the Moho scripting API docs scraped from mohoscripting.com, community and built-in Moho Lua tool scripts, and the Blender source tree, all in the project folder. The app is private, so Blender's GPL license only matters if it is ever distributed.

## Core concepts

The data model follows Moho: a document holds a tree of layers, almost every property is an animated channel, and actions are alternative timelines stored inside those channels. Action bones fall out of that design almost for free.

**Document.** Frame range, frame rate, canvas size (the Document tab's Resolution: W and H with a link button between them; linked, changing one changes the other, keeping the proportion they had when linked; saved with the file; Python: document.resolution_linked), background colour, a 3D camera (track, zoom, roll, pan/tilt channels), timeline markers, a shared style library and layer comps (saved sets of visible layers). Frame 0 is the rigging frame: the rest pose, never rendered.

**Setup frame (frame 0).** Open Animate keeps Moho's model: frame 0 holds the setup pose, and there are no separate rest and pose modes. Action bones need this, because an action bone action's effect is measured from its frame-0 values. Blender's clear sense of mode is added on top:

- **Setup toggle** in the viewport header, with a shortcut: it jumps to frame 0, and pressing it again returns to the frame you were on.
- **Clear signals on frame 0:** a red border on the viewport and the timeline (and a red playhead), a "Setup" label in the header, and frame 0 drawn apart at the start of the timeline.
- **Rigging tools only work on frame 0**, as in Moho: adding and deleting bones (X on another frame deletes no bone: in the Motion Path tool, with nothing on the paths picked, it would take the bone being animated), binding points and layers, bone strength. Elsewhere they are greyed out with the tooltip "Switch to Setup (frame 0)". Each of the toolbar's boxes has its tools for every frame, then under a bar its frame-0 tools (Draw, Shape, Split Curve, Weight Select, Weight Paint), greyed out on other frames with the reason ("Setup frame only: press Tab to go to frame 0"); a frame-0 tool picked stays picked there, greyed out, and a click with it (or its shortcut) says so (see Viewport toolbar).
- **Auto-keying never applies on frame 0**, because changes there edit the setup pose and carry into every frame.
- **Keys are changes from the setup pose**, as Blender's pose bones and shape keys, Spine's deform keys and Harmony's deformers keep them (Moho keeps own values): a bone's keys hold its Transform (along the bone's rest axes) and Angle; a point's its Transform, applied before bones bend the drawing, so a keyed shape follows the bones; a handle's its Handle Angle and Length (the turn, and the length added), so a handle swings along an arc between keys. Away from frame 0 the Properties editor shows these, 0 at rest: the Bone panel's Transform and Angle, the Point and Shape panels' Transform, and the Point panel's Handle In and Handle Out Angle and Length (an aligned partner turns with it). Reshaping frame 0 after animating keeps every pose's motion. A point added to an animated curve (Split Curve) takes its neighbours' motion, blended by distance along the curve (past an end, the end's), so adding points doesn't break the animation. Older files and Moho imports open converted.

**Layers.** Every layer has a name, a stable ID, visibility, lock, label colour, an origin, animated transform channels (position, scale, X/Y/Z rotation, shear, flip), opacity, blend mode, masks, an optional parent bone, a timing offset and a modifier stack. Types for our app:

| Layer type | Holds | Version |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Vector | Points, curves, shapes and styles | v1 |
| Group | Child layers; its masks apply to everything in it | v1 |
| Bone | A group that owns a skeleton and controls its children | v1 |
| Switch | A group that shows one of its children (or none) per frame, chosen by a held key that names the child | v1 |
| Media | Images, image sequences, video and sound, as clips along the timeline; a Guide by default (shown while you work, never rendered) | v1 |
| Mesh 3D | An imported OBJ or glTF mesh placed in the scene | Later |
| Text | Text kept as typed, its letters made into vector shapes (each a shape with the run's look, holes and all): click its text to type on the canvas, the Text tab sets fonts, looks and layout, Convert to Vectors makes ordinary vector layers of it | v1 |
| Particle | Child layers emitted as particles | Maybe |
| Note | A sticky note in the viewport | Maybe |

**Vector drawings.** A drawing is a pool of points, curves through those points, and shapes. A point can sit on several curves, which is how Moho welds lines together. Each point has an animated position, an animated width and an optional bone binding. Every curve point has two Bezier handles, and every handle is free, like Blender's Free handles: stored, and moved only by hand (or with its point). A new point gets its handles once, from Moho's smooth spline (curvature, weight and offset) or, at corners and straight sides, a third of the way toward its neighbours. Align (right-click a selected point or handle) lines a point's handles up and keeps them in line: moving one turns the other. With one handle picked, that handle turns; with the point picked, both turn as far, toward each other, meeting on the line halfway between, each keeping its length (folded back on each other, they line up along the way from the point before to the one after). They stay in line on every frame: keys that would turn them apart (one handle keyed alone, or an action turning the two by different amounts) are made to agree, the handle turned into line taking the other's turn wherever either is keyed, in the timeline and in every action, each keeping its own length (curve_point_align_keys in src/data/vector.cc). Right-clicking selected points edited from another layer (a bone layer, or several layers at once) also offers Select Layers of Selected Points: the layer holding them becomes active (others with selected points are picked), the points staying selected. Alt-dragging a handle moves it alone and unaligns the point; Unalign does the same without moving anything. Peak and Smooth (no longer in any menu; Python: oanim.ops.mesh.peak(), mesh.smooth()) set a point's handles once (straight, or smooth and aligned). **Subdivide** (right-click with points selected, under Smooth Points; Blender's Subdivide) cuts each segment between two selected neighbouring points into Number of Cuts + 1 pieces (1 at first; changed after in the Adjust Last Operation panel), keeping the curve's shape exactly (each cut an exact split, as the knife's), the cuts spaced evenly along the segment's length rather than its Bezier parameter so they don't bunch where its handles are long; the new points join the selection, at a shape's detail level above 0 they are detail of that level (as the knife's), with mirror editing the images' segments are cut too, and a curve's closing segment counts. One undo step; Python: oanim.ops.mesh.subdivide(number_cuts=). **Decimate Curve** (right-click with points selected, under Subdivide, and in the header's Drawing menu; Blender's Decimate Curve) takes out the selected points the line changes least without, one at a time (each one's neighbours measured again as it goes, so a curve thins evenly and a bump keeps its points), until its Ratio of each curve's selected points is left: 1 keeps them all, 0.5 half. Never an end, a corner, a point two curves share or one with weights of its own, so fewer may go; unselected points always stay. The Adjust Last Operation panel has the Ratio and says how many of the selected points went ("12 of 30 points removed"); dragging the Ratio runs it again live, so a higher one puts back what a lower one took out. It starts at the Ratio it was last run with (0.5 at first). Greyed out, with the reason in its tooltip, where every selected point is on a drawing it can't change (one with its points keyed, a reference, detail levels). One undo step; Python: oanim.ops.mesh.decimate(ratio=), and oanim.wm.last_operation_note() reads the count. **Smooth Points** (right-click with points selected; Blender's Smooth Vertices, for curves) evens out the selected points: each goes toward the middle of its neighbours along its curves (a welded point averages every curve's), half its Smoothing of the way (so 1 takes a zigzag flat in one go), and its handles as far toward smooth ones (in line along the way from the point before to the one after, each a third of its side long; at 1 they stay aligned); unselected points hold, and open curves' ends stay unless Smooth Endpoints is on. The ViewportActionPanel has Smoothing (0.5), Repeat (1), Position, Handles, Radius (evens the line's width; off), Keep Shape (Taubin's push back out after each step, so a closed shape doesn't shrink; off) and Smooth Endpoints (off), and Keep Poses on the setup frame. It smooths what shows, through the bones, follows mirror editing, on a detail level smooths the points that level shows, and away from frame 0 poses them (auto-key keys it). Python: oanim.ops.mesh.smooth_points(factor=, repeat=, position=, handles=, radius=, keep_shape=, ends=). **Separate** (P, Blender's Separate > Selection) moves the selected points, the curve pieces between them and the shapes those pieces fully cover (what Shift D would copy; a shape they cover only part of gives them a shape of their own in its look: a piece of a stroke keeps its line, without the fill it can't close, and a piece of a fill with no line stays a fill with no line, showing once closed, never gaining a stroke; Shift D leaves no handle picked on its own behind, so moving the copy can't swing the original's handles) into a new layer just above, in the same group, named after it (Face 1) with its settings, bone binding and keys; the original loses those pieces, keeping points that its other curves still use. The new layer becomes the active one (several layers separated: the active one's new layer is active and the others are picked, as Layer Duplicate does). One undo step; Python: oanim.ops.mesh.separate(). M opens the Merge menu (Blender's, for points): Merge makes the selected points one point at their middle, and Merge by Distance does so for the selected points within Merge Distance (0.001, in the Adjust Last Operation panel) of one another, each group at its middle. Curves through merged points run through the one kept, a segment between two of them goes (a shape along it carrying on across), and shapes that met only at some of them become one, a line with a line and a fill with a fill, in the first one's look (a fill and its outline on the same curve stay as they are). With X mirror editing the mirror images merge too, each side at its own middle; points picked on both sides merge together. Python: oanim.ops.mesh.merge(), mesh.merge_by_distance(distance=). With one point selected, the Vector tab's Selected Points panel shows its handles as **Handle In** and **Handle Out** rows, each an Angle (degrees, counter-clockwise from the layer's x axis) and a Length (how far it reaches from the point), as Moho and Illustrator give handles; an open curve's first point has no In row and its last no Out; a point on several curves shows each curve's, numbered; a handle picked on its own shows just its row. Changing one moves the handle (an aligned partner turns with it), one undo step (Move Handle); away from frame 0 they show how far the handle has turned and grown from the setup pose instead, 0 at rest. In the Select tool, pressing on a point and dragging moves it as G does (Blender's tweak), ending as the mouse comes up, one undo step (Move): an unpicked point is picked alone first, a picked one brings the whole selection; in Edges mode pressing on a segment drags its two points. Clicks reach generously, in screen points scaled with the display (and Preferences > Selecting > Click Reach, 0.5x to 3x): points and handle tips within 20, motion path dots, keys and handle tips within 14, lines and edges within 9; the nearest within reach wins (a press too: a handle takes it only if no point is nearer), and of two about as near the one on top: a handle tip over a point, a key over a dot, a picked point over an unpicked one. A click without moving (a few pixels, further with a pen) still only picks, and a drag from empty space, or with Shift or Ctrl, box-selects. A **Transform Points** tool in the toolbar (for drawings; any frame) puts the same box gizmo Transform Bones has round the selected points and handles picked on their own, in the active layer's space: drag inside to move (Shift keeps to one axis, Ctrl snaps to a point), the band around it to turn about the pivot (Shift: 45° steps), a handle to scale (Alt from the pivot, Shift keeps a corner's proportions); Ctrl on a side handle shears instead (as Illustrator's and Inkscape's: a top or bottom handle slants the points along x, a left or right one along y, the opposite side staying, or the pivot with Alt; the cursor shows the way, and the undo step is Shear Points); a selected point's handle leading to a point left out keeps its length and only rides along with its point (a smooth point's stays in line with its other handle), so the part not picked keeps its shape: scaling a circle's top, right and bottom points from the right leaves its left half round (with proportional editing every handle maps, the points left out coming along); while the box moves, turns, scales or shears them, the points and handles aren't drawn, so the shape is what's seen; curve handles don't show (nor can be picked) while it is the tool, and with the overlays off (Shift Z) the box, as Transform Bones' box, isn't drawn and clicks select, the pivot to move it (Ctrl snaps to a point); outside, a click selects and a drag box-selects. Points move as G, R and S move them: across every edited layer, through their bones, with mirror and proportional editing, keyed away from frame 0. Its header has the mode switch (Transform, Distort, Warp), Position (the pivot), Scale, Angle, Shear, Flip H, Flip V and Symmetrize; they run mesh.transform_selected (move_x/y, scale_x/y, angle, pivot_x/y). Its look, edge shear, Distort, Warp and the turning knob are under Box gizmos. **Mirror** (right-click with points selected, a submenu as Blender's: X Global, Y Global, X Local, Y Local) flips the selected points, and handles picked on their own: Global along the document's axes through their layer's origin (each layer's own, when several are edited), Local along the active layer's own axes (turning with it) through the middle of the selection; through their bones, with mirror and proportional editing, keyed away from frame 0, one undo step ("Mirror X Global"), the options in the ViewportActionPanel; Python: oanim.ops.transform.mirror(axis="X" or "Y", orientation="GLOBAL" or "LOCAL"). **Symmetrize** (right-click with points selected) makes the selected points mirror images across the layer's vertical axis, copying one side onto the other (Right to Left by default): points within the Threshold of the axis snap onto it and their two handles mirror each other (made flat when within 10° of flat, so one point on the axis just evens its handles), unless its partner's mirror image is as near it, so raising the Threshold never breaks a pair; the others pair up by where each one's reflection lands nearest in the setup pose, and each copy takes its partner's place, handles (reflected, each onto the handle facing the same way, on every curve through a welded point, which an axis point's handles do between themselves too; where one side's line runs through a detail point and the other's through a point of its own, the handles match as drawn), width and corner (and, with Weights, its bone weights, _L and _R swapped). Each side needs as many points as the other: otherwise nothing changes, the status bar warns ("Uneven sides: 2 on the right, 1 on the left") and the points without a partner are selected. On frame 0 it reshapes the drawing. Off frame 0 Match Current Shape is on by default and goes by the shape as it shows on that frame, through its bones: sides and pairs from where the points are now (a curve's two sides from opposite ends when they are even, so the line mirrors as well as its points, else by the nearest), each copy on the exact mirror image of its partner, and points near the axis onto it. Axis picks the layer's line or the Middle of Selection (of both layers, on a sided pair), for a shape that has moved off it. It keys that frame only: Weights is hidden and shape looks aren't copied, as they hold on every frame; the pairs aren't kept, so X mirror editing still goes by the setup's. Turned off, it mirrors the pose from the setup's pairs (each copy's Transform becomes its partner's, reflected, and auto-key keys it). Python: oanim.ops.mesh.symmetrize(match_shape="0", axis="LAYER" or "SELECTION"). Run from the menu off frame 0, points the shape can't pair go by the setup's pairs, else across the middle of the selection, so the panel opens on what ran, and the status bar says so; told which way, it warns instead, saying whose sides it counted. A change in the ViewportActionPanel that can't run (uneven sides, say) leaves the last result, with the panel still open on what was asked, so another option can make it work. The copied side's shapes give their look to their images (Fill and Outline, colours, width, brush and material; a shape that is its own image keeps its own), across a sided pair too. In a sided layer (Ear_L) it works across the pair instead: its selected points and the partner's points nearest their reflections (one each) become mirror images across the pair's axis, Direction saying which side is copied (Right to Left copies the +x one), Threshold and Weights as above; with more selected than the partner has points to pair, nothing changes and the status bar says so. Viewport Overlays > Handles shows None, Selected, Shape (the default: every handle of the shapes being worked on, any of whose points is selected) or All; Point Size and Handle Size under it (pixels across, 2 to 24; 6 and 6.5 by default, as Blender's Vertex Size) set how big points and handles' ends are drawn, a click within 20 screen points of one (or 10 past a bigger one; times the display scale and Click Reach) picking the nearest point or handle; Edges selection mode shows none, as it picks segments rather than points. A handle facing shown detail draws, picks and drags where it really bends the line, shorter than it is stored. Wires are #919191 and 1.5 pixels; only a piece that is hard to see against what is drawn under it (read back from the drawn canvas without waiting for the graphics card, so a click never waits on it, and looked at a few pixels at a time, held until clearly readable again so it doesn't speckle; a click that only picks something draws the same picture, so the colours aren't worked out again) takes #AEAEAE over something darker or #6E6E6E over something lighter; points the same, each by what is under it. Picked wires are orange; a segment with one end picked fades from orange there to the other end's colour, as Blender's edit mode. Handles are light red (#FF9D9D), and #FF3737 where picked, with no outline: like wires, only a stretch (or tip) that is hard to see against what is under it takes a lighter or darker shade of its red: a picked point lights its handles all along, a picked tip alone fades from the tip to the point; tips are orange when picked; points and handle tips are all round. Preferences > Overlay Colours sets each of these colours. A shape is a list of curve segments with a fill and outline style. Styles cascade: a shape has its own style plus up to two shared styles from the library.

**Holes.** A shape can be several loops filling together, as Moho's shapes with holes and SVG's compound paths: its **Fill Rule** (the Shape panel, shown for a shape of several loops) says how. **Even-Odd**: a loop inside another is a hole (and one inside that filled again), whichever way it was drawn; **Nonzero**: each loop's direction counts, so one drawn the other way round is a hole and one the same way adds to the fill (how fonts are made, their overlapping strokes staying filled); **Each Loop**: every loop filled on its own, holes filled over (what shapes of several loops were before holes, and what files from then keep). One shape: one look and material, one click to select, and a click in a hole picks what is behind it; its outline goes round every loop. **Join Fills** (F with whole loops selected, as Blender's F makes a face of a loop and Moho's Create Shape takes an outline and its holes; or the right-click menu) makes one shape of the selected shapes that go round, Even-Odd, with the first one's look; **Separate Fills** (right-click) splits a shape of several loops into a shape each. Holes show in renders, masks, End Lines at Edge, picking and the Boolean modifier (which joins shapes with holes and keeps the holes of what it makes). Fills are worked out with Clipper2 and cut into triangles with earcut. Python: layer.shape_style(i)['fill_rule'], layer.set_shape_style(i, fill_rule="EVEN_ODD" or "NONZERO" or "LOOPS"), oanim.ops.mesh.join_fills(), mesh.separate_fills(); oanim.layers_at((x, y)) gives the layers drawn at a point.

**Text layers.** Text kept as typed, as Blender's text objects (Moho's text layers): runs of it, each with a font (any installed family), weight (Thin to Black), italic, size (document units) and a shape's look (Fill and Outline, colours, line width and brush, or a shared material: a run linked to one changes with every shape using it). Its letters are made with DirectWrite (Windows' text engine: kerning, ligatures, right-to-left scripts, letters a font lacks taken from another) into the layer's drawing, a shape per letter named after it, filling Nonzero, made again whenever the text, its fonts, layout or materials change (from the Text tab, typing, Python or a driver). The letters are saved with it, so a file shows its text where the font isn't installed (the Text tab says which fonts are missing). Layout: Align (Left, Center, Right, Justify), Line Spacing (a multiple of the font's own), Letter Spacing and Word Spacing (ems), Shear (a slant about each line's baseline) and Box Width (0: point text, lines breaking only at new lines; else lines wrap at it). Its origin is the first line's top: for point text on its left end, middle or right end as aligned, for a box its left edge. It moves, turns, scales, fades, masks and follows bones as a layer: G, R and S move, turn and scale it as a whole (as a Media layer, about its origin), and **Don't Auto Key** (the Text tab's Layout, on by default) keeps moving, turning, scaling or fading it from setting keys whatever the record button says (keying it by hand still does); its points aren't edited. **Typing** with Transform Layer and Edit Text (the tools a text layer has: Select has nothing to pick there): Transform Layer, the one a text layer starts with, moves, turns and scales it with its box gizmo, and a double-click on the text starts typing there (Edit Text picked); with Edit Text, click the text to type in it, the mouse an I-beam over it, and drag one of its two side handles (halfway down the text) to wrap the lines at that width: point text becomes a text box, its left edge where the text's was, the other side staying; double-click a handle for point text again, its left edge kept (undo steps Text Box Width and Point Text). Typing goes on the canvas with a caret and selection, as Blender's text edit mode: arrows (Ctrl by words), Home and End (Ctrl: the whole text), Up and Down, Shift selects, a drag or double-click selects, Backspace and Delete (Ctrl: words), Enter for a new line, Ctrl A, C, X and V, Ctrl B and Ctrl I for bold and italic (the selection, or all of it), Ctrl Z steps back through the typing; Esc (back to Transform Layer), Tab, a click away or another layer made active finishes, as one undo step (Edit Text). While typing, every key is the text's (Ctrl S still saves). The Properties editor's **Text** tab: the text in a box (edits keep each run's style where the text is unchanged), Font (a searchable list of the installed families, Weight, Italic, Size), Look (Fill, Outline, Material, colours, Line Width), Layout, and Convert: **To Vectors** (an ordinary vector layer of the letters, one way, as Blender's Convert to Curve), **To Letters** and **To Words** (a vector layer each, in a group in its place). With letters selected on the canvas, Font and Look change just them (the panel says so). New text: the Layers editor's + > Text. Python: oanim.ops.layer.new_text(text=, font=, size=, x=, y=), layer.text_body, layer.text_runs (dicts: text, font, weight, italic, size, has_fill, has_outline, fill, line, line_width, brush, material), layer.text_align, text_line_spacing, text_letter_spacing, text_word_spacing, text_shear, text_box_width, text_dont_auto_key, layer.text_bounds, oanim.ops.text.style_set(start=, end=, font=, size=, weight=, bold=, italic=, fill=, outline=), text.convert_to_vectors(split="NONE", "LETTERS" or "WORDS"), oanim.text_fonts(), oanim.text_font_from_postscript(name), oanim.wm.text_simulate(text) (typing, for tests).

**Point groups.** A layer's drawing has point groups, as Blender's objects have vertex groups: named sets of its points, each point in one with a weight (0 to 1), a point in as many as you like. They're selection sets now, and what modifiers will be limited to (a Bevel by group, its weights scaling each point's amount). The Vector tab's Point Groups panel is laid out as Blender's Vertex Groups: the list, each row the group's icon (a grid of points), its name and its padlock (click it to lock the group, so Assign and Remove leave it alone), the active group lit blue; click a row to make it the active group, double-click to rename it (a name already taken gets a number, "Group.001"); under the list, an arrow that opens its filter (a name search, and A-Z to show it in order of names) and a grip that drags the list taller or shorter. Beside the list: + (a new "Group"), - (removes the active one; its points stay), a menu (Sort by Name, Copy Point Group ("Group_copy", its points and weights), Remove from All Groups (the selected points, from every unlocked group), Delete All Unlocked Groups, Delete All Groups, Lock All, Unlock All) and the up and down arrows that move the active group in the list. Under it, two joined pairs on one row: Assign | Remove (put the selected points in the active group, with the Weight below, or take them out; greyed out with nothing selected or the group locked) and Select | Deselect (the group's points, added to or taken from the selection); then Weight, Blender's filled bar (click or drag to set it, Shift for fine), what Assign gives, kept while Open Animate runs. Blender's Auto Normalize is left out: it only evens out bone weights while weight painting. Groups keep their points by id, so they hold through edits: a point split in between two of a group's points is in it, weighted between theirs, as Blender's Subdivide does; a copy (Duplicate) is in its original's groups with its weights; a point deleted leaves its groups. A copy layer (reference) uses its original's points, so it has none of its own. Saved with the file. Python: layer.point_groups ([(name, {point index: weight}, locked)]), layer.active_point_group, oanim.ops.mesh.point_group_add (name=), _remove, _set (name=, active=1, lock=; index= or group= for one not active), _assign (weight=), _unassign, _select, _deselect, _move (direction=), _copy, _sort, _unassign_all, _remove_all (unlocked=1), _lock_all (lock=).

**Grids.** The Shape tool's Grid (beside Box and Circle) draws a grid in a vector layer: drag a box (Shift square, Alt from the centre, Ctrl snaps), its Columns and Rows (3 each at first, 1 to 32) in the header. The drag ends as Add Grid (mesh.primitive_grid_add): until anything else is done, the ViewportActionPanel changes its Columns, Rows, Location X and Y and Width and Height, making it again in place, as Blender's Add Mesh panel does, and the next grid starts with those cells; a Box or Circle ends the same way (Add Box, Add Circle, with Fill). It is ordinary drawing, points and curves: a node point where lines cross, a straight line along each row and one up each column sharing the nodes, all of them one outline shape named Grid (an outline-only shape whose lines meet three or more at a point is drawn line by line, each curve's run on its own, joined only where just two meet, so no stroke folds back on itself at a crossing), the new points selected; the layer keeps a note of which points are its nodes, row by row from the bottom. So every tool that edits points edits a grid (G, R, S, the Transform gizmo, handles, proportional editing, mirror, keys, bones, action bone actions), and a layer can hold several grids among its other drawing. What a grid is for: a Warp modifier (to come) bends layers by it. Each cell bends what is in it as a Coons patch, the surface the four Bezier segments round it make: an unchanged grid changes nothing, a point on a grid line follows that line, a line curved with its handles curves what lies along it, and past the grid's edge places go on straight (along the patch's slope there); a place is found in the setup pose by the patches themselves, so a grid reshaped on frame 0 works too. A grid that loses a node is ordinary drawing from then on. Saved with the file. Python: layer.add_primitive("GRID", [corner, corner], columns=, rows=), layer.grids ([(name, columns, rows, [node point index])]), layer.grid_map(point, grid=0) (where the grid takes a place from its setup to now), oanim.ops.wm.tool_shape(shape="GRID", columns=, rows=).

**Extrude (E).** On the selected points: an open end's curve grows a new point past it (shapes covering the whole curve growing with it); any other point (a box's corner, a point inside a curve, a grid's crossing) starts a new open curve welded to it, as Moho's Add Point and Blender's Grease Pencil extrude do, closed curves included. The new points follow the mouse; E again from a new end keeps growing it. The end it grew from had no handle out (it led nowhere); now it takes one the way the curve came in, so the curve runs on smoothly there rather than turning, and as the new point moves the segment's handles follow it: the handle out stays in line with the handle in, a third of the segment long and shorter the further it turns (none straight back, so a sharp turn doesn't loop), and the new end's handle is that way mirrored across the segment, one even bend, both points Aligned. A peaked end stays a corner, the handles running straight along the segment; a branch's or a lone point's first segment runs straight. A branch's segment shows: in an outline-only shape through the point (a grid's), else as a new outline shape in the look of the shape there (its fill untouched). Python: layer.extrude_selected() (the first step, without the move), layer.extrude_align(point) (the handles for where the new point is now), layer.shape_runs(shape) (a shape's pieces as drawn).

**Bones.** A skeleton is a tree of bones. Each bone has a rest position, angle and length plus animated position, angle and scale channels. Scale is X (across the bone: its thickness) and Y (along it: its length), as Blender's bone axes, and is for posing only: on frame 0, S resizes bones instead. Bones support IK with locks and angle limits, independent angle, maintain volume (squash and stretch), controlling bones (one bone's angle drives another's), bone dynamics (bones hang like weighted strands that swing, jiggle and stretch as the body moves), colour tags and a display shape. Points follow the bones by weights, as Blender's vertex groups: a new point takes the average of the points next to it, and one connected to nothing has none; Automatic Weights gives a drawing weights (nearer bones pull harder, a bone's strength widens its reach), and weights can be assigned or painted. A whole layer can be parented to one bone instead: Parent Bone in its Layer tab lists Custom Weights (the default) and the bones, and the box says what the points follow (Custom Weights: Eye_L, Custom Weights: 3 bones, or Custom Weights (no weights)). A group reads that from the drawings inside it, and a group with a bone decides for everything inside it, which can't pick their own until it lets go. Picking a bone leaves the weights, so Custom Weights brings them back. Making and changing weights is the weight tools' job, in their headers and Weights menu. Images, video and text have nothing to weigh, so they list None in its place.

**Local armature viewport.** Every armature has two places its bones can live. **Viewport bones** sit in the scene: they move the character, deform artwork and show in the normal viewport. **Local bones** live in that armature's own local armature viewport, a separate canvas that belongs only to it, and never appear in the main viewport. This is where control bones go, such as action bone dials, sliders and switches, so they stop floating around the workspace. Both kinds belong to the same armature and key onto the same timeline. Bones are added and edited the same way in either place.

- **Control-only.** Local bones drive action bone actions and can be controlling bones for viewport bones, but they cannot bind or deform artwork.
- **Separate trees.** A local bone can only be parented to another local bone, never to a viewport bone, and vice versa.
- **Helper artwork.** The local viewport can hold drawings that never render: labels, slider tracks, a face image to click on. Together with the local bones this makes a rig control board.
- **An editor type.** "Armature" is an editor you can put in any split area. It follows the selected armature and can also open as a floating window. Turning a dial there updates the character in the main viewport live.

**Animated channels.** One typed channel class covers numbers, 2D and 3D vectors, colours, booleans and strings. Channels can split into separate X/Y channels, be muted, and have keys selected for the timeline.

**Interpolation: Moho modes on the surface, Blender F-curves underneath.** Each key is stored like a Blender keyframe: a value, an in-handle and an out-handle, and a per-key interpolation mode. The modes are Moho's full set plus Blender's Back easing. Most modes compute their handles automatically, so they behave exactly as in Moho; in the graph editor, dragging a handle turns the key into a Bezier key, the way Blender's auto handles become free. Moho's per-key extras sit on top: a hold length, an interval for animating on 1s, 2s or 3s, a stagger flag and a colour tag. New keys get the new-key mode (saved with the preferences; Smooth to begin with), or, with Copy Previous Key on, the mode and settings of the key before them.

| Mode | What happens between this key and the next | Settings | Handles |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Linear | Constant speed | — | None |
| Smooth | Eases through the key using its neighbours, clamped as Blender's auto-clamped handles: equal neighbouring keys hold still and the motion never overshoots a key | — | Automatic |
| Ease In/Out | Starts slowly and slows into the next key | Strength | Automatic |
| Ease In | Slows into the next key | Strength | Automatic |
| Ease Out | Starts slowly from this key | Strength | Automatic |
| Step | Holds the value, then jumps at the next key | — | None |
| Bezier | An ease shaped by two handles, as a CSS cubic-bezier: the same curve fits a stretch of any length, so one shape can go to many keys; position keys go along the path Smooth makes, at that timing, and across breakdowns it times the whole span | The two handles (x1, y1, x2, y2: time 0 to 1, progress -2 to 3) | Two, dragged in the key settings |
| Back | Overshoots slightly, then settles (from Blender) | Overshoot amount | Formula |
| Bounce | Bounces into the next key | — | Formula |
| Elastic | Springs past the next key and settles | Amplitude (how far the first swing goes), period (how long each wobble is) | Formula |
| Noisy | Random jitter around the interpolated value | Amplitude, frequency | Formula |
| Cycle | Loops back to an earlier key | Frames back, or an absolute start frame | Copied from the cycled keys |
| Pose | Holds a value taken from an action | Which action pose | From the action |

All modes also take the hold length (frames to wait before moving) and the interval (1s, 2s, 3s). The Ease modes' Strength is one of Robert Penner's easing families, from Gentle (Sine) through Quad (the default), Cubic, Quart and Quint to Strong (Expo), plus Circ. Because keys share Blender's storage, drivers can later use the same curves.

**Picking a mode.** A key's interpolation is for the stretch arriving at it from the key before, as Moho's: how the motion gets to this pose (Step holds the key before, then jumps to this one; Ease In slows into this key). Its curve settings (Strength, Overshoot, Amplitude and Period, Amount and Wiggles) go with it; Hold, Animate On and Cycle are for after the key (Cycle loops after its key, so the stretch into a Cycle key is Smooth). The first key's mode does nothing. Files from before (format 3 and older, the Moho converter's too) had each key's curve for the stretch after it: opened, each moves onto the key after, so their motion stays the same. Action bone actions play the stretch to their first key with that key's curve too, as the action shows when edited (before, always Smooth). The timeline's header has one joined group, without arrows: Animate On (1s, 2s or 3s), the Interpolation dropdown (its curve and name, as Moho's), the key type (a key's diamond or a breakdown's circle) and the settings button; each opens above its button, a small caret pointing at it, as Blender's popovers (the settings window above the group's centre). With keys selected the group is red: it edits them (the dropdown reads Mixed when they differ); with none selected it is grey and sets what new keys get. T over the timeline opens the Interpolation menu at the mouse, and right-clicking a key has it under Interpolation (the right-clicked keys join the selection first). Every item shows a small drawing of its curve, in groups: Smooth, Linear and Step; Ease (Ease In, Ease Out, Ease In/Out, Bezier, and a Strength submenu); Bouncy (Back, Bounce, Elastic); Special (Noisy, Cycle). The header's dropdown is the modes alone; the right-click and T menus also have Key Type, Copy Previous Key, Copy Interpolation and Paste Interpolation (one key's mode and settings onto the selected keys, their values untouched), Use for New Keys, Match Timing and Settings. The key type's dropdown shows each type as the timeline draws it. The settings button (or Settings in a menu) opens a window with a strip saying what a change goes to (red, "Editing 3 selected keys"; grey, "Interpolation of New Keys"), Key and Breakdown, and the curve, drawn as progress from the key before (0) to this one (1), with the first key's stretch's frames marked on it and, when the selected keys' curves differ, the others faint behind it. For the modes two handles can shape (Linear, Smooth, the Eases, Back and Bezier) the curve has two handles, as nearly as two can draw it: dragging one makes the keys Bezier, the same ease on every selected key whatever its length, and past the top or bottom overshoots. Even, In, Out and In-Out set the handles to those shapes. Then the mode and its own settings: Strength, Overshoot, Amplitude and Period, Amount and Wiggles, Loop Back; then Hold and Animate On (1s, 2s, 3s); Copy, Paste, Use for New Keys and Copy Previous Key. Python: anim.keys_interpolation_set(bezier="x1,y1,x2,y2"), a key's "bezier". A change goes to every selected key (a drag is one undo step); with no keys selected it changes what new keys get. A yes/no property's keys (visibility) stay Step whatever is chosen.

**Keys show their mode.** In the timeline a key's shape tells its family: a diamond for Smooth, a thin diamond for Linear, a square for Step, a circle for the Ease modes, a star for Back, Bounce and Elastic, and a hexagon for Noisy and Cycle; a frame where the keys of a row differ is a diamond. (A key whose handles were dragged gets a mark once the graph editor can drag them.)

**Stepped Preview.** A stairs button after the playback buttons (also Playback > Stepped Preview) plays everything as Step, holding each key until the next, to judge the timing of the poses while blocking. No key changes; turning it off brings the curves back.

**Actions.** An action is a named, alternative timeline that lives inside every channel of a layer; the regular timeline is the mainline. Actions belong to a bone layer and cover it and everything inside it. Editing an action switches the whole document into that action: its keys are swapped into the channels, layers outside the bone layer keep the mainline, and frame 0 stays the shared setup pose (editing it in an action changes the setup, and every bone pose with it). Actions can be inserted into the mainline as a copy or by reference, and blended.

**Action bones** (Moho's smart bones). An action bone is a bone with an action of the same name, its **action bone action**. Inside the action, the bone's own angle is keyed over time, for example 0° at frame 0 and 90° at frame 24. On the mainline, the bone's current angle is looked up in that keyed range to find a matching action frame. Every other channel in the action is sampled at that frame, and its change from frame 0 is added on top. Visibility and Draw Order hold each key until the next, so they switch when the bone turns past a key's frame, never blending (a head turn that hides an eye at frame 24 hides it once the head turns past that frame's angle). Influences from several action bones always add together. A second action named after the bone plus " 2" handles rotation in the other direction. It plays live while the action bone is dragged, moving its bones and their points as it turns (bones the action moves without keys of their own included). On the setup frame (0) an action bone plays nothing: turning it there sets its rest angle, as in Moho. **360 action bones** (Moho 14.4's): an action bone action whose bone is keyed exactly one whole turn from its setup (within a tenth of a degree, either way; Ctrl while turning finds it) goes round and round: the bone's turn counts modulo a turn, so turning on past the end starts it over and turning back past the setup plays it from the end (a dial for a 360 head turn). The timeline's action list marks it 360°; Python: layer.actions' "full_turn". Action bones can drive action bones: an action can key another action bone, which then plays its own action, after the one that turns it, adding to what the mainline gives that bone; while an action bone action is edited the others play under it, so a dial can be built from them (a 360 from a left and a right head turn, handing over at the back with Step keys, which hold between frames too). Actions turning each other play once each, as they are. Bone Symmetrize's Mirror Action Bone Actions (off at first, in the ViewportActionPanel) makes each symmetrized bone's action bone action on the other side as a new action (Head_Turn_L into Head_Turn_R, replacing what that had), the original left as it is; with both bones picked and both actions there, Direction says which is copied. _L and _R bones and layers swap their keys, reflected, bone layers inside it included (a pair takes each other's bones' keys, a bone layer with no side mirrors its own), and _X ones and those with no side swap them inside themselves: points by where they pair in the setup pose, handles by what they reach for, each handle going across as it is shaped on the key. Draw Order and switch keys name their layers' partners. A point with no partner keeps its place along the mirrored line, which can't take on a bend the other side's point makes. A drawing in a switch that can't pair up gets a flipped copy beside it (Front_Nose_X 1 Flipped), which the new action picks. Masks aren't keys and stay as they are. Python: oanim.ops.armature.symmetrize(action_bone_actions="1", direction="L_TO_R" or "R_TO_L").

**Masking.** A layer's masks are **cards** whose steps name other layers (as Rive's and Figma's boolean stacks, After Effects' mask modes and Live2D's clipping); **Mask cards** below says how a card is built and how cards combine. Below, a row's shape settings are a step's, and Affects, End Lines at Edge, Ends, Reach and the viewport and render switches are its card's. Several layers in one row make one shape by the row's **Combine**: Union (all of them together, the default), Difference (the first layer's shape with the others cut out of it, so their order counts) or Intersection (only where all of them are), as Blender's Boolean; a group named means all of it, as one layer. Combine is three joined icon buttons (two overlapping rings, the part kept filled) just left of the layer chips, greyed out while the step has one layer; the summary joins the names by it ("inside head + hat", "inside head - mouth", "inside head & hat"), picking and End Lines at Edge follow it, and a file from before it opens as Union. A row takes its layers' shapes as drawn, before any masks of their own (those mask how a layer shows, not the shape it lends; only Clip to Below takes what its base shows); a file from before this lists, when opened, the layers whose masks named a layer with masks of its own. Each row has Invert (use what's outside its shape), Grow (pixels; negative shrinks; a pixel hides thin seams along the edge), Shape (which parts mask: As Drawn, fills and strokes as drawn; Fills Only, Moho's option, without strokes; Strokes Only, just the lines), Fill Every Shape (a checkbox: the inside of every shape masks, filled or not, an open one closed straight across, as Rive clips with a shape's path; greyed out with Strokes Only, and the summary then reads "on ring's lines"), Inside the Stroke (a checkbox, with Fills Only: the fills end at their lines' inner edge rather than down their middle, at any zoom, and wherever the line is drawn (a brushed line's turned or jittered dabs reach past its width, soft edges included), so an arm cut by a body shows over the body's whole outline where they join, as a copy of the body's fill on top would; each layer's lines trim only its own fills, so the lines inside a group (strands drawn over another layer's fill) don't cut the fills around them), Cuts (what of the masked layer the row masks: All; Fills, its fills only, as Toon Boom cuts colour art apart from line art; or Lines, its lines only, ended or sliced, its fills showing as if the row weren't there, so a head and an ear that cut each other's lines where they join keep both fills rather than leaving a hole where they overlap; picking and Clip to Below go by the fills; the summary adds "(its lines)" or "(its fills)"; a file with the older Lines Only opens as Lines), End Lines at Edge (a checkbox: the masked layer's lines end where they cross the row's shape, worked out along them before a brush lays its dabs, instead of being sliced there, with Ends: Brush (the line stops short of the edge with whole dabs, so nothing crosses it, with the brush's own taper and fade, and isn't sliced by the row's pixel clip; its Reach, in line widths from -2 to 2, keyable and drivable, moves the end: 0 just touches the edge, 0.5 puts the last dab's middle on it, more runs past it, less leaves a gap) or Flat (the line then cut straight along the edge); fills are masked as always, unless Cuts is Lines; with Cuts Fills it does nothing; the parts cut away show as faint dashed wires; a group's row ends the lines inside it; an Add row after one that ends lines ends them too, with that row's Ends, so it brings back the lines that row ended without End Lines at Edge on itself (the card says so under its checkbox); the summary adds "lines ending at head's edge"), Extend (None, Up, Down, Left, Right: the shape stretched to the edge of everything that way, in the mask layer's own directions, turning with it and its bone; a filled shape, or any with Fill Every Shape, stretches all of it, an outline-only one just the edges it draws, so Subtract the mouth line extended Up hides the mouth and everything above it, letting teeth show over the lower lip without a hand-drawn shape) and its own viewport and render switches, as modifiers have. **Clip to Below** (Photoshop's clipping mask) shows a layer only where the layer below it shows, as it shows: only its visible layers count (hidden ones and, in renders, guides don't), each cut by its own masks as drawn; several layers in a row can clip to one base, the first layer below them that doesn't clip; it applies after the rows. Its Exclude Strokes (under the checkbox) clips to the base's fills only, so shading stops inside the outline instead of covering it. A group's mask applies to everything in it. Masks don't depend on Draw Order or visibility: a hidden layer still masks (a mask shape is usually kept hidden), and one mask layer can mask many layers. A mask layer's own mask counts (its shape masks only where its rows show it). Copied together (Duplicate with both picked), a layer and its mask layer stay a pair: the copy is masked by the copy; a reference of a group masks itself with its own copies. Where they are set: the Layers editor shows a masked layer with just a mask symbol after its name, no text (orange, the colour masks are shown in everywhere; grey when all its rows are off): hovering it lists the rows and the result in words and outlines their layers in the viewport, and clicking it opens the Masking tab. A clipped layer is indented with an orange hook pointing down to its base. **Alt-dragging** a layer onto another adds a row of it there (Add; Shift: Subtract). **Alt-clicking** the line under a layer (the lower part of its row) clips it to the layer below, or releases it; **Ctrl Alt G** does the same for the picked layers. The Masking tab in Properties (next to Layer) starts with the result in words ("Shows: inside head, minus mouth, plus tongue"), a row to a line ("Shows:", then "everything but head", "plus tongue", "minus mouth and above"; hovering a line outlines its row's box and layers, clicking it opens and scrolls to the row), then Add Mask (a menu: Show Inside a Layer... starts a card on black with + Shape, Hide Inside a Layer... on white with - Shape; then click the mask layer, in the viewport or the Layers editor), Join Lines (with two layers picked that meet, a head and an ear: each gets a Subtract row of the other with Fills Only, Inside the Stroke and End Lines at Edge, the lower one's cutting only lines so its fill covers the overlap; a row already naming the other is reused) and the Clip to Below checkbox ("Clipped to base" under it), then a box per row as the Modifiers tab shows modifiers (Blender 2.90's panels): a header (open arrow, its name ("Mask 1"), or folded the row in words ("minus Ear_L (its lines), lines ending at Ear_L's edge"), viewport and render switches, a menu with Duplicate, Move to First, Move to Last and Delete, an x that deletes it, and a grip to drag it up or down, which can change the result) and, folded inside, Layers (a chip per layer, x taking it out; + for the list of layers to tick, the eyedropper to click one; hovering a chip, or a layer in the list, highlights it in the Layers editor and outlines it in the viewport), Invert (use the outside) and Affects (the Cuts setting) with **View** at its end (a half white, half black square): while it's held down, the viewport shows the mask as it stands after this row, over the drawing at 90% so the art shows faintly through: white where the layer's fills show (its lines, when the row affects only Lines) and black where they're hidden, its groups' masks counting, with a label saying which ("Lines mask of Ear_L after row 2 (Ear_Line_Show): white shows, black is hidden"); rows that leave that part alone are left out and rows that end lines count by their shapes; letting go shows the drawing again (viewport only, never renders), then two subpanels, folded at first, whose bars open them and, folded, show what's set inside ("Fills, inside the stroke, +1.0 px, extended up"; "Brush, reach 0.34"): Masking Shape (Mask With, the Shape setting, with Fill Every Shape and Inside the Stroke indented under it, Grow and Extend) and Line Ends (End Lines at Edge, then Ends and Reach, greyed out while it's off). Settings with a few choices (Affects, Mask With, Extend, Ends) are rows of joined buttons, Blender's expanded menus, with icons (fill and line, fill, line; arrows for Extend). A row that's off in the viewport is faded. Hovering a row outlines its layers in the viewport. On a mask layer, a Used as a Mask section below lists the layers it masks or that clip to it (click one to select it). The Mask menu (the Layers editor's right-click menu, and the viewport's right-click menu with nothing selected) lists the rows and has Clip to Below, Add Layer..., Subtract Layer..., Join Lines and Remove Masks. In the viewport the active layer's mask layers are outlined dashed in orange, labelled (Viewport Overlays > Drawing > Mask Outlines turns this off; they still show while a mask symbol, row or Layers field is under the mouse). Undo steps say what happened ("Mask head: Subtract mouth hole", "Clip stripe to base"). Rendering evaluates each masked level's rows in order in the stencil (a result bit per level, a scratch bit for each row's shape, which with Difference or Intersection takes each layer's shape from a bit of its own; shrinking clears a band around each area's edge). Files from before rows, whose masks said Inside or Outside and all had to hold, open exactly as they looked: an Outside mask becomes Subtract, an Inside one Add as the first row and Subtract with Invert after, Expand a 1 pixel Grow, and Mask With Layer Below becomes Clip to Below's operator. Files from before that, with Moho's masking groups (Hide all / Reveal all, each layer Masked, Add, Subtract or Clear), open with those turned into rows (a mask layer's old Expand and Fills Only going to the rows naming it): an Add row of the layers added before it (since the last Clear) and a Subtract row of the ones cut, and the masking-only layers become hidden layers. The Moho converter leaves masking out. Files from before cards convert as Mask cards below says. Python: layer.masks (cards and their steps; see Mask cards), layer.mask_summary, layer.clip_to_below, layer.masking, oanim.ops.mask.add / set / remove / move / duplicate / join_lines / clip_to_below.

**Mask cards.** Each card is a mask of its own, painted as Photoshop's layer masks are: it starts white (**Start With: Everything**, all shown) or black (**Nothing**), and its steps, read top to bottom, each paint their shape on it: **+ Shape** paints white (shows inside it), **- Shape** paints black (hides inside it), and a step's Invert paints the outside instead (- Shape with Invert keeps only what's inside). A step's shape is its layers combined (a chip each; Combine: Union, Difference or Intersection), with its own Shape (As Drawn, Fills Only, Strokes Only), Fill Every Shape, Inside the Stroke, Grow and Extend. The card says what of the layer it masks (Affects: All, Fills or Lines) and how it cuts lines: sliced by pixels, or, with End Lines at Edge, ended where they cross the edge of the card's whole shape (Ends Brush or Flat, Reach). The Masking tab calls a card a Mask (the cut, and how it cuts) and its steps Masking Areas (the black and white that build it). A mask's areas are a stack, as the Layers editor's: Show All or Hide All at the bottom (its Start With, always the bottom row), and each row above painting on what is below it, + white, - black, Keep Only black outside it (saved as - Shape with Invert), so the top row is applied last. **Inverse**, a toggle on every area and group, paints the outside of its shape instead: a + area then shows everything but it, and a - area hides what's outside it, which is Keep Only, so the row becomes one; each use of a global area keeps its own. Clicking a row picks it, and Current Masking Area, below all the masks, has its settings, so the stack stays a short list; clicking a row's icon goes on to the next of the three, rows pick as the Layers editor's do (Shift click picks from the active row to this one, Ctrl click adds or takes out one, a plain press on a picked row keeps them all for a drag and narrows to it without one), and right-clicking a row has Convert To (Add, Subtract, Keep Only), Join as Group (Ctrl G) and Delete (X or Delete with the mouse over it; the last area takes its mask with it), each for all the picked rows if it's one of them; rows drag to reorder, together, and into and out of groups. Joined, all + stays a + and all - a -, painting as they did; any other mix keeps each one's own, the group taking its paint from the base, as a new group does. The + beside Masking Areas adds an area (Add, Subtract or Keep Only Area..., then click the layer), adds a Group, or adds a Show All or Hide All row (the whole canvas, at the bottom of the stack or of the picked group, so a group can start from white and cut from it; right-click the base row to change that); Add Mask stays, with Show Inside a Layer... and Hide Inside a Layer.... A mask's first area picks its base. A Group's areas build a shape of their own from nothing, for the group to paint as a layer's would, so a head's mouth hole reads Show All, then - a group of + InsideMouth_X, - SideMouth Cut and Keep Only Lip_Line_X; the summary says it "everything but (InsideMouth_X, minus SideMouth Cut, within Lip_Line_X)". Groups do not nest. A group works anywhere in the stack: where it can be it is turned into ordinary steps (directly on the base, or one that makes one shape), and any other stays a group, its shape worked out on the side (a spare stencil bit in the renderer) and painted as a layer's would be, by the renderer, picking, End Lines and export alike. With no + area or Show All in it, a group is empty and says so on its row. Global Area, on a row of the stack, shares an area between masks as a material is shared: Make Global, a list to link one, its name and user count, clicking the count to make that one its own again, looking the same. Layers, settings and a group's contents are shared, each use keeping its own +, - or Keep Only; a copy whose layers change (Symmetrize, Duplicate with the layers it names) is its own from then on. A row that changes nothing is dimmed and says why (+ on Show All, - on Hide All), Keep Only outside a group warns that it hides everything outside that layer, and an area on a layer whose closed shapes have no fill, As Drawn and not extended, warns that just its line masks, with Fill It to turn on Fill Every Shape. View in Current Masking Area, held, shows the mask as far as that area. Thumbnails on the rows are for later. The layer shows only where every card that masks that part shows it: cards multiply, so one can only hide, never bring back what another hid, and a card that leaves the fills alone doesn't touch them. So a head can have one card for the ear (Everything, - Shape Ear_L, + Shape of the outside of Ear_Line_Show, lines ending at its edge) and another for the neck (Nothing, + Shape Head_Base_X). A card's first step sets its Start With (+ Shape: Nothing; - Shape: Everything), and so does changing a lone step's mode; after that it changes only by hand. Clip to Below is a card of its own that starts from everything. In the Masking tab each card is a box: its header has the open arrow, its name, a field edited in place as a modifier's (one undo step, "Rename Mask"; left empty it reads "Mask 1" by its place, and a named card's line in the summary starts with its name; hovering it shows the card in words), the viewport and render switches, a menu (Duplicate, Move to First, Move to Last, Delete), an x and a grip; inside, Affects with View first, Line Ends under it, then Masking Areas with its + and the stack, and below all the masks Current Masking Area. The summary at the top says each card on a line ("fills: everything but Head_Base_X", "lines: everything but Head_Base_X, plus Ear_Line_Show, lines ending at the edge"). Add Mask makes a card (Show Inside a Layer... starts it on black with + Shape, Hide Inside a Layer... on white with - Shape, then click the layer); Join Lines makes one card on each of the two layers. Rendering puts a layer's cards in up to three stencil levels by what they cut (fills and sliced lines, fills only, sliced lines only), each card worked out in a spare bit and kept only where the level already shows. Files from before cards turn each layer's rows into cards that show the same: the rows that masked its fills become a fills card and those that masked its lines a lines card (one card for both when they're the same rows), each starting where the layer's Start With, or its first row, said, the lines card ending lines if a row did; an Intersect row becomes - Shape with Invert flipped, a Difference row + Shape, and a row that was off a card of its own, off. Python: layer.masks (cards: name, start, cuts, end_lines, ends, reach, enabled, render and steps: mode ADD or SUBTRACT, verb ADD, REMOVE or KEEP_ONLY, name, group, children, global, users, invert, layers, combine UNION, DIFFERENCE or INTERSECTION, grow, shape, filled, inside_stroke, extend; a card's first step's keys are on it too), oanim.ops.mask.add (a new card; card= adds a step to one), mask.set (name=, start= and the card's settings; step= for a step's, child= inside a group, verb=, area_name= and combine= among them), mask.group, mask.area_move, mask.area_global (action= MAKE, LINK or UNLINK), mask.add group=, mask.remove and mask.move (step= for a step), mask.duplicate; drivers: masks[i].start / cuts / end_lines / ends, masks[i].steps[j].mode / combine / invert / grow / shape / filled / inside_stroke / extend.

**Draw order.** Every layer has a keyable Draw Order: Normal (where the Layers editor puts it), In Front Of a layer, or Behind one. Its keys hold until the next key, as visibility's do. A rule can name any layer in the document, across groups and bone layers; a rule on a group moves the whole group; rules are settled outer groups first, then in the Layers editor's order. Masks name their layers, so a masked layer drawn in front of another layer is still clipped to its own mask. A group faded below full opacity is drawn as one image, so nothing inside it can leave it: a rule inside it that names a layer outside moves the whole faded group (the Draw Order row and the Layers editor say so). A rule naming a layer that is gone draws as Normal and shows "(missing)". On frame 0 the commands set the setup order; on any other frame they key it. **Ctrl ]** / **Ctrl [** (Bring Forward / Send Backward) move a layer past the next drawn layer that overlaps it, so every press shows (past one that doesn't only when none does); with **Shift**, Bring to Front / Send to Back. A step that lands where the Layers editor has it anyway goes back to Normal. **In Front Of... / Behind...** wait for a click on the other layer, in the viewport or the Layers editor: the layer pointed at is outlined and named, and the result shows before the click; Esc cancels. The Draw Order menu (right-click in the viewport with nothing selected, the points' right-click menu, and the Layers editor's right-click menu) has these and Normal, under "Now: In Front Of Torso". The Layer tab's Draw Order row has the mode, the layer (a list or the eyedropper) and the key button (in the viewport the eyedropper picks the group beside what moves, the outermost one holding the drawing clicked that doesn't hold the moving layer: a whole Eye group, not its pupil; Ctrl-click picks the drawing itself; the Layers editor picks the row clicked; Python: oanim.wm.draw_order_pick_target(hit, moving)). The Layers editor marks a layer drawn out of place on this frame with an arrow (up in front, down behind; red when its layer is gone): hovering says what it's drawn in front of or behind, clicking selects that layer. Its Order button lists the drawing layers in this frame's draw order instead of the tree, each with its group; dragging one onto another (upper half in front, lower half behind) sets its rule. In the timeline a Draw Order row (under its layer, beside Opacity and Visibility) has square hold keys and, between them, a bar naming the rule in effect ("In Front Of Torso", or an arrow and the layer's name where the bar is short); hovering a bar outlines both layers in the viewport. Keyed Here lists it as "Draw Order: In Front Of Torso". Undo steps say what happened ("Hand In Front Of Torso"). Viewport clicks that pick a layer take the topmost as drawn. Python: layer.draw_order, oanim.data.draw_order (names as drawn, bottom first), oanim.ops.layer.draw_order_set(mode=, target=).

**Switch layers.** A Switch is a group that shows one of its children, or none (Moho's switch layers, Spine's slots, Rive's Solos, Harmony's drawing substitution; the research is in reports/Switch layer design.md and reports/Switch layer design review.md, one folder above the repo). Built in stages, all five done (2026-10-29); what's left is under Later.
- **What a key holds (stage 1, done).** The switch's Active channel holds the child's layer id, as Draw Order holds its target's, so renaming or reordering children never breaks a key (Moho keys by name and falls back to the top child; Harmony's rename trap; DragonBones and Rive by position). Files, Python and the UI show and take names. None (0) is a value of its own. Frame 0 holds the default child; a new switch's default is its top child, and a layer added to an empty switch becomes it. Keys only hold, as Visibility's do: no interpolation, no morphing between children (Moho's Interpolate Sub-Layers needs the same points in each child and breaks with action bones). A key naming a child that is gone shows the default and says "(missing)"; Replace Keys With... points such keys at another child (stage 2). Copying or duplicating a switch keeps its keys pointing at the copied children.
- **Who wins (stage 1, done).** Choices never add (a child can't be added to another, the trap Blender's enum blending falls into); each frame has one winner, highest first: (1) a key made by hand on the timeline, after frame 0, holds until the next key; a **Rig** key hands the switch back to what is below it (Cartoon Animator's Release); (2) generated lip-sync keys (later, in their own layer, so running lip-sync again never touches hand keys); (3) action bone actions: an action has a say once its bone has turned past one of its switch keys (a Rig key in it says nothing), and when several have a say the one listed first among the bone layer's actions wins; (4) the default (frame 0). Moho lets the action bone win over hand keys with nothing on screen saying why ("the smartbone will override any manual keyframes you try to set"); here the hand wins and the timeline shows which source is in charge (stage 2). Visibility still adds its action bone changes on top; moving it to this rule is a change of its own, not made yet.
- **Picking (stage 2, done).** One operator, layer.switch_set, picks a child and keys it at the current frame (on frame 0 it sets the default), following Auto Keying as any property does: with it off, a pick on a keyed switch shows until the frame changes and a pick on one not keyed yet is its value everywhere, nothing keyed (the status bar says so), and I or the key button keys it (I with one of its drawings active keys the switch's pick too); with Alt, hold=1, it picks for the whole hold the playhead is in instead, the key starting it (the default when that is frame 0) taking the child, no new key (Harmony 15.0.2 also made "this frame only" its default). It is reached from a dot before each child's type icon in the Layers editor (Rive's radio buttons: filled for the pick, a ring for the others, an orange ring for a child shown only for editing; the eye stays the viewport-only toggle), **[** and **]** (Previous and Next in Switch: the child above or below in the Layers editor, wrapping, from the one the viewport shows), and a **Switch: <name>** submenu in the Layers editor's and the viewport's right-click menus ("Now: Mouth A (keyed)", "(from the rig)" or "(the default)"; the children, the pick ticked; None; Rig; Replace Keys With). The switch's row in the Layers editor names its pick after its name. The timeline's Switch row draws a bar for each hold naming the child it shows, coloured by who decides there: teal for a key made by hand, orange for the rig (a Rig key, or the stretch from frame 0 when action bone actions key the switch: "Rig, else Mouth E"), grey for the default, red for a child that is gone; its keys are the bars' edges, dragged as any keys. While one of a switch's drawings (or a layer inside one) is picked, the timeline lists the switch too once it is keyed, so a pick's key shows where it was made. Selecting a hidden child (or a layer inside one) in the Layers editor shows it in the viewport in place of the pick, to edit, without keying (Layer::edit_out), on the frame it was selected: once the frame changes (scrubbing, playing) the switch shows its picks again, though it stays active; renders and exports keep the pick, and a pick made while editing a child moves the active layer to the picked child. **Replace Keys With...** (layer.switch_replace_keys, from= a name or a gone child's id) points every key showing one child, frame 0's and actions' too, at another, and says how many. **Convert to Switch** (the Layers editor's right-click menu, layer.convert_to_switch) turns the active and picked groups into switches where they are, their children the drawings, the top one the default (name, place, masks and the rest kept; greyed out with no group picked). Python's layer.wrap_in_switch still puts the active and picked layers into a new switch, as Group With Selection does a group. Later: onion skins showing the other children (once onion skins exist), Shift [ and ] to jump between switch keys, and a thumbnail popup at the mouse, which comes with the asset shelf's thumbnails (stage 4).
- **Sub bone groups (stage 3, done).** A child of a switch can be a bone group with its own bones (a leg's thigh and shin, Spine's skin bones), hung from a bone of the bone group around it with Parent Bone (the hip). A bone group inside another is a **Sub Bone Group** (Layer tab, on by default; anim/bone_family.hh): while either is worked in, the bone groups worked in together (a family: out to the outermost through sub bone groups, then the sub bone groups shown inside) draw their bones together (the group worked in and the sub bone groups inside it alike, the groups around it faint), and a click goes to the best bone of the family under it, a joint before a body, ranked together so a long bone running past doesn't hide a small one inside (clicking the same spot again goes down the list); a bone of a sub bone group inside the group worked in is picked with that group staying active, the Bone tabs and the commands on the active bone following it; a bone of a group around it makes that group the active one (with Shift, either joins what's picked, the active group staying). From a bone group, A, Alt A, Ctrl I and a box take in the bones of the sub bone groups shown inside it too, picked bones showing in the family's faint ones; a box that catches no bones there takes the first group around it that it catches. G, R and S move what's picked in those groups too, each group's bones in its own space, unless a picked bone already carries the group (a bone it hangs from by Parent Bone), which brings it along as it does a child; the Transform Bones tool's box still moves the active group's alone. So a character's bones all answer clicks. A sub bone group inside a switch belongs only while the switch shows it, so its bones come and go with its drawing; its keys are kept while hidden. Drawings follow only their nearest bone group (Moho's rule) and a bone's parent is in its own bone group or one around it, so a drawing can't be bound to another child's bones and no bone parents across children: the warnings planned for those aren't needed. A bone of a sub bone group can hang from a bone of a bone group around it, staying where it rests: Parent in its Relations panel lists those under each bone group's name, Ctrl P works with the parent active there (the bones picked in the groups inside it hang from it), and Python takes bone_parent_set(parent_layer=) and shows outer_parent. It then moves as any child, whatever moves its own group, moved with its parent it comes along once, and a dashed line shows the link. Clear Parent lets go, and it lets go by itself where it rests if the parent is deleted or its group leaves. It can't be Connected, and a mirrored copy lets go. **Select Bones This Drawing Uses** (the Layers editor's right-click menu on a drawing in a bone group; armature.select_used_bones) selects the bones its points are weighted to and its Parent Bone, its bone group becoming active. **Match Pose from Previous Drawing** (the Switch menu; armature.match_pose_previous, not on frame 0) turns the active child's bones, and moves its roots, to lie in the picture as their counterparts in the drawing the switch's keys showed before this hold lie on this frame, and keys them whatever Auto Keying says: no pop at the swap (no surveyed tool has one). Later: IK targets on the shared skeleton for a child's bones (constraints name bones of their own bone group only, so this needs constraints that reach another bone group), and skipping a hidden child's dynamics.
- **Bone swaps (stage 3, done).** A bone can stand in for another in another child of the same switch (Bone::swap_layer, swap_bone: by id, so renames keep it): the Bone Relations panel's **Stands In For** (shown for bones of a switch's child), armature.bone_swap_set (a loop is refused), and **Auto-link Bones by Name** in the Switch menu (armature.swaps_auto_link: each child's bones stand in for the first child's of the same name). Bones that stand in for the same bone, along a chain of swaps, are counterparts. Swaps don't share poses: a front view's head tilt can be an action bone while the side view's is the head bone's angle. A pick made while working in a child's bones selects their counterparts in the picked child, which becomes the bone group worked in; Match Pose lines counterparts up; the asset shelf offers a selected bone's switches. **Link All Channels** links a swap's channels two-way at factor 1 (stage 5). Python: a bone's stands_in_for ((bone layer, bone) or None), layer.sub_bone_group, layer.bone_family (the family's names, outermost first).
- **Two-way links (stage 5, done).** In the Driver window, a driver with one variable and an expression like var, -var, var * 0.5, 2 * var or var / 4 (or Average, Sum, Min or Max of its one variable), without Remap, can be ticked **2-Way Linked** (anim.driver_two_way; Data: Driver::two_way); others grey it out with the reason. It becomes **Factor** (never 0) and **Flip**, and the keys are stored once, the variable's: either side can be edited, an edit to the driven side going back into the variable (two_way_edits_back, before drivers work out; recording then keys the variable), as Synfig's linked values do (Blender's drivers are one-way; two pointing at each other loop). The change from rest is shared: driven = its rest + factor x (variable - its rest), so each side keeps its own rest (a bone's pose is a change from its setup already; another channel's rest is its frame-0 key, or what it was before it was first driven, which a driver remembers). Linking two animated properties asks which keys to keep and says how many go (keep=SOURCE or TARGET; the driven side's keys turned into the variable's); a two-number channel's keys go only when both its numbers are linked. Several links reading one property make a group (a dial, its front tilt and its side tilt); a property is in one group only (the side read can't be driven by another link, the driven side can't be read by one), and an action bone can't be linked with something its own action keys. Unticking makes it a one-way driver again (var * factor); **Unlink** (anim.driver_unlink) takes it off, the driven side getting keys of its own that move it as the link did. Linked fields, either side, are teal (driven ones purple) and stay editable; their tooltip names the other side; their right-click menu has **Select Linked** (anim.driver_select_linked: its layer active, its bone selected) and Unlink; the driven side's timeline row shows the keys it shares. **Link All Channels** (the Relations panel beside Stands In For; armature.bone_link_all) links a bone's angle, position and scale at factor 1 with the bone it stands in for. Saved with the file; Python: a driver's two_way and factor (minus with Flip) in oanim.data.drivers. Later: Set Factor from Pose, and a side that IK or a constraint drives shown read-only.
- **Asset shelf (stage 4, done).** A view of its own, the **Asset Shelf** editor (in the editor-type menu and Window > New Area; the Layout workspace has one beside the timeline, Info to its right), grown from the Sculpt tool's brush shelf (Blender's asset shelf; src/editors/asset_shelf.cc). Its header has Context and All Switches, Pin ("Pinned": the rows stay whatever is selected), what it follows ("Mouth, in Head Front"), and search (Ctrl F), tile size and Preview, wrapping onto a second line when narrow.
