The header as Blender's Grease Pencil Draw: the pen, line colour, Width with a pressure toggle (F sizes it by dragging), Opacity (Shift F), fill colour, brush; then the Stroke dropdown (Stabilizer, Smoothing or String and Catch Up, Fidelity with how many points the last line makes at it, Close and Fill, Weld Ends), Advanced (the pressure curve, Min Size, Pressure Smoothing, Start and End Taper, Width From Speed and Tilt, Prediction) and Cursor (Width Circle). Opacity has no pressure toggle: a line has one opacity, not one per point. The header never starts a stroke, even between its buttons. The Fill Pen and Fill Eraser (stage 4, done) brush an area: a disc swept along the line, so tight turns and loops come out right, tapers and pressure included, with a width of their own that isn't the line width shapes take. The Fill Pen paints in the fill colour with no outline, as Illustrator's Blob Brush: it merges with fills of the same colour it overlaps (no outline, no material, no keys, no shared curve), down the stack as far as a different fill it overlaps, and the blob stays where the topmost of them was, so lines drawn over a colour stay over it; with nothing to merge with it is a new shape on top. A hole stays a hole (Even-Odd). Other colours are never merged. The Fill Eraser cuts the area out of fills, on lifting (a red ribbon shows where it has been); a shape with an outline keeps it whole on its own curves, what is left of the fill just below it as a new fill-only shape; a fill cut in two becomes two shapes, and one cut away goes. All Layers cuts every drawing being edited, Selected Only only the selected shapes. A shape with keys isn't cut, since cutting rebuilds its points, and the Info editor says so. The Stroke Eraser and Vector Trimmer (stage 5, done) cut lines without rebuilding them: the curve is split where the brush's edge meets it (an exact split, keeping the line's width there) and the pieces between go, so keys and weights stay on the points that stay, shapes with keys included, and the new ends take the line's round caps. The brush reaches a line at its edge, its half width counted, so a line wider than the eraser still parts. Along a fill's edge the piece is hidden (Moho's Hide Edge) rather than gone, so the fill keeps its boundary; a fill's edge with no line isn't a line to erase. The Stroke Eraser's Whole Line (the Whole Line Eraser preset) takes the whole of each line it touches. The Trimmer takes each piece it touches out to its nearest crossings with the drawing's lines, itself included, or to its end, as Clip Studio's Erase up to intersection; another line's end stopping on it, a T, counts as a crossing; a closed line crossing nothing goes whole; with All Layers it cuts every drawing being edited, and each counts the others' lines as crossings. Trim Overshoots in the Stroke dropdown takes a stroke's ends past the last line they crossed, when no longer than 30 screen pixels (with one crossing, only the shorter side). Ctrl or the pen's eraser end erases with the matching eraser: Stroke Eraser from the Stroke Pen, Fill Eraser from the Fill Pen, at the erasers' width. The tool bar's right-click lists Stroke Pen, Fill Pen, Bezier Pen, Fill Eraser, Stroke Eraser and Vector Trimmer, and each comes back with the preset it last used. Splitting a segment, the Knife's too, now keeps the line's width at the new point. The engine: every pen sample (stage 1, done), and the Stroke Pen's line (stage 2, done): Smart smoothing by default, the line a spring pulled by the pen, loose while it is slow, which leaves a hand's wobble out, and tight while it is fast; or Pulled String with Catch Up, or Off. Pressure is smoothed apart from position, with a pressure curve and a Min Size; speed and tilt can thin the line too; tapers run by distance, in document pixels. What is drawn while the pen is down, at its real width and narrowing at its tip as its end taper will, is the line that stays: nothing jumps on lifting, except that a flick off as the pen leaves the tablet is left out, and a stamp brush shows as a solid line until then. On lifting, a curve fit of position and width together makes it few, well-placed Bezier points (handles in line, Fidelity saying how close), with corners where the pen slowed and turned; a closed stroke's ends are fitted across. A tap is a dot. Python: oanim.wm.draw_settings(), oanim.wm.set_draw(stabilizer=, smoothing=, string_length=, catch_up=, fidelity=, pressure_smoothing=, pressure_curve=, min_size=, speed_width=, tilt_width=, taper_start=, taper_end=, line_width=, weld=, cursor_circle=, fill_size=, fill_color=, line_color=, erase_all_layers=, erase_selected=, erase_whole=, trim=), oanim.ops.wm.tool_draw(mode= FREEHAND, PEN, FILL_PEN, FILL_ERASER, STROKE_ERASER or TRIMMER), layer.shape_contains(index, point), layer.curve_points(curve), oanim.wm.draw_presets(), oanim.ops.draw.preset_load(name=), preset_save(name=), preset_reset(name=), preset_remove(name=); oanim.ops.draw.stroke(samples="x,y,pressure,seconds;...", pixel=) makes a stroke as the tool does (erase=1: Ctrl); oanim.wm.ink_stroke(samples, ...) runs the engine without the window. Preferences has a Tablet API (Automatic, Windows Ink, WinTab; preferences.tablet_api_set(api=), oanim.wm.pen()'s api, wintab_installed, wintab_open and from_wintab): see Pen tablet support.

**Materials.** Shared looks for shapes, as Blender's materials (Moho's library styles, Illustrator's global swatches). A shape has its own look, or is linked to one material, which then sets everything about its look: fill colour, line colour, line width and brush (Fill and Outline on/off and the points' widths stay the shape's). Changing a linked shape's look in the Material panel (its Brush section too, or Python) changes the material, and every shape using it changes with it. Which you're editing is plain to see: the panel's header, even folded, says "Material: Skin (shared by 12 shapes)", or "Material: Own look"; while one of a shared material's fields is under the mouse or being changed, every shape using the material is outlined in the viewport and the header says "editing Skin changes 12 shapes". Linked shapes carry a white corner mark (Illustrator's global swatch triangle) on their swatch in the Shapes list, on the browse button and in the materials list. The Shape tab's Material panel (folding, under the Shape panel, which keeps Name, Position and Fill / Outline) holds the look: its first row is Blender's data-block selector, then Fill Color, Line Color, Line Width and the Brush row (below). Each of those has an **eyedropper** at its right end: click it, then a shape in the viewport, and the selected shapes take that shape's fill colour, line colour, line width or brush (the colour picker's own eyedropper samples a pixel instead: anywhere on the screen, Open Animate's windows or any other app's, as Substance Painter's: the cursor becomes a pipette, its tip the pixel picked, and a round swatch at its bottom right shows the colour under it as it moves; a click picks it without reaching the window under it, right-click, Esc or switching away cancels; the RGB is taken and the alpha kept, as in Blender). Python: oanim.ops.mesh.style_copy_from(layer=, shape=, what=FILL|LINE|WIDTH|BRUSH). The selector: a browse button (the shape's swatch, a disc of its fill ringed in its line colour) opens the list of materials, each with its swatch and how many shapes use it (unused ones greyed out), a search field at the bottom (typed into as soon as it opens) and Remove Unused; hovering one outlines its shapes in the viewport, clicking links the shape, right-click > Delete Material. Beside it, the material's name (rename it there; names are made unique, "Skin.001") and the user count: clicking the count gives the shape the look as its own (unlinked, looking the same). With its own look the row is browse and **Make Shared**, which makes the look a shared material and links the selected shapes; right after leaving a material, New names the variant after it ("Skin.001"). Nothing is deleted on its own: a material no shape uses stays until Remove Unused, and deleting a material leaves its shapes its look as their own. A duplicated layer's shapes share their materials; a reference's copies take theirs from the original (the row is greyed out there). Saved with the project. The Moho converter brings looks in as each shape's own. Python: oanim.data.materials, layer.shape_material(i), oanim.ops.material.new / assign / unlink / rename / remove / remove_unused.

**Line weight at any zoom.** A line keeps its true weight however far the view (or a render) is zoomed out: one thinner than a pixel is drawn a pixel wide and as much fainter, rather than every hairline looking a pixel thick; a brushed line's stamps fade so that, overlapping, they come to its thinness. Masks still take hairlines a pixel wide.

**Brushes.** Open Animate copies Moho's stamp brushes: a line can be drawn as a row of image stamps instead of a solid stroke. A brush is part of a line's style, so shared styles share their brush. The stamps stick to the artwork: they are laid out along the line as drawn on the setup frame, each at a place on one of its segments, and kept at that place as points move and bones bend the line, so they stretch and squash with it instead of sliding along it (Moho's slide as a curve stretches; Toon Boom's textured lines are fixed to the line like this). A segment stretched past 1.5 times its length fades in stamps halfway between its own (and again at 3 times, ...), and one squashed below three quarters fades out every other one, so a line never breaks into dots or clots, after Bénard et al.'s self-similar line textures. Brush Settings' When Stretched picks whether those stamps fade (the default) or pop in and out whole. The layout doesn't depend on the zoom, so the viewport and renders agree.

- **Brush row**, in the Material panel: a preview stroke in the line colour, named in its corner by the preset it matches ("Ink"), or "Custom" when it matches none (edited since, or never one), or "Solid line", with Blender's **preset button** (three sliders, as tall as the preview) at its left. The button opens Brush Settings on its settings; clicking the preview opens it on the presets (the Brushes tab). In the preview's bottom right, the **brush eyedropper**: click it, then a stroke in the viewport (anywhere on it as drawn, its width included), and the selected shapes get that shape's whole brush, as one undo step (Esc, right-click or a click off any shape stops it; the cue at the mouse says so). The Draw tool's header has a button for the brush new strokes get, opening it on the tool.
- **Brush Settings window**, after Photoshop's Brush Settings and Brushes panels. It floats over the app and doesn't block it, so the viewport shows every change as it's made, and says what it edits ("3 selected shapes", "The Draw tool's brush"). It follows the selection: it shows the active shape's brush, and a change goes to every selected shape as just that setting, so they keep their other differences. Two tabs:
  - **Brush Settings:** Photoshop's sections down the left, each with its switch (a section switched off keeps its values): Brush Tip Shape, Shape Dynamics, Scattering, Color Dynamics, Transfer, Line Boil. The chosen one's settings are on the right and a large preview stroke runs along the bottom. Brush Tip Shape has the tip grid (None, a solid line; the built-in **Round** tip; the library's shapes; shapes only this file's presets carry; picking a shape changes only the shape, every setting stays), Angle and Roundness beside Photoshop's angle widget (drag to turn the tip; Ctrl: 15 degree steps), Flip X / Y, Hardness (the Round tip's), Spacing, When Stretched (Fade In or Pop In), Merge alpha, Tint and Random stamp order. The line's width isn't a brush setting: it stays the material's Line Width, and presets don't keep it.
  - **Brushes:** the presets as cards of preview strokes, the Project's and then the library's, grouped by category, the folders of Brushes (Photoshop's brush folders), each folding (Project and Basic open to start with, the rest folded), with a search field that shows matches in every group. Hovering a card shows it on the shapes until the mouse leaves; a click loads it; right-click has Save to Library (a submenu of categories, plus New Category...) and Delete; clicking a library category's header makes it where new library brushes go.
  - Along the bottom of both, Substance Painter's save row: a name field, where it goes (**Project** or **Library**, remembered), with Library a second dropdown of categories (the library's folders, the last one used, and New Category..., which turns it into a box to type a new folder's name), and +. Library is greyed out without an asset library. Saving opens the group it went to and scrolls to its card; the status bar says where it went and whether its shape was copied into Brush Shapes. Library brushes always have a category (My Brushes when none is named).
- **The built-in Round tip** (Photoshop's General Brushes: hard to soft by Hardness) is drawn by the app, so it's never missing and nothing is bundled.
- **Brush presets:** a shape plus every setting, saved by name (as Krita keeps presets apart from brush tips). Library brushes live in `<library>/Brushes/<Category>/<Name>.oabrush` (one JSON file each, a folder per category), for every project; built in are Hard Round and Soft Round (Basic, on the Round tip, which can't be deleted); project presets are saved in the .oanim, each with a copy of its shape's images, so a file always carries what its presets need. They're listed in the Brush Settings window's Brushes tab (above). Loading copies the settings in (nothing stays linked, so deleting a preset never changes what used it), so it follows materials like any edit. Names never clash: a taken name gets a number ("Ink.001"), no question asked. Saving to the library copies the shape too when the library lacks it; the same images under that name are reused, other images get the copy a number ("CK Ink.001") and the saved preset points at it. A preset whose shape is missing is greyed in the menu with a warning; loading it keeps your shape, applies the rest and says so in the status bar. Python: oanim.assets.brush_presets(library=False); oanim.ops.brush.preset_add (library="1", category=) / preset_load / preset_remove / preset_save_to_library (category=), oanim.assets.brush_categories(), oanim.ops.brush.setting_set(setting=, value=) for one setting on every selected shape (target="TOOL" for the Draw tool's brush), oanim.wm.brush_settings_open() (tab="BRUSHES" for the Brushes tab). The tip grid and the Brushes tab read shapes and draw previews only for the cards in view, about 8 ms of them a frame, so a large library opens at once and fills in over a few frames.
- **Brush shapes are only images:** `<library>/Brush Shapes/<Name>.png` is a shape of one image, a folder of PNGs a multi-image shape (used in turn, or at random). They carry no settings: the look is the brush's (its preset). Stamps are dark ink on white (darkness is coverage), or colour with alpha for colour brushes.
- **Asset library:** all of the app's brushes live in one asset library folder, set in Preferences (`<library>/library.json` marks it, format version 2; `<library>/Brushes/` holds the brushes by category, `<library>/Brush Shapes/` their images). If none is set, or its folder is gone, Open Animate asks for a folder at startup and initializes the library there. Preferences shows how many brushes and shapes it has, with Reload. Nothing is imported from Moho any more.
- **Upgrading an older library:** a version 1 library (Moho's brushes in `Brushes/`: `.mohobrush` files, PNGs with settings in their names like `Brush522_1_0_200.png`) is upgraded the first time it's read. Each brush's images become a shape under its own name (so documents using it still find it) and its settings a brush file named after it (without the settings in the name: "Brush522"), sorted into a category by what it looks like: Basic, Ink, Texture, Hair & Fur, Nature or Fun (others: Other). The old folder is set aside while that happens and deleted once everything is copied; if anything fails, it's kept as "Brushes (old)" with a warning.

| Setting | Stored as | What it does |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Align brush with curve | `align` | Stamps rotate to follow the direction of the line (Photoshop's Angle Control: Direction) |
| Random stamp order | `randomOrder` | Multi-stamp brushes pick stamps at random instead of in sequence |
| Merge alpha | `mergedAlpha` | Overlapping stamps merge into one transparency instead of building up darker |
| Tint brush using stroke color | the style's tint flag | Stamps take the line colour |
| Line boil | `randomize` | Stamps re-randomise over time so the line shimmers like hand-drawn animation |
| Jitter angle | `jitter`, in radians | Random rotation range per stamp; 360° means any angle |
| Spacing | `spacing` | Gap between stamps as a percentage of brush size |
| Size variation amplitude | `sizeVariationAmp` | How much stamp size varies |
| Size variation scale | `sizeVariationScale` | How quickly that size variation changes along the line |
| Randomness interval | `randomInterval` | Frames between line-boil changes |
| Angle drift | `angleDrift` | Gradual rotation of stamps along the line |
| Color drift: hue, saturation, value | the style's drift values | Random colour variation per stamp (Photoshop's Hue, Saturation and Brightness Jitter) |
| Tip Angle, Roundness, Flip X / Y | `tipAngle`, `roundness`, `flipX`, `flipY` | Photoshop's Brush Tip Shape: every stamp turned, squashed across, mirrored |
| Hardness | `hardness` | The built-in Round tip's edge, hard to soft |
| When Stretched: Fade In / Pop In | `popIn` | The stamps filling a stretched segment's gaps (and those thinned from a squashed one) fade in and out, or appear and vanish whole, halfway through the fade |
| Size Jitter, Minimum Size | `sizeJitter`, `sizeMin` | Shape Dynamics: each stamp smaller at random, never under the minimum |
| Taper Start / Taper End | `sizeFadeIn`, `sizeFadeOut` | Shape Dynamics: the line thinning to a point at its start or end, over that many stamps (0: no taper; Photoshop's Size Control: Fade) |
| Roundness Jitter | `roundnessJitter` | Each stamp squashed at random |
| Scatter, Both Axes, Count, Count Jitter | `scatter`, `scatterBoth`, `count`, `countJitter` | Scattering: stamps pushed off the line (in line widths), several at each place |
| Opacity Jitter, Fade In Start / Fade Out End | `opacityJitter`, `opacityFadeIn`, `opacityFadeOut` | Transfer: stamps more see-through at random; the line turning see-through at its start or end over that many stamps (0: no fade) |
| Section switches | `shapeDynamics`, `scattering`, `colorDynamics`, `transfer` | A section switched off keeps its values but doesn't apply (Shape Dynamics also holds align, jitter, drift and size variation) |
| Preset | `preset` | The preset the settings were loaded from, so the UI can say "Ink (edited)" |

**Detail levels (multiresolution shapes).** A shape can be edited with fewer or more points without losing its look, as Blender's Multiresolution modifier and ZBrush's subdivision levels do for meshes and Finkelstein and Salesin's multiresolution curves do for lines: the overall sweep is edited at a low level while the fine detail rides along.

- **Levels.** Level 0 is the shape's own points (what bones, weights and Parent Bone act on). A detail point at level k lives on one segment of level k-1: where it sits along the segment and how far it stands off it, measured along and across the segment and scaled with its length (Stretch: a segment twice as long has its detail twice as wide and twice as tall, so scaling a shape scales its detail and a shrinking segment never makes spikes). Its handles are kept the same way. Moving, bending or stretching the coarse curve carries the detail with it. On a higher level, though, the detail points that level shows are points of their own: moving a coarser point there leaves them where they are, and only the finer detail hidden above the level rides; keyed moves of the coarse points still carry every level as they play. Bones move detail points by their own weights, which they get from their neighbours as any new point does. A parent's handle facing finer detail is shown and edited as its handle at its own level (its coarse handle, longer than the piece it shapes); lines draw with the handles the splits give.
- **Per shape.** Each shape keeps its own level, saved with it; changing it is an undo step (Detail Level). Each shape's curves show their points up to its level (base points drawn bigger, the level's own at normal size, finer ones hidden); a curve shared by shapes at different levels uses the higher one. The full detail always shows and renders. Points hidden by stepping down are deselected.
- **Adding detail: the knife only.** Nothing subdivides on its own: the Split Curve tool (K) adds every detail point. At a level above a shape's top level (stepping one past it opens an empty level, with the hint "Knife (K) adds detail points at this level") the knife adds a detail point at the shape's level, on that segment only; at level 0 it adds a base point as it always has.
- **Where it is set.** The viewport header shows Detail ‹ 1 / 3 › (arrows, drag or type) while a shape is selected; with several selected at different levels it shows a dash, and setting it puts them all on that level. Ctrl+0 to Ctrl+4 set the selected shapes' level (with none selected the status bar says "Select a shape first"); Alt+Page Up and Alt+Page Down step it. The viewport's info text adds the active shape's level (Detail 1/3). The Shape tab's Shape panel has a Detail section: the same Level field, the shape's levels and detail points (Levels 3 · 14 detail points), Delete Higher (the detail above the current level), Apply Detail (flatten it into base points) and Rebuild Levels (turn a dense drawing, such as a Moho import, into a base plus detail: its detail flattened first, each point within the Tolerance of the curve its neighbours make without it becomes detail; when none can, nothing changes), each on the selected shapes and undoable. Apply Detail and Rebuild Levels work on the setup frame; keys they can't keep (a detail point's offsets) are removed, and the status bar says so. Python: layer.detail, oanim.ops.mesh.detail_level_set(level= or delta=), detail_delete_higher, detail_apply, detail_rebuild(tolerance=); layer.split_segment adds detail at the curve's level.
- **Wires** show each curve at its shape's level: through the points shown, with their handles as they are at that level, so the wire is what you bend; the stroke under it draws all of its detail. Clicking a line in Edges or Strokes mode picks that same curve.
- **Animation.** Detail points key like any point, relative to the setup pose and in their segment's frame, so animating the base points carries the detail and a lower level is the place for big moves.
- **Smooth to Lower Level** (right-click with detail points selected) moves them, or handles of detail points picked on their own, onto the curve of the level below, so they keep none of their own offset; the ViewportActionPanel's Influence (0 to 1) sets how far. Away from frame 0 it poses them (auto-key keys it). Python: oanim.ops.mesh.detail_smooth(influence=).
- **Move to Higher Level** (right-click with points selected) moves them one detail level up, keeping the curve's look (their places along the segment between the coarser points around them worked out anew), so they ride those points and hide below their level. An open curve's ends, points on more than one curve and points at the top level stay (the status bar says how many); a closed curve keeps at least two coarser points. Setup frame only; their keys go (as Rebuild Levels'). Python: oanim.ops.mesh.detail_raise().
- **Move to Lower Level** (right-click with detail points selected) moves them one detail level down, keeping the curve's look: from level 1 they become base points. The finer detail around them takes them for a parent (its places along the new segments worked out anew), so it rides them from then on. Base points can't go lower (the status bar says so). Setup frame only; their keys go. Python: oanim.ops.mesh.detail_lower().
- **Editing the base.** Deleting a base point joins its two segments and their detail moves onto the joined one (the status bar says so: "Merged the detail of 2 segments"); splitting a segment at level 0 gives each detail point to the half it is on. Symmetrize, mirror editing, references and action bone actions treat detail points as points (each has an id).

**Layers and masking**

| Feature | From | Tier |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Vector, group, bone, switch and media layers | Moho | v1 |
| Layers panel: drag to reorder, visibility, lock, label colours; Shift-click picks the rows from the active layer to the clicked one (the active layer stays, as Blender's Outliner), Ctrl-click adds or takes out one layer (taking out the active one hands it to another picked layer) | Moho, Blender | v1 |
| Show Active: **.** (or Numpad .) with the mouse over the Layers panel scrolls to the active layer, opening every group it's in, and flashes its row, as Blender's Outliner. A view, not an undo step. Python: oanim.ops.layer.show_active(), layer.expanded | Blender | v1 |
| Guides: a layer with Show in Renders off (its camera button off) is a guide, shown while you work and never rendered, and the viewport's overlays include it: Shift Z (overlays off) or Overlays > Guides off hides every guide, so the viewport shows what renders. The camera button shows a dashed light-blue frame for a guide (brighter while guides are hidden) with tooltips saying so; guide rows fade in the Layers editor while hidden, and the viewport's info line says "(guide)" for the active layer and "N guides hidden". Hidden guides still mask; a Media layer's Guide is the same switch (new Media layers start as guides; older files' Media Guide opens as it) | After Effects | v1 |
| Masks as ordered rows that name layers: Add, Subtract, Intersect, Difference, Invert, Grow/Shrink, several layers per row, groups, hidden mask layers, the result in words; Clip to Below (Alt-click between rows, Ctrl Alt G); Alt-drag adds a row | Rive, Figma, After Effects, Photoshop, Live2D | v1 |
| Blend modes and layer opacity | Both | v1 |
| Multi-layer editing: point tools work on every selected layer at once (see below) | Community | v1 |
| Layer comps | Moho | Later |
| Reference layers: live copies with per-channel overrides and local masks (see Core concepts) | Moho | v1 |
| Layer effects: blur, shadow, shading, outline | Moho | Later |
| Media layers: images, numbered image sequences, video and sound to trace over, rotoscope or time to, as clips along the timeline (see Media layers below) | Both | v1 |

**Media layers.** A Media layer shows an image, a numbered image sequence, a video or a sound, read with Windows' own decoders (WIC for images, Media Foundation for video and sound: what plays in Photos and Media Player opens here).

- **Adding one takes a drop, no dialog.** Drop a file on the viewport and it becomes a Media layer above the active one, starting on frame 1; dropped on the timeline, it starts on the frame it lands on; dropped on a Media layer's clips row, it joins that layer as a clip there instead (picked, over what was there). While the file is still being dragged, it shows live where it would land: in the timeline a dashed bar as long as the clip it would make, at the frame under the mouse ("Add clip walk.mp4: frames 81 to 128" on a clips row, "New Media layer ..." elsewhere), and over the viewport the outline of its picture fitted to the camera's frame with what it is (Open Animate's own drop target reads the files as the drag enters; SDL's names them only on the drop). File > Import > Media and the Layers editor's + > Media... do the same with a file dialog. A numbered image ("walk_0001.png" with siblings) comes in as an image sequence. Named after the file. Its options then show in the ViewportActionPanel: Start Frame, Fit, Opacity, Guide; the Properties editor turns to its Media tab.
- **Tracing defaults.** A guide (Show in Renders off: shown while you work, hidden with the overlays, never rendered, as After Effects' guide layers and Blender's reference images), at half opacity, fitted to the camera's frame (at zoom 1). Clicking in the viewport never picks it, so drawing over it is safe; it is picked in the Layers editor or the timeline, and the Select tool says to add a vector layer above it to draw. With it active, G, R and S move, turn and scale the layer itself, as Blender does an object (about its picture's middle; X or Y (or Z, as Y) keeps to one axis, Ctrl snaps, a typed number sets it; keyed away from frame 0), and Alt G, Alt R and Alt S clear its Location, Rotation and Scale (0, 0 and 1), as Blender's Clear Location, Rotation and Scale. Right-clicking in the viewport while it is active opens its menu: **Fit to Camera Frame** lays the picture over the camera's frame as it is now (wherever the camera has moved, zoomed or rolled, and through the groups and bones above the layer), setting the layer's position, rotation and scale, with Fit, Fill or Stretch in the ViewportActionPanel; then Clear Location, Rotation and Scale. Python: oanim.ops.media.fit_to_camera(fit=).
- **Clips.** The layer holds clips along the timeline, as a video editor's track: each is a file shown from its Start for its Length, from In seconds into the file (After Effects' in point). A new clip is as long as its file (a still image: to the end of the frame range). Clips never overlap: one moved or trimmed over another overwrites it. Changing the file part way through the animation (Replace from Current Frame) ends the clip there and starts a new one. After End says what a clip shows once its file runs out (Hold Last Frame, Loop from its In point, or Hide). Timing: Real Time (a 30 fps video plays at its own speed in a 24 fps scene) or Frame by Frame (one video frame per scene frame, for a reference made at 12 fps; its sound follows the speed); a sequence is always one image per frame. The setup frame shows what frame 1 does.
- **Media tab.** A picture of the frame showing, the file's name and what it is ("Video, 1920 x 1080, 30 fps, 4.2 s, with sound"); Replace... (another file, the clip's timing kept, as After Effects' Replace Footage), Reload, Pack / Unpack, Replace from Current Frame...; the Clip panel for the clip on the current frame (else the one picked in the timeline, else the nearest, saying which frames it is on): Start, Length, In, After End, Timing, and its timing in words ("30 fps video in a 24 fps scene: plays in real time"); Look: Guide, Fit (Fit, Fill, Stretch, Original Size), Opacity (keyable), Greyscale, Tint Color and Tint (a light table's look: the dark parts take the tint, the light stay white, as blue-line pencil), In Front (over everything, unmasked), Don't Auto Key (on by default: moving, turning, scaling or fading the footage never sets keys whatever the record button says, so while it isn't animated a change holds on every frame; keying by hand still keys it; saved with the project; Python: layer.media_dont_auto_key); Sound (when it has some): Volume, Mute.
- **Timeline.** While a Media layer is active, Selected shows its clips (a row two rows tall) and its own keyed channels (where it is, its opacity). Each clip is a bar: a filmstrip of its file (made in the background, so playback never waits for it), a waveform for sound, its name, and hatching past the file's end (Hold, Loop, Hidden). Clips move and trim as in ToonSquid: a click picks a clip (Shift adds), framing it in light blue with a dark pill handle at each end; dragging a clip moves it (an unpicked one moves whole, even grabbed near its edge), the picked clip's handles (grabbed wider than they are drawn) trim it, and Alt-drag slips it (the bar stays, the part of the file shown moves); the cursor says which, and while trimming or slipping the whole file shows faded behind it. Without the magnet no other clip changes, as ToonSquid's timeline with its magnetic mode off: a handle stops at the neighbouring clip, and a clip dropped on another is outlined red and goes back ("hold Ctrl to push the others along"). Holding Ctrl while dragging turns the magnet on, as ToonSquid's magnetic mode: trimming the end carries the clips stuck to it (no gap) along and pushes those it runs into, trimming the start retimes the clip before it (they stay together, or it gives way), a moved clip pushes the clips it lands on along, and edges snap to the playhead, the frame range's ends and other clips' edges ([ToonSquid handbook: Timeline](https://toonsquid.com/handbook/interface/timeline/)). Python: media.clip_drag (kind MOVE, TRIM_START or TRIM_END; magnet). A readout follows the mouse ("Trim   Start 1   Length 47   End 47   In 0.00 s"). Each drag is one undo step (Move, Trim or Slip Clip); Esc or a right-click puts it back. X or Delete over the timeline deletes the picked clips (Shift-click picks more); with none picked it deletes keys as before. Right-click a clip: Replace..., Replace from Frame N..., Split at Frame N, Delete Clip, After End, Timing (and Locate... when its file is missing).
- **Files.** Linked by default, saved with an absolute path and one relative to the .oanim, and found from either (a moved project folder still opens). Pack keeps a copy inside the .oanim (`media/<id>/<name>`, stored, not recompressed) so it opens anywhere; Unpack goes back to the file, writing it beside the .oanim (media/) if it is gone. A missing file shows: a red mark on its layer in the Layers editor, a red striped clip ("Missing: walk.mp4") and a Locate... button in the Media tab; opening a project with missing files says so once in the status bar (no dialog in the way). Locate finds the file, and every other missing file in that folder with it (Premiere's Relink Others Automatically).
- **Decoding.** Each video decodes on a worker thread of its own, as Blender's movie clip cache does: a frame asked for seeks to the keyframe before it and decodes forward, then a little further ahead, so playback finds its frames waiting (cached by time, so variable frame rates work). Until the exact frame is ready the nearest decoded one shows, never a blank; a render waits for the exact one. Pictures are halved until their longer side is at most 2048 pixels. Sound decodes whole, in the background, to 48 kHz stereo.
- **Sound.** Anything with sound plays it with the animation (forward playback, as in Blender), mixed from every Media layer at its Volume; Mute silences one. The timeline's Playback popover has Blender's Audio settings: Scrubbing (a frame's worth of sound each time the frame changes while scrubbing) and Play Audio, and Sync: Play Every Frame, Frame Dropping, or Sync to Audio (the frame follows the sound being heard). Python: oanim.media.probe(), pixel() and mix(), Layer.media_clips and media_shown(), and the media.* operators (add, clip_add, replace, replace_from_frame, reload, pack, unpack, locate, clip_set, clip_split, clip_delete).

**Multi-layer editing.** Select several vector layers in the Layers panel (Ctrl-click one at a time, Shift-click a run of them) and the point tools work on all of them at once.

- Points of every selected layer are shown and selectable; the active layer is highlighted, the others drawn slightly dimmer.
- Box and lasso select pick points across all selected layers in one drag.
- Move, rotate and scale (including G, R, S) transform every selected point together around one shared pivot. Each point still moves correctly inside its own layer, even when the layers have different transforms or bones.
- Curvature, line width, delete, and the sculpt brushes also act on all selected layers.
- On an animated frame, keys are set in each layer that changed. The whole edit is one undo step.
- **Edit Descendants** (a group's checkbox in the Layer tab): while the group is picked or active, every drawing inside it, however deep, is edited as if picked too (a hidden or locked one, or one inside a hidden group, stays out). On for groups, off for bone groups (whose drawings come with their bone collections); converting one to the other sets it to match. With a group active, the Select, Split Curve, Transform Points and Sculpt tools work on those drawings' points (Draw and Shape still need a drawing layer), the timeline lists the animated layers inside it, as for a bone group, and keying it keys them too: I and Alt I key or unkey every layer inside, and a key button keys (or unkeys) the same property of each, all the way the group's own goes. Saved with the project; Python: layer.edit_descendants.

**Bones and rigging**

| Feature | From | Tier |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Bone collections, as Blender's (Armature tab > Bone Collections): a list with, per collection, a dot (filled while something of it is selected, hollow while it has members), an eye to show or hide it and a star to solo it; right-click a collection to Rename or Delete it (All has neither); + and -, a menu (Move Up/Down, Show All, Unsolo All, Select All in Collection, Remove Unused Collections), a grip that sizes the list and a name filter; Assign, Remove, Select and Deselect under it; double-click renames. All comes first in every bone layer: it holds every bone and every drawing Editable in Bone Group, can't be renamed or removed, and nothing is assigned to it or removed from it (Assign and Remove refuse while it is active). Bones and editable drawings can each be in several other collections; new bones join the active one. As in Blender, a bone (or editable drawing) shows while any collection holding it shows, so while All shows everything does, and hiding All leaves the other collections' eyes to decide; while any collection is soloed only soloed ones show (soloing All shows everything). Hidden bones can't be picked or moved (hiding deselects them); a hidden drawing loses its points and wires and isn't edited with the bones, its artwork still rendering. A drawing joins the bone layer with Editable in Bone Group (its Layer tab), then Assign or Remove (picked in the Layers editor, or its points selected in the viewport) and M / Shift M move it between collections. M opens Move to Collection (leave the others, join one) and Shift M Assign to Collection (join one, keep the others), each ending in New Collection: with the bone layer active (over the viewport or the Layers editor) they take its selected bones and the editable drawings picked or with points selected; with an editable drawing inside it active, over the Layers editor only (M in the viewport is left to the drawing), that drawing and the other picked ones (not the bones). Subdivide, Duplicate and Symmetrize keep membership; Moho's bone tags convert to collections (Tag 1, Tag 2...). Nested collections: Later | Blender | v1 |
| Add, select, transform and reparent bones as in Blender's armature edit mode: head and tail joints selectable on their own on the setup frame (posing, on any other frame, a click or box picks whole bones, as Blender's pose mode: a click on an end picks the bone, a box picks a bone either end of which is inside), an active bone, Shift A adds a bone at the origin, E extrudes from the selected tips (connected), Shift D duplicates, Ctrl P parents to the active bone (Connected or Keep Offset), Alt P clears or disconnects. On frame 0, as in Blender's edit mode, only the picked joints move: G on a ball moves that end (the bone stretches), G on the body moves both ends, R and S turn and size picked bones; a child that isn't connected stays where it is, and a connected child's head (its parent's tail) comes along while its tail stays. Away from frame 0, as in pose mode, G on the top ball turns the bone toward it, G on the bottom ball or body moves the whole bone, R turns it about its bottom ball, and children come along. Holding Ctrl while turning bones (R, or the Transform Bones tool's band) lands the turn on the nearest of: the bone pointing a multiple of 45° on screen, its angle when the turn began, and away from frame 0 its setup angle, each with whole turns more or less (so a 360 action bone's full turn is one Ctrl away); the bone measured is the active one (else the first picked), and the header says what it snapped to. Away from frame 0, G on a connected bone turns it about its base instead of moving it. Away from frame 0, Alt G, Alt R and Alt S take away the selected bones' own change to their position, angle or scale on that frame. With no bone picked, Alt G does the same for the selected points, and Alt R for their handles (or a handle picked on its own). They go back to the setup pose as the action bone actions move it, so a bone or point an action bone moves lands where the action bone puts it instead of being keyed back against it; an action bone itself goes back to its rest, playing its actions back to the start. X deletes a bone picked by any part; off the setup frame its children rest under its parent as deleting it on the setup frame would put them, keeping their own keys. A dotted line joins a child to its parent (Viewport Overlays > Armature > Relationship Lines, on by default, as Blender's). Viewport Overlays > Armature shows bones Off, Selected (only selected bone layers') or On (every bone layer's, the default); bones of a layer you're not working in show as faint outlines. Unless it's Off, the weight tools show their bone layer's bones, with the selected bone solid. Right-click a selected bone for Subdivide (a chain of connected pieces), Symmetrize and the other bone commands. With X mirror editing on, the other side (_L / _R) follows moves, turns, extrudes, subdivides and deletes. The sides pair by name, as Blender's flip_name: a suffix (_L, .L, -L, " L"), a prefix (L_, L., L-, "L "), either case, or Left / Right anywhere, with a number at the end kept after a dot, space or underscore (arm_L.001 and arm_R.001, L_foot and R_foot, Eye_Lash_L 2 and Eye_Lash_R 2); Python: oanim.utils.flip_name(name) | Blender | v1 |
| Box-select bones with the Select tool (the ends inside the box; Shift adds, Ctrl removes). A selects every bone, Alt A none, and Ctrl I inverts the selection (Select menu > All, None, Invert while a bone layer is active; hidden bones stay unselected). A Transform Bones tool in the toolbar, after Moho's sz_transform_bones_gizmo: a box around the selected bones to move (inside), turn (the band around it) and scale (eight handles; Alt from the pivot, Shift keeps proportions), a draggable pivot (Ctrl snaps to bone ends), Position/Scale/Angle/Shear fields, Flip H/V and Symmetrize; unselected children stay where they are; edge shear, Distort, Warp and the turning knob as under Box gizmos | Moho, community | v1 |
| Weights instead of Moho's binding: a new point takes the average of the nearest points along its curves, and one connected to nothing has no weights until Automatic Weights or painting gives it some; a Weight Select tool (select points, Ctrl-click a bone, Ctrl+Shift-click to add or take out one; Automatic Weights, Automatic: Selected Bones, Assign, Remove, Normalize) and the Weight Paint tool (Automatic: Selected Bones in its header too), whose header has a Weights menu in place of Drawing (Blender's). They work on the active layer alone, whatever else is picked; on an active bone layer, or group with Edit Descendants, they work on the drawings inside it that go by those bones (a bone layer inside has its own, and a layer with a Parent Bone follows that). So only one bone layer's bones are in play, and only those show. A weight tool stays on going from a drawing to its group, and under a Parent Bone, where weights do nothing, the tools wait. Automatic: Selected Bones (weight.automatic(bones=SELECTED)) binds to the selected bones only, replacing what the points followed, and does nothing with none selected; in Weight Paint it takes every point unless Selection Mask is on (points=ALL). The menu: Remove All Weights (the selected points let go of every bone and follow none; nothing without a selection), Clear Weights (every point of what the tools work on; weight.clear), Clean (bones moving a point less than Limit, 2%, let go of it, the bones left sharing what they had, a point keeping its strongest bone; the selected points, all when none are), then Automatic Weights, Automatic: Selected Bones, Assign, Remove and Normalize; Python: weight.remove_all(), weight.clean(threshold=) (with a Selection Mask), both with **Keep Animation** in their header (off by default, kept while Open Animate is open; as Parent Bone's): a weight change (made on any frame, in any action) leaves the drawing where it shows on every keyed frame of every action it plays in (the mainline and its bone layer's actions; action bone actions first, then the mainline, so nothing is put right twice), the points it bends differently keyed back there (detail points too, and their handles; the Weight Paint brush keeps every action too, from any frame; every weight operation also has Keep Animation in its Adjust Last Operation panel, Assign its Weight too, to change after the fact), where without it the poses follow the new weights (Python: keep_animation=1 on weight.assign / remove / normalize / automatic); Parent to Bone for whole layers (the Layer tab's Parent Bone, a dropdown with a search field on top (typing narrows it, Enter takes the first bone left) and an eyedropper beside it: click the bone in the viewport, the bone under the mouse named beside it; under it Deform: Layer moves the layer as a whole, so the bone's squash and stretch squashes its lines and brush stamps too, Points moves its points as if each were weighted all to the bone, so lines keep their width and stamps their shape (Points is the default for drawings; a file saved before it was keeps Layer on the layers bound then; the points land in the same places either way; a group moves as a whole; Python: layer.parent_bone_deform, oanim.ops.layer.parent_bone_set(deform="LAYER" or "POINTS")); under them **Keep Animation**, a checkbox (off by default, kept while Open Animate is open): changing the bone (or setting it to None) keeps the layer where it shows on every keyed frame, Keep Poses' reverse: the setup frame is left alone and the poses are kept, the layer's position, rotation and scale keyed again on each frame its own, its groups', the bone layer's or any bone's keys are on (in every action the layer plays in, the mainline and its bone layer's actions, done from whichever one is open; with Deform: Points too, the bone's move taken out by the layer's transform; Python: keep_animation=1; one undo step, "Parent to Bone, Keeping Animation"); giving a layer's points weights sets it back to None; a group's Parent Bone moves everything inside it as a whole, overriding their own Parent Bone and weights, which show the group's bone greyed out); a layer's Editable in Bone Group option lets its points be picked, moved and deleted while the bone layer is active, along with the bones (while its bone collections show), and in a sub bone group while a bone layer around it is active too (its handles too: pressing on one drags it, as on the layer itself); bone strength sets the automatic reach. Older files' point and flexi binding become the same weights | Blender, Moho | v1 |
| IK with bone locks and angle limits: Auto IK (a toggle in Tool Settings on Select and Transform Bones) makes posing a bone by its tip turn its parents, up to a bone Ignored by IK, inside Limit Angle; Arc Solver bends a chain evenly; an IK constraint reaches for a target bone (chain length, max stretch) | Moho | v1 |
| Bone display (Bone tab > Viewport Display, any frame), as Blender's: a Shape, Bone or Custom (a vector layer, picked from a list, dropped from the Layers editor, or with the eyedropper from the viewport or the Layers editor): the layer's drawing as on the setup frame, where that layer sits at rest, moving with the bone (its hidden edges, the layer's own or a reference's, left out); clicking anywhere on it picks the bone, and a missing layer draws a bone. A Bone Color: Default Colors, Blender's 15 theme colour sets, or a Custom Color Set of three swatches (Regular, Select, Active; choosing it starts from the set the bone had); bones, pins and custom shapes are drawn in their set's regular shade, and its selected or active shade when picked, see-through as bones are. Copy Bone Color to Selected gives the other selected bones the active bone's colours. Custom Bone Shapes in Viewport Overlays (on by default) draws the shapes; off, every bone is drawn as a bone. While bones are moved on the setup frame (G, R, S, a drag or the gizmo), the moving ones are drawn as bones until the move ends, so their ends show. Moho's pin bones (no length) convert as Pin bones (below). Offsets for custom shapes, and drawing one at another bone: Later | Blender, Moho | v1 |
| Custom bone shapes: draw a bone as any vector shape | Blender | v1 |
| Bone colours and colour-group visibility | Both | v1 |
| Action bones (action bone actions): with one bone selected, the top of the timeline's action menu lists that bone's action bone actions and, while it has fewer than two, New Bone Action (an action named after the bone; the second, "<bone> 2", turns the other way); action bone actions are listed apart from ordinary ones. Turned past the action's last frame, the bone holds that frame (one drag keeps turning the same way past half a turn). What they add is kept apart from the data, so saving, undo and keying never bake it in | Moho | v1 |
| Fix actions (correctives: Maya's combination shapes, Blender's corrective shape keys, without Moho's extra bone; poses after Live2D's keyform grid): one fix action per set of action bone actions playing together, fixing one or more of their poses. When action bone actions playing together don't look right (a head turned and tilted up), turn their bones there on the mainline and choose **Fix This Pose**: at the top of the timeline's action menu under **Playing now** (each action bone action turned at least 5%, at the frame of it that plays: head_turn 30 · HeadTurnUp 49; one turned less doesn't count) or in the Armature tab's **Fix Actions** panel. It makes the fix if there is none ("New Fix Action for This Pose", named after its action bone actions, HeadTurnUp × head_turn; renaming a bone renames it too, unless it was renamed by hand), opens its pose here if it has one within 5% on each action bone action ("Edit Fix: … · pose 2"), or adds this pose ("Add This Pose to Fix: …"), starting from what the fix played there, so nothing changes until you key. A pose is labelled as it is kept, by each action bone action's frame (head_turn 45 · HeadTurnUp 20), everywhere it shows. Each pose has its own frame in the fix (10, 20, …; a fix saved before poses keeps its one pose on its last frame), marked on the ruler (the pose shown named in full). The fix opens in violet ("Fix: …") on that frame, its action bones held at the pose (outlined in violet and marked held; while it's edited they can't be picked, picking one says why, and their Properties fields are greyed out) and the viewport saying "Editing fix: … \| pose 2 of 2: …", overlays or not; on a frame between poses it warns, in red, that keys there don't play. Key the correction there. On the mainline each pose plays exactly as keyed at its pose; between poses they blend on a grid, as Live2D blends keyforms: along each action bone action its rest and every share a pose has, and inside a cell the correction blends linearly from its corners (rest adds nothing), so moving one action bone action blends only the poses on that line and a knob moved straight moves things straight; a corner no pose was made on is filled in along each action bone action from the nearest made corners either side, averaged (holding the last past it); one pose alone fades with the product of its action bone actions' shares of it (Maya's Multiplication), so a fix is none while any of its action bone actions rests and all there at or past a pose. It plays after the action bone actions, never turning an action bone, and what it moves shows pink. Fixes add up: with three action bone actions turned, Fix This Pose makes a fix for all three, keyed on top of the fix for two of them (which plays under it while it's edited, and goes on playing with it), and the menu also offers "Only a × b: …" to fix the pose in the smaller fix instead. The panel: each fix with how much of it plays now, Edit (its pose nearest the pose now) and Delete; Playing Now and the Fix This Pose button; for a fix of two action bone actions a pose map (one across, the other up, rest at the bottom left): a dot per pose and a cross where the pose is now; click a dot to edit it, right-click it to remove it (a fix keeps at least one), click elsewhere to add a pose there; with three or more, a list. Deleting a pose's keys removes the pose, and the fix goes with its last one (undo brings them back). While one is edited: what it fixes, the map, the pose shown, Remove This Pose and Back to Mainline. The action menu lists Actions, Action Bone Actions and Fix Actions apart (each fix with how much plays now); right-click any for Rename… (not an action bone action: rename its bone) or Delete. Deleting an action bone action breaks its fixes (red, saying why; kept with their keys; they play nothing). Saved with the file. Python: layer.actions, layer.fix_actions ({name, drivers, poses [{frame, at, shares, label}], strength, nearest_pose, broken}), layer.action_bone_actions_playing; oanim.ops.anim.fix_action_new(drivers="a,b"), fix_pose_add(name=, shares="0.5,0.3"), fix_pose_remove(name=, pose=N), fix_pose_snap(name=, pose=N) (its action bones put at that pose, keyed as any move), action_set(name=, frame=), action_rename(name=, to=) | Maya, Blender, Live2D, Moho | v1 |
| 2 Axis Controllers (Toon Boom's Master Controller, on action bones; anim/controller.hh): a bone's type is Regular, 2 Axis Controller or Pin, set by **Convert To** in the bones' right-click menu or Type in the Bone tab, on the setup frame and the mainline. A **Pin** (Moho's pin bone) is one point, drawn as a pin with its direction tick and a Pin Size kept on screen whatever the zoom, whatever its Shape: G moves it, R and S work, it traces one motion path (at the pin), and it has no tail or length (none in the Bone tab or Python), its length kept hidden so converting back gives the bone it was. Nothing connects to a pin: converting lets its connected children go where they are, Ctrl P and the Bone tab's Connected keep the offset, E grows a bone from the pin hanging with an offset, and an IK chain stops at it. Files from when Pin was a shape open as the Pin type; Python: bone_convert(type="PIN"). A controller draws as a box with a knob, and its four sides each play an action, picked in the Bone tab (a side with an action has a pencil that opens it, shown pressed while it is open, clicked again back to the mainline; an empty side has + to make a new one and open it, and M to make Left from Right, mirroring the other side's; the list's New Action at the bottom makes one, puts it in the side and opens it). The knob's position is the action frame: its position keys hold frames, right and up positive, so a side reaches as far as its action is long (its last key; an action with no keys yet counts as 24), and 30 frames one way and 40 the other make the box off-centre around the rest point. Frame Size says how far a frame is, the same both ways, so the box's shape is how long its actions are; it is fitted when actions are picked until set by hand or by dragging the box's corners on the setup frame, which keeps that shape; it never moves a key off its pose, and nor does lengthening an action; past a shortened action's end a key shows its last frame. A side with no action isn't there, so Left and Right alone make a slider. The sides add, as action bones do, and Fix This Pose works from a corner as from two bones, a dot in the box marking each pose fixed. The Bone tab's **Fixes** panel lists the fixes its sides drive (linked dials' actions too): each with how much plays now, Edit and its checkbox (unchecked: muted, as in the Actions panel), right-click for Delete Fix; its poses under it as they are kept (turn_r 45 · look_up 20), the one nearest the knob in violet; click a pose to put the knob there (keyed as any move; not on frame 0 or inside an action bone or fix action), double-click it to open the fix on it; + is Fix This Pose and − removes the picked pose (the nearest until one is clicked; the fix goes with its last one). Its **Spots** panel keeps named places for the knob, the rig's (any frame, never keyed, one undo step each), on a small copy of the box: a square at each spot (filled while the knob is on it), the fix poses' dots and a cross where the knob is. Click a square to put the knob there, keyed as any move (Shift: keyed held there, Step, even without Auto Keying; not on frame 0 or inside an action bone or fix action); click or drag elsewhere to move the knob (Ctrl: onto the nearest spot, fix pose, rest or crossing of keys), one undo step. Hover a square for its name, where it is as kept (turn_r 30 · look_up 10) and a picture of its pose (the bone layer drawn from a posed copy, every spot framed alike); a moment later the viewport shows that pose, drawn from a copy so nothing moves, keys or saves. Right-click: Move Here (where the knob is; a fix pose on the spot asks whether it moves too, and stays where one of its actions would rest), Fix This Pose Here (the knob there, Fix This Pose for its two actions), Rename, Delete. Beside the box + (where the knob is: Spot 1, …), − (the spot the knob is on) and the menu's Add Corners (rest, each side's end, each corner, named after their actions; none where a spot is); under it the name of the spot the knob is on, to rename it, or where the knob is. In the viewport each spot is a small square in the box, named under the mouse; clicking one puts the knob there (Shift: held). Ctrl while dragging the knob lands it on the nearest of its spots, fix poses, the rest point and the places its keys cross. Symmetrize gives the other controller its spots, Left and Right swapped (Wink_L becomes Wink_R). The knob works like a pin inside its box: drawn as a pin with its direction tick, it traces one motion path (at the knob, the Motion Path tool and timing chart re-timing how its actions play), and R turns it (keyed, shown as Knob Angle in the Bone tab; it plays no action); posed, the box stays where it rests, riding on its parent (Box Parent in the Bone tab). **Knob Is Independent** (Relations, setup frame, off by default): the box moves with its Box Parent while the knob doesn't: it keeps its place and direction in the picture, or rides its **Knob Parent** (a dropdown shown when it is on, with search and eyedropper; not the controller or what hangs from it), so where it sits in the box, and what it plays, changes: eyes keep looking at one spot as the head moves, or follow a target; it never leaves the box (held at the edge), and its keys, spots and the Knob field place it against its Knob Parent as that rests (Python knob_independent, knob_parent; bone_relations_set); it picks its frames after the actions and fixes that move its box or Knob Parent have played (a head turn and its fixes before the eyes riding it), so what plays is where it sits. **Contain Knob in Box** (Bone tab, on by default): the knob stays inside its box, held at the edge, dragging too (a drag goes from where it is seen, so dragging back moves it at once); off, it goes where its keys and parents put it, past the box too, what hangs from it with it, its actions playing the nearest point inside (a ring on the edge, a faint line to the knob; Python contain_knob, bone_set(contain_knob=)). What hangs from the knob moves and turns with it: bones with **Follow Knob** (Bone tab > Relations, on for new children; off, a bone rides the box, as children in files from before do), layers parented to it and points weighted to it (automatic weights still leave it out); while one of its actions is edited (a side's, or a fix it drives) the knob sits at that action's frame (in a fix, held at its pose's corner), as Moho poses a smart bone in its action, so what hangs from it moves there as it will on the mainline. **Side timing**: in a side's own action the knob moves along that side's axis only (dragged or bone_set(knob=)), and its keys there say how far out it is on each frame; on the mainline how far out the knob is picks the frame through those keys (straight between them, evenly past the last; with none, frame N is N Frame Sizes out). Keys never go back (a lower one, or one at rest, is held at the one before), linked dials, typed frames and moved fix poses go through the timing, the box reaches where the knob plays the action's end (Frame Size still fits the actions' lengths, so keying never rescales it), spots, fix poses, ticks and corners follow the timing, the keys never move the knob on the mainline, and deleting them gives even timing back. **Constraints** on a controller: those that place a bone (Look At, Copy Position, Child Of, Limit Shape, Follow Point, Dynamics) move its knob, its keys on top, and its actions play from where they put it (Dynamics springs the knob as it would a bone's head; dragging, snapping and going to a spot start from where the knob is seen); those that turn one (Copy Angle, Track To, Independent Angle) turn the box with the knob in it; the rest can't be added (the menu lists only these). Look At with an Eye Shape looks as on any pupil (the shape says how big the eye is, and the knob stays inside it); with none, the box is the eye, and looking all the way to a side takes the knob to that edge. Its actions play from where the look puts the knob on that frame however the frame was reached: action bone actions are played once more after what poses bones later (Look At in an Eye Shape, Follow Point, Limit to Shape, outer parents; `posed_after_action_bones` in `src/anim/animation.cc`). They are off while one of its actions is edited, so that keys a clean pose; a faint ring shows where its keys alone would have the knob. Nothing connects to a knob, as to a pin: converting lets connected children go where they are, Ctrl P and Connected keep the offset, E grows a bone from it with an offset, and an IK chain stops at it. S has nothing to act on. Its frames are never read as a place: Copy Position's Follow Move, Apply Pose as Rest Pose, Keep Poses and Auto-Freeze Keys leave the box and its keys alone (Auto-Freeze and I key the knob, never a linked dial; a linked dial keyed by hand, on the setup frame too, has its keys moved onto the knob in the same undo step). The box lies upright when converted; R on the setup frame turns it with the bone (controller_box_angle), a slanted box running the knob along it. Pressing the knob drags it at once (Ctrl lands it on the nearest spot, fix pose, rest or crossing of its actions' keys, ticks on the box's edges), and only the knob takes clicks, so the box can sit over anything, even on a connected bone; on the setup frame its edges move it. The knob's keys are ordinary position keys: Auto Keying, the timeline, interpolation and Alt G as any bone's; G, the Transform Bones tool's box and its Distort cage move it alike. An ordinary action is played by the controller. An action bone action stays its bone's, so what else turns that bone (a 360 dial made of it) goes on working: the bone (a dial) and the knob are **linked**, one set of keys stored on the knob. Moving the knob turns the dial, to the angle its action has keyed at that frame, added as an action's amount is so the dial's plain value stays its setup, and the action plays the knob's frame (a turn an action goes out and back through stands for two); turning the dial moves the knob, to the first frame with that turn; a knob another controller's action moves (a master controller) turns its dials too; selecting either shows the same keys, and I on the dial keys the knob. Linked fields are teal. The knob has one place on an axis, so while it is on one side the other side's dial is **held** at rest (marked, and the status bar says why); moving back to rest is never refused, turning both together moves neither, and a dial stops at its action's ends. A ring in the box shows where its actions really play when something else turns a dial. The link is the mainline's: inside an action the dial is keyed as ever, and the knob shows the frame. Linking moves the dial's keys onto the knob, saying how many, and when the knob has keys on that axis too it asks whose stay (keep=). Emptying a side, deleting the controller or converting it back gives the dials real keys that move them the same way (converting back, the knob's frames for those sides go with them, the rest staying as moves). Converting a dial itself puts its actions in Right and Left and its angle keys on the knob, taking them from a controller they were in, keys and all, and warning if another action turns it (add a new bone for the controller instead); converting back restores them. Symmetrize with Mirror Action Bone Actions puts the mirrored actions in the other controller's sides; a side named after its bone mirrors to one named after the other (Pupil_L Left into Pupil_R Right), and it never gives a controller its own other side or mirrors one of a bone's actions over another of the same bone. A copy (Shift D) has no sides, and the timing keys it had in them stay behind. Saved with the file; Python: a bone's type, controller_actions, controller_frame_size, controller_box, knob, knob_shown, knob_playing, controller_fix_poses and on a dial controller and held; controller_spots ([(name, (x, y))]); bone_set(knob=, knob_snap=True (as Ctrl), controller_frame_size=), knob= refused on frame 0; oanim.ops.armature.bone_convert, controller_side (side=, action=, keep=), controller_action_new, controller_mirror, controller_spot_add (name=, x=, y=), controller_spot_remove, controller_spot_rename (to=), controller_spot_order (direction=), controller_spot_go (hold=), controller_spot_move (fix=FOLLOW or KEEP), controller_spot_fix, controller_spots_corners (each bone= by name, else the active bone). Later: a round area, controllers shown while working in a drawing. | Toon Boom, Moho | v1 |
| Actions panel (the Armature tab, as Blender's Shape Keys; anim/animation.hh): the bone layer's actions in a list, Mainline first, each row with its kind (a dot: played by its own Frame; a dial: an action bone action; a box: a controller's side; a diamond: a fix, indented under the action bone action that drives it last), its **Frame** with its key button, and a checkbox (unchecked: muted, it doesn't play, its keys kept). Clicking a row edits that action (double-click renames; right-click Rename and Delete); beside the list + (new), − (delete the one edited), a menu (New Fix for This Pose, Mirror Action Bone Actions, Clear Frames) and up and down (order: earlier actions win on a switch). One set of keys per action, never a driver between two: an action with no bone or knob plays at its own Frame, keyable (`actions["name"].frame`; 0 is not playing; set on the mainline, off frame 0); an action bone action's Frame is where its bone has it, and setting it turns the bone (the keys on the bone); a controller's is where the knob has it along its side (the keys on the knob); a fix shows how much of it plays. Under the list, the edited action's **Loop** (past its last frame it starts over, a whole number of rounds ending on its last frame; on by itself for a 360 action bone action, and turning it off holds that one at its end), its length, and **Linked To** (a click selects the bone or knob). Python: `layer.actions` (frame, frame_by, holder, mute, loop, length), `anim.action_play_set(name=, frame=, mute=, loop=)`, `anim.action_move`, `anim.action_frames_clear` (a Frame that isn't a number is refused). Symmetrize's Mirror Action Bone Actions gives the other side the same Loop. Range limits and Solo come later. | Blender, Moho | v1 |
| Local armature viewport: a per-armature canvas for control bones and action bone dials (see Core concepts) | Open Animate | v1 |
| Symmetrize a rig using \_L and \_R names, including action bone actions | Blender, community | v1 |
| Apply Pose as Rest Pose and Apply Selected as Rest Pose (Armature menu > Apply, or Ctrl A over the viewport, as Blender's pose-mode Apply menu): the bones' pose on the frame shown, in the action shown, becomes the setup pose (frame 0), for all bones or the selected ones. Their position and angle do; a keyed scale stays a pose (the setup has none), and what action bone actions add stays out of the rest (they still add it). Unlike Blender's, the drawings come along: the setup drawing is bent to the new rest (points, stored handles, and layers moved by a bone as a whole). Every keyed pose in the mainline and every action shows as it did: the bones' keys are worked out again against the new rest, a timeline with no keys for a moved bone gets one (frame 1 on) holding its old rest, and with Keep Animation (on by default, in the Adjust Last Operation panel) the points the new rest bends differently are keyed back where they showed on each keyed frame, as near as a frame that squashes them nearly flat (an eye seen edge-on) allows: a point goes at most three times as far as an unsquashed one would need, never units away to make up a little across the squash. Frames before a timeline's first key ease out of the new rest, and between keys a point blended between bones may differ slightly. An action bone keeps its rest, so its actions play from the same turns (the report says so); with Apply Selected, the bones left out keep where they rest. One undo step; refused on the mainline's frame 0. Python: oanim.ops.armature.apply_pose_as_rest(), apply_selected_as_rest(keep_animation=) | Blender | v1 |
| Bone Follows Selection (Layer tab > Bones, under Deform, for drawings and groups): on every frame the selected points (all of the layer's when none are; a group's: those of the drawings inside it, its bone the one it follows) are keyed in the timeline being edited, the layer's parent bone is keyed where the selection shows, with Move Only Bones (the drawings it bends are keyed back where they showed, and layers or groups it moves as a whole have their transform keyed back), each new key moving on as the selection's keys do. The bone's own keys on other frames there are fitted too (an older key, such as an Auto-Freeze one from before it moved, would pull it away for a frame), and Auto-Freeze Keys doesn't key the other bones meanwhile. So are the keys of what it moves (the drawings it bends, the layers it moves as a whole), and every new key takes the interpolation of the selection's key it follows. A group it moves as a whole that holds the selection gives the bone its motion: its drawings' points are keyed back where they showed and its own transform is left alone, unless a layer in it has a keyed turn or size (then its transform is keyed back, which stays exact between keys). Move Children (on) brings the bone's children along; off, they are keyed to stay where they were, so their keys hold their move from it. For a bone that follows a feature through an action bone action, as an eye through a head turn. Keep Offset (on) moves the bone by the selection's move from frame 0, off puts its head onto the selection's middle; Fit takes the selection's Move, Move & Turn, or Move, Turn & Scale (a best fit), both in the Adjust Last Operation panel. The bone's children come along. Exact on the keyed frames; with Fit turning, the drawing may drift a little between keys. One undo step; refused on the mainline's frame 0 or when the selection has no keys there. Python: oanim.ops.layer.bone_follow_selection(keep_offset=, fit=MOVE|TURN|SCALE, move_children=) | Moho, Blender | v1 |
| Controlling bones (Copy Angle / Position / Scale with factor and delay), Independent Angle, Maintain Volume (Moho's Squash & Stretch), as bone constraints. Copy Position goes to where its target's base is (To Target, as Blender's Copy Location; Factor the way there, whatever its parents do), or copies the target's move from its setup pose (Its Move, Moho's control bones; files saved before keep it). A constraint's Target is picked from a list with a search field, as Parent Bone's, or with its eyedropper by clicking the bone. Python: bone_constraint_set(follow_move=) | Moho, Blender | v1 |
| Bone Relations, as Blender's: a Relations panel in the Bone tab for the active bone. Parent (a searchable list and an eyedropper; on the setup frame; the bone stays where it is), Connected (setup frame: its head goes onto the parent's tail), Local Location (its Transform reads along the bone as it rests, X down it; off, along its parent's axes; only the numbers shown change, never the pose or the keys), Inherit Rotation (posing, it turns as its parent has turned from its setup pose; off, it keeps its own angle in the picture, as Moho's Independent Angle, its head still carried by the parent), Inherit Scale (Full: the parent's change in size, its squash and stretch too; Uniform: only its overall size; None: it keeps its own size), Keep Animation (off by default, kept while Open Animate is open: changing Inherit Rotation or Scale keys the bone's angle and scale back on every frame keyed on it or a bone above it, in the mainline and every action, so each still shows as it did; where a parent is turned and stretched at once a Full-scale child is skewed either way, and is kept as near as an angle and a scale can), and the bone collections it is in with their eye and star. The setup pose always hangs from the parent as it rests; moving, turning and scaling posed bones takes the relations into account, and so do Limit Angle's lines (measured from the parent's rest direction for a bone that doesn't inherit its turn). Python: oanim.ops.armature.bone_relations_set(bone=, connected=, inherit_rotation=, inherit_scale=FULL/UNIFORM/NONE, local_location=, keep_animation=); bones' inherit_rotation, inherit_scale and local_location | Blender | v1 |
| Bone dynamics (after Spine's physics constraints, Live2D and Cartoon Animator's spring bones rather than Moho): a Dynamics constraint (always last) makes a bone hang like a weighted strand. It follows what moves it on every frame it is simulated through, action bone actions included: hair hanging from a head an action bone turns swings with the turn and settles onto the turned head, and editing an action bone action's keys starts the simulation again. Angle: when what it hangs from moves or turns, the strand's tip stays behind and swings back (a run of dynamic bones is one strand, thrown off as a piece over its whole length, each bone then aiming from where the one above has swung to, so a wave runs down it). Position: the head lags and jiggles. Squash & Stretch: moving along the bone stretches or squashes its length, and, as an elastic pendulum, its own swing pulls it longer the faster it goes (levelling off, so a whip stretches it most at its fastest point) and gravity and the wind along it stretch or squash it (with Maintain Volume it thins as it stretches). Each channel has Settle Speed (how quickly the swinging dies down, about half a second at 1) and Bounce (how far past it swings, 0 to 90%, 30% by default: forward, back, still); Angle and Position add Inertia (how much movement throws it off; 0% moves rigidly); Squash & Stretch adds Stretch (how stretchy: movement and swing); and each has an optional soft limit, ticked on beside its value (Max Swing, Max Distance; Squash & Stretch has two, Max Stretch and Max Squash, shown as times its length, 1.5× and 0.7× ready, and squashing never goes below 0.1× limit or not; files saved before Max Squash hold their one limit both ways). The bone as a whole has a Weight (how heavy it is: how hard gravity pulls it, down, and along the bone for Squash & Stretch) and a Wind Catch (how much the wind catches it, 100% by default); as in Unreal's AnimDynamics the wind doesn't follow Weight, since it pushes on a surface, not a mass. Moving the bone layer counts as movement. Moho's spring, damping and weight, the earlier Torque, and the earlier Weight of each channel (Angle's becomes the bone's), convert. Simulated from the Simulation start (bones start already bent by gravity and the wind, and already moving with them when keys are changing them) and cached (while something is being dragged the frames before the shown one stay as they are, and they are simulated again once the mouse comes up); gravity and wind in the Document tab, or a bone's own with Own Gravity and Own Wind, each ticked on its own (starting as the document's the first time it is ticked, and kept while unticked). The wind isn't steady, as Unity's wind zones: Gusts make it stronger and weaker and waver its direction, sweeping across the drawing like a wave through grass (timed by where each bone sits in the setup pose, so a moving bone doesn't run through them), Gust Speed sets how often they come, and Turbulence adds a small, fast flutter along and across it (from the frame, so repeatable). The wind drags on how fast the air goes past a bone, as Houdini's, Disney's and Unreal's wind do: moving into it a bone is pushed harder, moving with it hardly at all, and strong wind calms swings across it; a still bone bends as before, and without wind there is no air drag (settling is Settle Speed's). Gravity, the wind settings and the Dynamics numbers are animatable, each with its key button; as settings they are keyed by hand (I and auto-keying leave them alone until one is keyed, then edits key it), and the document's show as a Simulation row in the timeline; the simulation feels keyed gravity and wind eased (averaged over about a tenth of a second, never reaching back before the simulation start to the setup frame, and smooth between frames), so a key where a ramp stops doesn't jolt the bones; Bake to keys (on a 2 Axis Controller the springs move its knob in its box, an angle spring turning the knob, the box resting, and the bake keys the knob). With Physics Ghosts on (Viewport Overlays, the default), bones are drawn, picked and edited where their keys put them, so a dynamic bone lands exactly where it is put, and pale blue ghosts show where the dynamics have them (the artwork follows the ghosts); off, bones are drawn where the dynamics have them. With Live Physics on (Viewport Overlays, the default), dynamics run live: while a pose changes on a frame (a bone dragged or turned), the dynamic bones simulate in real time, trailing, swinging and settling as you work, and stay as the live simulation has them until the frame changes (scrubbing or playing shows the frames' own results); it is only shown, so keys, the cache, playback and baking stay repeatable. Collisions and a keyable reset: Later | Spine, Live2D | v1 |
| Deform modifiers (smart warp): Curve Deform (an open line), Cage Deform (a closed shape) and Mesh Deform (a grid, a hand-made mesh of cells or a closed shape as one cell) on another layer bend a drawing as it moves from where it is on frame 0; Automatic or Custom (painted) weights; on a group, every drawing inside it; Follow Deform carries a drawing on a shape without bending it | Moho, Live2D, Blender, Toon Boom | v1 |
| Rigid-body physics | Moho | Maybe |
