Its panel has Blender's rows: **Width Type** (what Amount measures: Offset, how far along each side from the corner the cut starts; Width, how long the cut is; Depth, how far the cut is from the corner; Percent, how much of each side it takes), **Amount** (keyable and drivable, with its key button), **Limit Method** (None: every point between two segments; Angle: only the corners where the outline turns more than the **Angle**, 30 degrees by default, measured on the curve itself, handles and all, so a smooth point is never cut; Point Group: only the points of the **Point Group** picked from a dropdown with a search field, each cut by its weight, so a point at 0.5 gets half the cut), then two folded subpanels: **Profile** (its Shape, Blender's filled bar: 0.5 round, a circle's arc; 0.25 straight, a chamfer; 1 square, almost the corner again; below 0.25 scooped inward, at 0 a circle's arc turned in) and **Geometry** (Clamp Overlap, on by default: two cuts on one segment take at most half of it each, so they never cross). Blender's Vertices / Edges switch, Segments and Shading are left out: there are no edges to bevel, one Bezier segment already makes a smooth round, and shading is 3D's. Each cut corner becomes two points, one on each side, joined by a new segment that every shape running through the corner takes in; the curve either side keeps its shape exactly, its segments trimmed (de Casteljau), not refitted. Line ends and points where curves weld aren't cut. The first point keeps the corner's id, keys and bone weights, the second is a copy of it, so the bones still move them. Brushed lines lay their stamps along the bevelled setup shape. Apply (the header menu's, or Ctrl A; on Setup) makes the cuts the drawing's own points. Python: oanim.ops.object.modifier_add(type="BEVEL"), layer.modifier_set(index, width_type=, amount=, limit= ("NONE", "ANGLE", "GROUP"), angle= (degrees), group=, profile=, clamp=); drivers and keys: modifiers[i].amount, and drivers on .profile, .angle (radians), .clamp.

**Resample modifier** (called Subdivide at first). Adds points between the drawing's own (which stay as they are, keys, weights and all), evenly in each segment, before the bones bend it, so one long segment bends along a bone chain instead of only at its two points: bendier animation without more points to animate (Blender's Grease Pencil Subdivide, for curves). Add it from Add Modifier > Resample; it goes above the Armature modifier by itself (below it, it says to drag it up). The split is exact, so at rest the drawing looks the same; corners stay corners and the new points are smooth. Once bones or a deform bend the drawing, the edit wire goes through its points, as the line draws, and a click on the wire in Edges or Strokes mode picks the segment of the drawing's own points that it is part of; those points keep their whole handles to edit. **Mode** (two joined buttons, as the bone Shape selector): Per Segment (default) splits every segment into Pieces (4), so it always adds points and how many is easy to tell; By Length puts a point at least every Spacing (0.05) along each segment, cut evenly along it as it is in the setup pose, so the pieces and where they sit stay the same on every frame: the cuts are only worked out again when the setup pose or the settings change, and a drawing that hasn't changed isn't split again, so when only a deform's shape moves, only the bending runs. Under them it says how many points it adds ("Adds 240 points"), and when it adds none, why, in orange ("every segment is shorter than Spacing", "Pieces is 1", no segment touching its point group); Show Points' tooltip gives the same count. The rest is folded under **More**: **Weights** (the same kind of buttons): Blend (default) mixes each new point's two end points' weights by how far along it is, a smooth rubber-hose bend; From Bones weights it from the nearest bones, so a segment can bend at a joint between its points. **Preserve Volume** (on by default) moves the new points by blending the bones' turns as rigid motions (dual complex numbers, Blender's Preserve Volume) rather than their matrices, so the line doesn't pinch where bones turn far apart; the drawing's own points move as before. Render (By Length only): a finer Spacing for the final render, Same by default. Handles the curve works out stay that way in spirit: Resample stores them, and the bones turn and stretch them with the curve around the point rather than rigidly with it. Where neighbouring bones turn very differently and point keys keep a shape right (stiff hair spikes), the blended points bend through the difference: there Resample changes the existing animation's look, and a Point Group or no Resample suits better. Max Pieces (64) caps any segment; Point Group limits it to the segments touching a group's points. Apply (on Setup, on a drawing whose points aren't animated) makes the new points real. Saved with the project; files and scripts saying SUBDIVIDE still work (a default name "Subdivide" opens as "Resample"); Python: object.modifier_add(type="RESAMPLE"), layer.modifier_set(i, split="LENGTH"|"COUNT", spacing=, render_spacing=, pieces=, max_pieces=, weights="BLEND"|"BONES", preserve_volume=, group=).

**Deform modifiers: Curve, Cage and Mesh Deform.** A shape on any other drawing layer bends the layer (Moho's Smart Warp, Live2D's warp deformers, Blender's Curve, Mesh Deform and Lattice), picked in the modifier as a Layer and one of its Shapes, or with the eyedropper at the end of the Shape row (as Boolean's: click the shape in the viewport and its layer comes with it, looking past this layer; one this deform doesn't take still goes in, and the modifier says which does; a layer clicked in the Layers editor goes in with the first of its shapes this deform takes, else its only one); Add Modifier lists the three under Deform, each taking its own shapes: **Curve Deform** an open line, **Cage Deform** one closed shape, **Mesh Deform** a grid, a mesh of cells drawn by hand (or a closed shape of any number of sides, as one cell; four-sided, a grid cell), or lines (an open line, or lines left dangling from a mesh). Their Shape lists offer only the shapes they take (the others greyed, naming the deform that takes them); a shape that stops fitting (joined into cells, closed, opened) makes the modifier say which deform it needs, with a Switch button, and the header menu's Change Type turns one into another, keeping its settings and painted weights. A closed shape fits Cage Deform and Mesh Deform both: Mesh Deform takes it as one cell of any number of sides. Files from when there was one Vector Deform open with each as the deform its shape fits (a line Curve, a single closed shape Cage, cells Mesh). They share everything below. What each does with its shape: a **Grid** (the Shape tool's) bends each cell's contents as its Coons patch, so a grid line curved with its handles curves what lies along it; each side's pull fades out smoothly toward the side opposite (3x² − 2x³, not in a straight line: half way across it is half, by the far side nothing and with no slope), so moving one side or handle barely moves what lies by the others; a closed shape is a **Cage**, what is in it following its outline as drawn, curves and all, so pulling a handle bends the inside, by Cage Deform's **Method** (a dropdown): **Mean Value** (mean value coordinates measured against the outline; smooth, and a point on the outline stays on it, but the inside squashes along a side that bulges out or stretches), **Rigid** (as rigid as possible, Sorkine and Alexa's, as Photoshop's Puppet Warp: a lattice of square cells over the cage, each node keeping how it sits with its four neighbours, turned as they turn together, with the outline held firmly where it goes; how far that moves the inside from Mean Value is laid on the lattice and read back by a cubic B-spline, so it is smooth; the inside keeps its shape as far as it can, squashing least, and the outline stays on the cage, to within about a third of a per cent of its size; for art drawn along the cage's edge; each round of its solve is exact, by the Cholesky factor of the lattice's system, worked out once with the lattice (it is the same every round and every frame), so nothing wobbles as the cage moves) or **Outline** (called Envelope until Envelope Deform came; in files before format 5 a Cage Deform's "ENVELOPE" still reads as it, from format 5 and in Python it is the Envelope method below; the default for a new Follow Deform, and for a new Cage Deform until 2026-11-03; after Toon Boom Harmony's envelopes, without their tearing, which bend a drawing's outlines while keeping its line widths: every point goes where Rigid puts it, then each side of the cage (the outline from one of its points to the next) sets the point's distance from that side back to what it was at rest (measured from the side's near places, each by its say: down to none at half as far again as the nearest, and a fiftieth of the side's length however close the point is, so no place ever jumps to another as the side moves; where a side bent into a hook has two stretches about as near, running opposite ways, they cancel, and a side doubling back around a point lets go of it, the cage as a whole carrying it, since what lies in a hook narrower than twice its distance from the side would have to cross itself), so a line or band along the outline keeps its width, inside the cage and past it, and art hugs the cage however a side bends, stretches or is pushed in; a side sets distances only, never where a point slides along it, so two sides meeting at a corner never pull a point two ways (in Harmony's own each side takes the part of the drawing nearest it and art tears where two meet); a side's say is full at it and less further off, as r / (r + distance) with r the cage's size, so the distance a point ends at always grows with the distance it had and nothing folds over, however hard a side pushes in, and deep inside the cage as a whole leads; the sides near a place share it by nearness, **Blend** (0 to 1, 0.5 by default) saying how evenly: 0, the nearest leads sharply (as the cube of how near it is), 1, gently (as how near it is), sharper than the cube folding a corner that closes up; a band a tenth as wide as the cage along a side pushed in to the middle keeps 88% of its width or more, where Mean Value keeps 6%; about Rigid's work); with Mean Value and Rigid, what lies past the outline keeps its distance from it: each side carries it from where its place on the side goes, turned as the side turns there but not stretched, the sides blended by nearness (inverse fourth power), so a band drawn along the outside stays as wide however a side bends in or out (mean value coordinates past the outline would squeeze it); an open shape is a **Line**, what lies along it keeping its distance along it (measured along the line, as Blender's Curve modifier and skeletal strokes do) and its offset from it; **Stretch** (on) spreads it end to end as the line grows (Illustrator's Art Brush), off it keeps its length and slides along; on the inside of a tight bend, offsets past half the bend's radius ease toward it so nothing folds over. Its place along and across the line is worked out on the line at rest and now alike (the same frame and bend easing), and how far a point is from where that puts it at rest is carried along in the line's frame, so a line that hasn't moved moves nothing and one moved or turned whole carries everything exactly; a point far from the line takes the way it runs over a stretch about as long as the point is far, so it doesn't hitch as the line's samples turn by small corners; the line is followed at 64 pieces a segment (a cage at 16); where the line winds so that a point has a near place on more than one part of it, it is carried from each and they are blended by nearness (the eighth power), as a wire's points are, so the drawing doesn't tear where the nearest part changes. A **network** drawn by hand (lines joined into cells, as Extrude and Make Segment make them) bends each cell's contents by that cell: a four-sided cell as a grid's cell does (so a hand-made grid is a **Grid**, bending as the Shape tool's would), any other as a small cage (**Mesh**); cells sharing a side follow it alike, so they join without a seam; a point on a four-sided cell's curved side counts as in the cell (judged by the side itself, not the polygon of its samples), a point on a side, and a handle along it, follows the bent side itself at the same place along it (exact, also at a corner where two sides run on in line, where the patch's own directions are parallel), and other handles there turn by the patch's own derivatives, near a corner where the two sides run on in line (a smooth shape cut into four sides) the patch's own directions are nearly parallel and it would kink what lies there, so it eases over to the cell as a cage (mean value coordinates on its outline) by how square those directions are; so a drawing that is a copy of the warp shape bends into exactly the bent shape, handles and all (Python: layer.evaluated_handles(curve, shown=False) reads the handles after the modifiers, shown=True as the viewport shows them; layer.evaluated_outline(shape, steps=16) a shape's outline as the evaluated drawing draws it). A Mesh Deform's lines are **wires**, like bendy bones (an open line alone is a **Wire**): each point is pinned on frame 0 to its nearest place on the line as a whole (that segment, that far along it) and its offset from there, and goes where that place goes, turned as the line turns there, so nothing slides along the line (Curve Deform, by contrast, lays what lies along a line out along it by distance); where a line folds back or winds, so that a point has a near place on more than one branch, the pins at each blend by nearness, so nothing jumps between branches; a segment of no length at rest (points stacked on one another, as a piece copied from a fill's outline can have) is left out, having no way to turn anything with; past an end it rides that end; points far from the line follow it smoothed over a stretch as long as they are far (the line carrying on straight past its ends), so a sharp bend is a gentle one to them and offsets never cross (the line at rest smoothed alike, how far a point is from where that puts it carried in the line's frame: a wire that hasn't moved moves nothing, one moved or turned whole carries everything exactly). Points outside a mesh's cells follow its wires and outline together, each the more the nearer. Bent handles take the deform's way and stretch right at their point, so a bent line has no jogs where its pieces meet. Points outside a grid, or outside cells that all have four sides, follow the outline's sides near them: each side carries them as it is at its place nearest the point (where that went, turned as the side runs there, their distance from it kept, so a side growing longer doesn't push what lies past it out and the drawing doesn't swell as the warp moves; just past a side they slide along it as the inside does there (its shear), easing off within about a quarter of the side's length, so a line crossing the outline has no crease, while across the side they keep their distance, so a thin band hugging the outline stays thin however the inside stretches, fading out toward a side's ends where the corners are), the sides blended by the inverse eighth power of distance, so the nearest lead, it is exact on the outline, and moving one side moves only what lies beyond it. Points outside a mesh with other cells follow its outline as a cage from outside (mean value coordinates against it): smooth everywhere, exact on the outline, and far away moving as the outline does as a whole (in a hole of a mesh, that hole's outline). Frame 0 is the rest: reshaping the shape there changes what it measures from and bends nothing (nothing bends on frame 0 at all, even mid-edit); moving or keying its points on other frames (by hand, bones or action bone actions) bends the layer, handles too. **Weights** say how much each point follows: Automatic (every point of the layer fully, past the shape too, as Blender's deform modifiers bend the whole object; never stored) or Custom (a point group named after the modifier, starting from the automatic weights, painted in Weight Paint with the modifier picked in its dropdown at the top of the viewport beside Armature; points it has no weight for take the automatic one; Reset to Automatic fills it again; switching back keeps it). Weight Paint shows Automatic weights as a heat map, with Switch to Custom. A point's weight also shares it between the deform and its bones: at 1 it follows only the shape (which brings the bones' move along when the shape follows them too), at 0 only its bones, so nothing moves twice; it measures from the drawing as it was before the Armature modifier, so it goes below it (where it is added). **Strength** (keyable) scales every weight, to fade a deform in or out. **Precision** (0.002, on by default; 0 Off) is how far a bent line may stray from the true bend: where the deform would bend a segment more than its points and handles can follow, points are added there first (Illustrator's Fidelity), so straight lines bend too and barely bent ones get none. A cut fades in rather than popping in: just past Precision the pieces of a segment's last halving draw exactly as the one piece they were (the point between them where that piece has it, the handles as that piece split there), easing over (in and out) to each piece bent on its own as the bend grows to twice Precision (the drawing is bent a second time without that halving to know where), so as the shape moves and points come and go nothing jumps (a thin band, an eyelid along an eye white, doesn't flick thicker for a step and back); where those points don't all follow the deform fully, the cuts stay as they are. **Resample Self** (on in a new Cage Deform, off in the others; Precision is off while it is on) splits the drawing itself, as a Resample modifier above the deform would, and bends the pieces, so the line follows the bend between the drawing's own points. Under the checkbox are the Resample modifier's rows: Mode (By Length at first, Spacing 0.009, measured in the setup pose, at most 64 pieces a segment; or Per Segment, Pieces) and how many points it adds. The drawing's own points, keys and handles stay as they are and nothing is added to it: the pieces exist only in what shows. It splits on Setup (frame 0) too, which looks the same, so the pieces are the same on every frame. A deform's Custom weights reach the pieces, each eased between its segment's two ends, as they reach the points a Resample modifier above the deform or Precision adds. A layer with Resample Self read as a cage's or a mesh's shape is read by its own points, each segment fitted to the bend. With Precision off (0), each segment's handles are fitted to their bent samples (as a sculpt stroke fits a line back): each handle keeps the way the deform turns it at its point, so a smooth point's two handles stay in line and the bent line has no kinks at its points, and only how long each is gets fitted by least squares (Schneider's fit), blended with the handles as bent by how closely each follows the samples (by the fourth power of how far each strays, on average as the root mean square, so a clearly closer one all but alone; the fit's handles held between a thousandth of the segment's chord and the chord, longer looping), so it barely strays further than without it and a small move never flips a segment from one to the other; a point the deform doesn't move keeps its own, and a segment whose handles as bent already follow the bend within a thousandth of its chord, checked at three places along it, keeps them, the fit easing in as they stray further; so a drawing split by a Resample modifier above the deform, or by Resample Self, the same thing, bends truly (a line's distance from the true bend, an Outline cage at 4 pieces: 0.0038 where fitting whole segments made 0.013), and both together more truly still; with Precision on, it has cut every segment till the handles as bent follow the bend within it. Points and handles show, and are picked and moved, where the deform puts them, and the edit wire between them follows the bend (each segment as the nearest single curve to it, its handle lengths fitted by least squares). Each deform keeps its shape's build (Rigid's and Outline's lattice solved for where the shape is, and the lattice itself, its links and each node's mean value coordinates by where the shape is at rest) and the drawing it bent, so a frame where neither the shape, the drawing nor the modifier has changed (a held frame, the view panned, another layer edited) bends nothing anew; the drawing's points, handles and Precision's checks are shared over the CPU's cores. Rainbow Dash's skirt (Outline, 968 lattice nodes): its cage built in 2.3 ms instead of 7.6 (40 conjugate gradient steps a round were nearly all of it), and Outline's map of each point walks each side once less (its distance from a side is its nearest place's), so a frame whose cage moves costs 4.7 ms of bones and modifiers instead of 11.3, the points bent the same to a ten-millionth. A Points mesh measures at each place only the lines that can still have a say there, and a shape's Resample at rest is kept, since every deform reads it on every update: a character whose eye group bends by a Points mesh drawn as one closed shape, with four Follow Deforms on its lash, stepped frames in 40 ms instead of 90, bent exactly the same. The shape is put into the bent layer's space (at rest and as it is now), and a layer bent by bones into its armature's, from the groups the two share down (layer_relative_transform), so those groups moving (a character walked across, its top group scaled) changes them not even by rounding and the build is kept: Rainbow Dash with its top layers' scale driven, 4.9 ms of bones and modifiers a frame to 0.7, as when it plays unscaled; clay015 so, 7.5 ms of CPU a frame to 5.9. A group's deform reads its shape the same for every drawing in it that doesn't move apart from it, so the drawings share one build. Placing a point in a Mesh Deform's four-sided cell (Newton's method) is done once however many steps ask, a cell the point is well outside of is looked in only when no cell holds it, a point well clear of a cell's side isn't searched for on it, and a mesh of one shape that isn't four-sided is its own cell without measuring: nothing shows differently, and the eye rig (two eye meshes, each eye's drawings in its group) redraws a changed frame in 29 ms of evaluation instead of 55, changes frame in 4.9 ms instead of 8.5 and works out its motion paths in 230 ms instead of 380 (oanim.wm.deform_counts counts that work). A render then bends the drawings it shows side by side on the CPU's cores before drawing any, each exactly as it would be alone: the eye rig's bending on a frame change takes 16 ms instead of 30, and a redraw 24 ms of CPU instead of 38. A shape bent back (through other deforms) by the layer it bends is a loop: neither bends, and the modifier says so. It can't be applied (it changes nothing on frame 0). Saved with the file. Python: modifier_add(type="CURVE_DEFORM" / "CAGE_DEFORM" / "MESH_DEFORM"), layer.modifier_set(index, layer=, shape=, weights="AUTOMATIC"/"CUSTOM", strength=, precision=, stretch=, resample_self=, cage_method="MEAN_VALUE"/"RIGID"/"ENVELOPE" (a file's "GREEN", a method since removed, opens as Mean Value), blend=, type= to change type; modifiers list each one's kind and fits, the deform that takes its shape), layer.deform_weights(index, reset=False). **Range** (Outline, 0 to 1, 1 by default) limits how far through the cage a side that has moved bends what lies around it: 1, the whole cage; half way, as far as the cage is big; less, less. Past it what lies inside goes as the cage does as a whole, and the reach grows with the side's move so nothing folds. A cage no longer reaches across a narrow gap in it (a slit, a mouth drawn nearly shut): Rigid's and Outline's lattice is cut into pieces along the gap, each side with nodes of its own, and an Outline's sides have no say over what they could only reach by going round through the cage. While Range's slider is dragged the viewport tints what it reaches; while Blend's is, it tints violet where two sides share the say (thin lines at 0, wide soft bands at 1), on the setup frame too. Python: range= in modifier_set; layer.deform_range_view(index), layer.deform_blend_view(index). **Envelope**, Cage Deform's fourth Method and Curve Deform's second, is Toon Boom Harmony's envelope deformer itself (the default Method of a new Curve or Cage Deform since 2026-11-03; a new Cage Deform with it has Resample Self off, so it bends the drawing's own points and handles as Harmony does; files and Python that name no method keep Smooth and Mean Value). It was a modifier of its own, Envelope Deform, until 2026-11-03: a file's ENVELOPE_DEFORM opens as Curve Deform on a line and Cage Deform on a loop, with Envelope and every setting and its name kept (tests/test_envelope_retired.py); Add Modifier keeps an Envelope Deform entry and Python type="ENVELOPE_DEFORM" (adding one, or as Change Type), both a Curve Deform with Envelope until its shape is picked; a Curve or Cage Deform with Envelope given a shape (from the list, the eyedropper or Python's shape=, unless type= comes with it) becomes whichever of the two takes it, and Change Type between them keeps Envelope. Measured against Harmony 24 (reports/Harmony envelope deformer.md; tests/test_envelope_deform.py and tests/test_envelope_stress.py check Harmony's own numbers): its shape is one line or loop, open or closed as Harmony's envelopes may be, each segment one of Harmony's Curve nodes. As Harmony's engine does, each segment is a row of 257 frames, at t = k / 256, each running the way of the chord from the sample before it to it (the first, to the next one), not the curve's own tangent; each point of the drawing binds at rest to one frame, the nearest of those whose slab holds it (from the frame's normal line to the next frame's), keeping how far out it is from that frame and how far along it, stretched as the chord to the next frame stretches, and is rebuilt from that frame now alone. The wedge where two segments meet follows the first one's end, past a free end the end frame carries it, neither stretched, and a loop's closing corner is two free ends (its first frame holds what is behind it, its last what is ahead, a tie going to the first); a point no frame holds stays put. No blending, so where two places are about equally near the drawing tears as Harmony's does. Checked on 60 random envelopes, open and closed, bent eight ways (10,140 points): every point not on a tear line within 0.0001 fields of Harmony's, its rounding. Lines bend by their edges, as Harmony's strokes do: each plain line (not a brushed one, which keeps its dabs along the bent curve) is drawn as its outline, both edges and its round ends and joins, cut fine (32 samples a segment) and each place moved by the deform on its own, then filled in the line's colour (Nonzero); so where a line crosses a tear each edge jumps at its own place and the fill between them is a band, or a gap where they twist, as in Harmony's render; a deform after it (in the drawing's stack, or a group's around the one the Envelope Deform is on) bends those edges too, each place on its own, so they stay with the line (a lash in an eye group with an Envelope Deform inside one with a Mesh Deform had stayed where the first left it, off the wire), and what can't carry them (custom weights, bones, Mirror, Bevel, Follow Deform, Boolean) draws the line along its curve again (Python: layer.evaluated_line_edges(shape, render=False), the outline's rings; the line's middle still bends as before, for Precision, the wire and other readers; 200 three-point lines take 32 ms a frame to bend instead of 28). On 17 open envelopes with 4 open pencil strokes each, rendered in Harmony at 90 pixels a field, Open Animate's ink differs from Harmony's (beyond 2 pixels) by 0.5% of it missing and 2.4% extra, nearly all of that in two envelopes where a line lies far outside the envelope about as near both its free ends: there Harmony's renderer keeps an edge on the near end where its own point binding (and Open Animate's) takes the other, and Open Animate draws a band Harmony doesn't (tests/test_envelope_edges.py checks spots on Harmony's bands and gaps). That is its Method's **Standard**; **Blended** (called Weighted until 2026-11-03, too like the Weights setting below it; files saved with WEIGHTED open as Blended, Python takes only BLENDED) is Harmony's Weighted Deform node on the same shape (decoded from its plugin and checked against its renders; tests/test_envelope_weighted.py): each segment is cut into 6 pieces by its own parameter, each piece offers its nearest place at rest, with that place's move (where it is now less where it was), and the point moves, never turning, by those moves shared by cardinal interpolation with a linear radial basis (weights w with the sum over j of w_j times the distance between places i and j equal to the point's distance from place i plus Pre-Blend's ln(1 + it), for every i; negative weights dropped; Post-Blend above 0 raises them to the power 1 + it, below 0 makes them 1 - (1 - w) to the power 1 - it, before they become shares of their sum), so nothing tears and the drawing far away moves by about the shape's average move (Harmony keeps every two places at least 0.01 apart in the solve, which on a small shape of many segments, such as a resampled eye warp, flattens it; here that floor is a millionth of the shape's size, so a small rig bends as a large one scaled; each point's weights are worked out once and kept, so moving the shape only re-blends them); Pre-Blend (0 or more) evens the sharing out near the shape, and Post-Blend below 0 can swing far points wide, as in Harmony. Harmony measures in its scene's 4:3 fields as they show, in units where the frame is 2 tall as Open Animate's are, so a Harmony rig matches with x scaled by 4/3. Standard's **Influence** is Harmony's region of influence (measured on Harmony 24's Curve deformer; reports/Harmony curve deformer.md; tests/test_curve_influence.py checks 1,340 of Harmony's points, most in fades and seams): **Everywhere** (Harmony's Infinite, the default: as above) or **Capsule** (its Elliptic). With Capsule each segment of the shape reaches only so far: a band out to its left (looking the way it runs; **Left**) and right (**Right**), offset from 16 frames along it at rest each turned as the chord into it, and past its start and end a half ellipse (**Begin**, **End**) along its first and last chord and centred on the middle of the band, so uneven sides set the cap off the line; **Symmetric** (on by default) makes Right Left and Begin End, shown as Width and Length. Inside a capsule a point is carried by that segment alone, fully; outside every one by the nearest, less the further out, to nothing at **Fade** past it (where it stays put); where capsules overlap the later segment leads, and near the edge it shares with an earlier one a point is shared between the two, half and half on the edge and all its own's a Fade in. Each share is eased as Harmony's fade exponent of 3 eases it (below a half, half of twice it cubed; above, a half plus half of twice it less one cubed), so a seam stays near half and half across most of its width. All in layer units; the defaults are Harmony's (Width 1/12, a field high; Length 1/9 and Fade 1/18, a field and half a field wide), and a Harmony rig's radii come over as across times 0.1 and along and fade times 0.1333 at the scale above. Against Harmony's whole grids the influence is off 0.0007 on average; just round an end cap's tip Harmony's capsule is a little flatter than a half ellipse, so a few points there are off up to 0.15 of a move. Python: influence="EVERYWHERE"/"CAPSULE", influence_symmetric, influence_left, influence_right, influence_begin, influence_end, influence_fade. **Curve Deform's Method** is **Smooth** (as below) or **Envelope** (the default for a new one): the envelope deformer above on its line (or its Part), with its own Envelope setting (Standard, which is Harmony's Curve deformer, or Blended), Pre-Blend, Post-Blend and Influence; it has no Stretch (Envelope always stretches along), and takes an open line or a Part. Cage Deform's Envelope is the same on a closed shape, the Envelope rows under its Method. Python: curve_method="SMOOTH"/"ENVELOPE", cage_method="ENVELOPE". Harmony's Curve deformer also poses its chain as bones (each Curve node's place and handles in its parent's end frame); Open Animate's line is posed by its points and bones instead, so that is not copied. Mesh Deform's Method, on a grid or cells that all have four sides, is Cells or Outline (called Envelope until 2026-11-03, and "ENVELOPE" in files and Python still reads as it; with Cage Deform's Blend). With Outline the outer cells keep what lies near the outline at its distance from the outline's sides, so lines along the edge keep their width. That eases off from each side to the mesh inside the outer cells (its lines and nodes off the outline), where it is gone, so inner cells, nodes and lines are exactly where Cells puts them. Where a cell squeezes it is held back as the square of the squeeze, so art folds about where the cell itself would. A side has its say only within reach of the outer cell around a point, so an edge cut into many sides works as it does in few; a side lets go as it shrinks to nothing, and one left with handles too long for it is read without the parts that run back on themselves. Python: mesh_method="CELLS"/"OUTLINE"/"POINTS" in modifier_set. **Points** (a third Method, on any mesh) makes every line of the mesh (each segment between two of its points) a bone: what lies near a line keeps its place by it, turned as the line turns there and never stretched along it, so a point's handles turn what lies by that point and a curved line carries what lies along it. A place is shared by the lines near it: the nearest leads, by Blend, and the others fade out by **Falloff** (0 to 1, 0.25 by default, a share of the mesh's size) past the nearest one's distance, measured through the mesh (across its cells and along its lines), so nothing reaches across a gap; the weights are worked out at rest and never change as the mesh moves. What lies off a line is as far off as the lines leaving its ends that way have stretched, and slides as they have sheared, so a cell pulled taller carries its art evenly, as Cells does, not in waves; past those lines' far ends it keeps its rest distance, so a stretch stays in the cells that stretched. With few points each side is a long bone, so the modifier warns of low point density (on the setup frame, a side longer than half the shape's width or height, a Resample modifier on the shape's layer counted) and suggests that Resample. While Falloff's or Blend's slider is dragged the viewport tints where the lines share. Python: falloff= in modifier_set; the modifier's sparse; layer.deform_falloff_view(index). **Smooth** (a checkbox, off by default, on a grid drawn as one, not a mesh of cells drawn by hand) bends by each row and column of the grid as one smooth line through its nodes, in place of its sides' own handles, at rest and as it is now, so what the grid bends has no corners at the nodes (on a mouth cage with no handles, the sharpest turn from one sample of a line to the next goes from 26° to 0.6°). Only the nodes are keyed, each line goes through every one, and the grid itself draws as it is. A node's tangent is the average of the steps either side, cut back if along either step it is over three times that step (Fritsch and Carlson's limit, so a line never runs past the next node however squeezed a cell is), and none where the line turns back; the limit is measured along the steps, not in x and y, so a grid turned whole keeps the same curves and what it bends just turns. A straight, even grid stays as it is. Spacing is smoothed as well as direction, so on a pose with a squeezed column what lies in the cells beside it moves toward the squeeze (a column squeezed eight times moved a mouth by up to 0.011 of a cage 0.088 wide); moving the nodes takes it back. Python: smooth= in modifier_set. **Part** (Curve Deform) follows one stretch of its shape, not all of it, so one closed shape can drive two drawings: a closed mouth line that is shut at rest has no inside to measure from, and as a cage it throws what it bends far off (a lip moved 2.76, about 30 mouth widths), but its top and bottom as two Parts carry each lip by its own side, with no extra layers or keys and nothing to drift apart. On the shape's layer, select the points along the stretch, end to end, and press Use Selected Points on the modifier (two at least, one run, not all the way round); Other Side swaps its two ends, which on a closed shape is the rest of the way round, and Clear takes it away. Curve Deform takes a closed shape for this, doing nothing until it has a Part. The Part is kept as its two end points' ids, so it stays as the shape changes, and the stretch is an open line as any other (Stretch and all): if an end point is deleted the deform does nothing rather than guess. Picking another layer or shape clears it. Python: part=(from, to) point numbers, "SELECTED" or None in modifier_set. A line dragged into a kink or a loop carries what follows it without a jump: keeping a point from folding over on the inside of a bend fades out as the line turns right back on itself, where neither side is the inside (near a half turn, from three quarters of one), and where a line has doubled back so that the chord a far point reads its way from is nearly nothing, it reads the way the line runs where it is. The moment a line comes to a sharp point it has no way it runs there, so a small step is left (0.006 on a mouth 0.15 wide, from 0.023). A reference with Pose: Same as Original bends by its original's deforms too, so a mask made of one fits the bent drawing, and a point moved on it lands where it is put; a drawing bent by a reference of itself is a loop, and does nothing. A dragged point lands under the cursor, on a drawing's own deforms as on a group's: its skin takes what the whole stack does right around it, its points moved a small step each way and worked out again, so bones and each point's share between them count. A skin only holds around where a point was, so a move goes on correcting until the point shows at its target, and a handle moved by itself likewise; a handle changes by how far it is moved rather than being worked back from how it shows bent, so grabbing one leaves the line as it was. With Resample Self the line is its pieces, so a handle's end is shown and moved as a place of its own, where the deforms put it, by the stretch at that place: a handle can reach out of a cell squeezed many times over that its point is in. A stack with a Bevel or a Boolean shows its dots through the deforms too. On a group (a Group, Bone or Switch layer) the Modifiers tab offers these three deforms only, and they bend every drawing inside it, however deep, a switch's hidden drawings too; images and video stay as they are. Each drawing runs its own stack first, then the groups' deforms, nearest group first, with Automatic weights, so there is no Weights row or Apply on a group; its Show Points marks what it adds on every drawing inside, while the group is active. The shape's movement counts once: a drawing the shape's bones don't move, one with no weights among them, follows it whole, movement and shape, its own bones running first; one the same bones move takes only the change of shape. Nothing bends twice: a drawing reading a neighbour as its deform's shape reads it without the deforms of the groups it is in itself, and a shape kept inside the group isn't bent by it. Moving the group itself moves what is in it on top of the deform. A Match Original reference goes by the groups around its original as well. Bones in a bone layer inside the group (or with the deform on it) show, pick and drag where they show, their data as it is. Ignore Group Deforms in a drawing's Layer tab leaves it out, and masks from outside the group stay as they are. Python: object.modifier_add on a group, layer.ignore_group_deforms. **Follow Deform** (Add Modifier > Deform, on a drawing) carries a drawing on a shape without bending it, with no bone: Follow Point as a modifier. It has Layer and Shape as the other deforms and takes any shape they take (a line, a closed shape, a grid, a mesh), with Method and Smooth saying how the shape is read. It keeps a point, starting at the drawing's middle; the whole drawing takes that point's change from frame 0 on top of what the stack before it gave, as much as Strength says. Limit Method, as Bevel's, says what moves: None, the whole drawing; Shape, one of its shapes (Limit Shape, from its list or with the eyedropper); Point Group, the points of a point group, each by its weight; Invert takes all but those. Whatever isn't taken in stays as the stack left it, and points something above has cut into a segment take their share from its two ends. The point starts at the middle of what it moves, unless it was put somewhere. A shape or point group that isn't there moves nothing, and the panel says why. Show Point shows the point, light blue: drag it on frame 0 to choose the place that's followed, on any other frame (or set Shift, with its key button) to key it, and it lands under the mouse through the bend. Second Point adds a second place, whose way from the first turns the drawing (Rotation, on by default) and whose distance from it scales the drawing evenly (Scale, off by default). Each point's row (Point, and Second for the second) says Own Place, or a point the shape already has, picked from its list or with the eyedropper, which shows the shape's points in the viewport even when its layer is hidden: the drawing then takes that point's change from frame 0 exactly, with nothing to drag or shift. Going back to Own Place leaves the point where the shape's point rests. A new Layer or Shape lets go of it, and a point deleted from the shape leaves the drawing where it is, the row saying Point gone. Symmetrize puts the copy's points across from the first, as its own places, without keys. Bones moving both the drawing and the shape count once. A group's deform reading the same shape leaves the drawing out, so it isn't bent as well. Precision, Resample Self, Weights, Change Type and Part don't apply, and groups can't have it. Saved with the file. Python: modifier_add(type="FOLLOW_DEFORM"), modifier_set(index, layer=, shape=, point=, point2=, show_point=, second=, rotation=, scale=, shift=, shift2=, strength=, limit= (NONE, SHAPE or GROUP), limit_shape= (a shape's name), limit_group=, limit_invert=, shape_point= and shape_point2= (a point's number on the shape's layer, None for its own place)), and point_to= or point2_to= (where it's to show now, in document space, as a drag does). Later: line width following the stretch; the Transform gizmo's Warp grid kept as a grid.

**3D**

| Feature | From | Tier |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Layer depth on Z and an animated 3D camera | Moho | v1 |
| Import OBJ and glTF meshes and animate their transforms | Blender | Later |

**Interface and workflow**

| Feature | From | Tier |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Blender-style layout and default theme | Blender | v1 |
| Operator search and a redo panel to adjust the last action | Blender | v1 |
| Editable keymaps | Blender | v1 |
| Undo and redo for every operation | Both | v1 |
| Python console, Python tools and add-ons | Blender | v1 |
| Theme editor | Blender | Later |
| Properties editor with Layer, Vector, Shape, Armature, Bone and Modifiers tabs (see below) | Blender | v1 |
| Built-in MCP server so AI assistants can inspect and edit the document (see below) | Open Animate | v1 |
| Top bar with File, Edit, Render, Window and Help menus (see below) | Blender | v1 |
| Menus that open at the mouse (right-click menus, X's Delete menu, Ctrl P, Alt P) remember the item last chosen and open with it under the mouse (the first item before any choice), as in Blender | Blender | v1 |
| Workspace tabs: Layout, Drawing, Rigging, Animation, Render, Scripting; text editor and info log for Scripting | Blender | v1 |
| Status bar along the bottom of the window with Blender's reports banner: the latest report, drawn as Blender's: a box in the report's colour (Info blue, Warning amber, Error red) holding its white icon, joined to a faint box of the same colour holding the message, in the middle of the bar ( a bold title and a hint when it has them, as "Reference layer points locked: Go to the Reference tab in Properties to unlock") flashes in, stays a few seconds (warnings and errors longer) and fades; hovering shows all of it, clicking lists the recent reports. Every refusal reports here (no warnings at the cursor); routine lines (undo steps, startup) only go to the Info editor | Blender | v1 |
| Statistics at the right of the status bar, as Blender's Scene Statistics and in its format ("Head_fr | Points:12/529 | Curves:1/40 | Segments:3/1,025 | Shapes:1/494 | Layers:1/47 | 0.1.0", selected/total, thousands separated): editing drawings, the active layer's name, Drawings (how many, when more than one: a group with Edit Descendants brings many) and the points, curves, segments and shapes of the drawings being edited (points above their curve's edit level are hidden and not counted; a segment is selected when both its points are, a curve or shape when all of its points are); in a bone layer, its name and its Bones (those in shown bone collections; selected by body or either joint), as "Rig | Bones:414/818 | Layers:1/47"; otherwise Layers and every drawing's Points. Then the version. Right-click the status bar to choose what shows: Statistics, Frame ("Frame:12/250", off at first) and Open Animate Version, kept in the preferences. The counts are worked out again only after a change (an undo step, the active layer, the frame, which layers are selected, shown or locked), and keep to the right of the reports banner. Python: oanim.context.statistics (mode, name, drawings, points, curves, segments, shapes, bones and layers as (selected, total), and the text) | Blender | v1 |
| ViewportActionPanel (Blender's Adjust Last Operation; the timeline has the same panel for its own actions): after an action with options is done in the viewport (Symmetrize; and every move: G, R and S on points or bones, and a Transform Bones drag or header change), a panel floats in its bottom-left corner, folded to the action's name ("› Symmetrize"); open (click its header, or F9), it lists the options as Blender does (labels on the left, values on the right: Direction, Threshold, Weights), and changing one undoes the action and does it again with it, as one undo step. Moves show their amounts (Move X and Y, Angle, Scale; the drag ends as mesh.translate, mesh.rotate, mesh.resize, armature.translate, armature.rotate, armature.resize or armature.transform). Alt G, Alt R and Alt S (Clear Location, Rotation and Scale, on bones, points or a Media layer, and Alt S's Scale Radius on points) show it too: **Amount** (how much of the change from the setup pose goes, 1 all of it), **X** and **Y** for Clear Location (and a Media layer's Clear Scale), and on bones Move Only Bones and Move Without Children, which share the moves' kept choices; Scale Radius ends as mesh.radius_scale (factor=). Bones moved while posing (not on frame 0) also show **Move Only Bones**: on, the drawings stay where they were: the points the bones bend (on the lowest detail level, with their handles) and layers following a bone get keyed on that frame to undo the bend, so only the bones move. Dynamic bones count as they swing after the move (the simulation worked out again for the new pose), and the move doesn't start a live swing, so hair on bones with Dynamics stays put too. They also show **Move Without Children** (G, R and S): on, only the picked bones move, their unpicked children staying where they are (a connected child keeps its direction and length, its base on its parent's tip); on the setup frame bones move without their children anyway, as in Blender's edit mode. Both choices are kept for the later bone moves (G, R, S, and the Transform Bones gizmo for Move Only Bones) until the frame or the action being edited changes, then they're off again. Moves on the setup frame (frame 0), of points (G, R, S, the Transform Points header) or bones, show **Keep Poses** instead: on, the move changes only the setup pose, and every key on another frame, in the mainline and every action, is worked out again so it shows the same pose (an action bone action's keys are offsets added on top as its bone turns, so they stay as they are and the frames it plays on show the same pose; only the action bone's own angle keys in its action are worked out again, so the same turn plays the same frame): a point's position key has the setup's move taken off, a handle's turn and length are measured again from the new setup handle, a bone's position (along its new rest axes) and angle are worked out again, and a bone lengthened on the setup frame has its keyed scale along it shrunk to keep its posed length. A channel with no keys has no pose of its own, so it follows the setup as always; the drawings a bone bends follow its new rest, as in Blender. Off (the default), the poses move with the setup, as keys hold changes from it. The choice is kept for the next setup moves until the frame or the action being edited changes, then it's off again (the Transform Points tool's drag uses it too). A bone's children keep their poses too: their keys are measured from it, and it keeps its posed pose (a child channel with no keys still follows its setup). Python: oanim.wm.move_options() says how Keep Poses, Move Only Bones and Move Without Children stand for the next move. Python: keep_poses=1 on mesh.translate / rotate / resize / transform_selected and armature.translate / rotate / resize / transform. It stays open or folded as last left, and goes away once anything else changes the document. Drawn in Blender's colours (#181818 outlined #242424; fields #282828 outlined #3D3D3D, with chevrons). Python: oanim.wm.last_operation(), oanim.wm.redo_last(**options) | Blender | v1 |
| Local View (Blender's): **/** (or Numpad /) shows only the picked layers, with what's inside them, in the viewport, leaving the view where it is; **/** again brings back every layer. Masks still mask, nothing's visibility changes, clicks only pick the layers shown, the info line says "Camera (Local)", and renders show everything. A view: not saved and not undone. Python: oanim.ops.view.local_view(), oanim.wm.local_view(). Media View: **`** shows every media layer at full opacity, guides too, with the viewport's overlays hidden (a label says how to leave it), to see the footage clearly; **`** again goes back. A view only: no layer's opacity or the overlay settings change, nothing is keyed, saved or undone. Python: oanim.ops.view.media_view(), oanim.wm.media_view() | Blender | v1 |
| How the project is shown is saved with it, as a .blend keeps its views: where the viewport looks (its zoom and pan; a file without one frames the camera), the Viewport Overlays (every switch, Handles, Point Size, Handle Size and Bones) and which panels and folding rows are open or folded (Properties panels and their sections, the Viewport Overlays sections, the timeline's rows, the ViewportActionPanel). Undo leaves them as they are, as Blender's does; changing one marks the file unsaved; a new file starts from the defaults. Every viewport shows the project's overlays. Python: oanim.wm.overlays(), oanim.wm.set_overlay(), oanim.wm.ui_open(), oanim.wm.set_ui_open() | Blender | v1 |
| The selection is part of the project: saved with it (selected layers, the active layer and bone, selected bones and their ends, points, handles picked on their own, keys picked in the timeline) and in the undo history, as in Blender: every change of selection is an undo step ("Select", or the selecting action's own name); one made without a step of its own (clicking layers in the Layers editor, a script) gets one once the mouse is up, so a drag makes one step | Blender | v1 |
| Autosave and crash recovery; starting without a file reopens the file that was open at quitting (as last saved; File > Recover > Last Session has any unsaved changes, and when there are none it is a copy of the saved file, so quitting doesn't write a large document a second time). Saving writes compact JSON (no indentation; settings at their defaults left out, the reader filling them in), each drawing's points and curves built on all the cores at once, and compresses it at miniz's fastest level: a 3,155-layer, 113,702-point file saves in about 1.2 s rather than 3.2. A crash writes a report to %LOCALAPPDATA%\OpenAnimate\crashes: a minidump (crash_<time>.dmp, for Visual Studio or WinDbg with the build's .pdb) and a readable crash_<time>.txt (the reason, the bad address, the call stack with function names and source lines); the next start says where it is in the Info editor. oanim.wm.debug_crash() crashes on purpose to try it | Blender | v1 |
| Command-line mode: OpenAnimate.exe --background --python script.py renders or batch-edits without a window | Blender | v1 |
| Asset browser: drag characters, props and styles in from other .oanim files | Blender | Later |
| Interface scale setting for 150–200% Windows display scaling | Blender | v1 |
| Save Incremental: scene\_002.oanim, scene\_003.oanim and so on with one shortcut | Blender | v1 |
| Auto-generated Python API docs, built from the property registry | Blender | v1 |

**Top bar.** Like Blender, the top of the window holds the main menus on the left and the workspace tabs beside them. The tabs start as Layout, Animation and Scripting, opening on Layout. Animation has the viewport with the Path Timing graph above the Asset Shelf beside it, Layers above Properties down the right and the timeline along the bottom; going to it picks the Motion Path tool, and going to Layout picks the Select Box tool.

| Menu | Contents |
| --- | --- |
| File | New, Open, Open Recent, Save (Ctrl+S), Save As, Save Copy; Import (SVG, images, OBJ and glTF meshes, layers from another .oanim file by reference); Export (SVG and PDF done; PNG sequence, video, GIF); Save Startup File; Quit |
| Edit | Undo, Redo, Undo History, Repeat Last, Adjust Last Operation, Operator Search (F3), Preferences |
| Render | Render Frame (Ctrl+R or F12, shown in a Render Result window with Save As PNG), Render Animation (Ctrl+F12), View Render (F11), Output Settings |
| Window | New Window, Toggle Fullscreen, Next and Previous Workspace, Toggle Python Console |
| Help | Manual, Python API Reference, Keyboard Shortcuts, Open Log Folder, About Open Animate |

**Workspaces.** A workspace is a saved screen layout for one kind of work, chosen from tabs in the top bar; + adds one, and each is saved in the .oanim file. You asked for Layout, Render and Scripting; I added Drawing, Rigging and Animation because each needs a different set of editors.

| Workspace | Layout |
| --- | --- |
| Layout (default) | Viewport, Layers panel, Properties editor, timeline along the bottom |
| Drawing (added) | Large viewport in Camera view, tool settings and style swatches, a slim timeline |
| Rigging (added) | Viewport and local armature viewport side by side, Layers panel, Properties editor on the Bone tab, timeline |
| Animation (added) | Viewport, dope sheet and graph editor, Properties editor, a second viewport in Camera view |
| Render | Rendered image viewer, output and render settings, timeline |
| Scripting | Viewport, Python console, text editor for scripts, an info log of every operator run (for copying into scripts), Properties editor |

**Properties editor.** Like Blender's Properties editor: a column of tab icons down the side, each tab showing the active layer's settings as collapsible panels. Each panel is a rounded box, as Blender's: header and body one shade (#3D3D3D) on the editor's background, with a one-pixel lighter outline and a grip at the header's top right; dragging the grip onto another panel moves it above or below that one (a blue line shows where), and each tab keeps its order, saved with the project as the folds are. Sub-panels inside a panel (Advanced, a reference's change groups) are folding rows with the same padding as fields; open, their rows sit on a darker band the panel's width. Every animatable field has a key button beside it, as in Blender: a dot when not animated, a hollow diamond when animated, a filled one when keyed on this frame; clicking it keys the property here, or takes the key away. Once a property is animated, a pin button beside it pins it to the timeline (red when pinned, and its row tinted red). The field is tinted green when animated and yellow when keyed on this frame, and pink while an action bone's action is moving it (it shows the live value, the action's part in; an action keys every bone, so pink is only where that action moves it from its rest; Python: layer.field_state(path)). So far: the layer's Visible, Location, Rotation, Scale and Opacity, and the bone's Angle and Scale. Visibility is an animated channel whose keys can only be constant (yes/no properties only hold) that auto-key leaves alone until it is keyed by hand. As in Blender, a property whose last real key is deleted isn't animated any more: its frame-0 key goes too, and it keeps frame 0's value.

| Tab | What it shows | What you can do there |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Layer | Name, type, visibility and lock. Transform: position, rotation, scale and Shear (X and Y, degrees: how far the layer's X axis and Y axis tilt from square, as Moho's layer shear, Flash's skew and Spine's bone shear; applied between rotation and scale, Translate * Rotate * Shear * Scale; keyed like the rest, saved with the file; Python: layer.shear_x, layer.shear_y, keyframes path "shear"), flip, origin. Opacity, blend mode, masks, parent bone, timing offset | Type exact values; set or clear keys per field; reset the transform; jump to the parent bone |
| Vector (vector layers) | The drawing's own data, as Blender's Data tab: its point, curve and shape counts; Point Groups; **Selected Points** (the selected points' Mean Position, Mean Radius, handles, their Handle In and Handle Out) and **Selected Edges** (whether their outline shows) | Make and fill point groups; type exact values for the selection |
| Shape (vector layers) | Every shape of the picked layers being edited (multi-layer editing), each layer's top to bottom in stacking order, under the layer's name when there are several (clicking the name makes it the active layer), with its fill and outline swatches and the shared-material mark; clicking a shape selects its points (Shift adds), dragging one reorders it within its layer, and the panels below show the active shape: the one last clicked, in the viewport (a point, handle or line of it) or in the list, while it's selected, otherwise the topmost selected shape. Its row is marked (lighter, with a white bar on its left) and the Shape panel's header names it and its layer ("Shape: S7  in Ear_L"). Below the list: its Shape panel, then its Material (the look) and Detail panels | Select a shape from the list or the canvas and they stay in sync; rename, hide, reorder by dragging, delete; change fill and outline; assign a shared style |
| Modifiers | The layer's modifier stack, top to bottom, each as a collapsible panel with its own settings | Add from a menu; reorder by dragging; show or hide in the viewport and in renders; Show Points (a third button after those, on the modifiers that add points: Mirror, Bevel, Resample, Boolean and the deforms, whose Precision or Resample Self adds them; the others keep its place empty so the buttons line up) marks the points that modifier adds as hollow teal dots on the layer being edited, where they end up after the whole stack, never picked or snapped to (and they show, Show Points or not, while its Pieces or Spacing is being dragged); off by default, greyed while the modifier is hidden in the viewport, saved with the file (Python: modifier_set(index, show_points=)); Show Mesh (a fourth button, after Show Points, on Curve, Cage and Mesh Deform only, which give their name field one button's room for it) draws an even grid over what the deform reaches (its shape and the drawing at rest, 15% wider each way; square cells, 16 across the longer side), each line bent as the deform bends that place now by its Strength, without weights or the rest of the stack, so where it stretches, squeezes or tears shows between the lines, as Toon Boom Harmony's Show Deformation Mesh is used for (Harmony's is the triangles it bends the drawing's art by; Open Animate bends the points themselves, so a grid is what shows its bend): orange, over every shown layer's drawing, grey and even on Setup (frame 0) where nothing bends; off by default, greyed while the modifier is hidden in the viewport, saved with the file, hidden with the overlays (Python: modifier_set(index, show_mesh=), the grid as layer.deform_grid_view(index)); remove with the x; the header's menu (Blender's: a down arrow before the x) has Apply (Ctrl A), Duplicate (Shift D), Move to First and Move to Last, and Ctrl A and Shift D work with the mouse over a modifier's box, as in Blender. Apply makes it part of the drawing, on Setup (frame 0) only: a Mirror's reflection becomes points of its own, a Boolean's outline replaces the shapes it joined. The Armature modifier comes with the bone layer, so Apply and Duplicate are greyed out for it and Ctrl A does nothing but say so. Duplicate puts the copy below, named Blender's way ("Mirror.001"). Python: oanim.ops.object.modifier_apply(index=), object.modifier_duplicate(index=) |
| Armature (bone layers) | The skeleton as a whole, as Blender's Armature data tab: Bone Collections, every bone as a tree, and Fix Actions | Select a bone from the tree; make and assign collections |
| Bone (bone layers) | The active bone only, as Blender's Bone tab: its Bone panel: name, IK settings and strength. On the setup frame (0), as Blender's edit mode: its Head, Tail and Length. On other frames, as Blender's pose mode: its Transform, Angle and Scale, each with its key button; Transform and Angle are the change from the setup pose, (0, 0) and 0° at rest, the Transform measured along the bone's rest axes (X along it as it rests, Y across it, as Blender's pose location) (their keys hold these changes, as Blender's pose bones do, unlike Moho's: moving a bone on frame 0 moves every pose and action with it; the timeline names the row Transform too; older files and Moho imports open converted). Then Relations (parent, connection, inheritance, collections) and Viewport Display (its shape and colours). With no active bone the tab stays, saying "No active bone: click a bone to edit it" | Type exact ends on frame 0 (a connected bone's head moves its parent's tail too) or exact pose values elsewhere; set or clear keys per field |

The tabs come in one order, as Blender's (Object, Modifiers, Data, Bone, Bone Constraints, Material): Document, Layer, Masking, Modifiers, the layer's data tab (Vector, Armature, Media or Text), then a drawing's Shape or a bone layer's Bone and Bone Constraints, then a reference's Reference; a tab the active layer doesn't have is left out and the others keep their order. The tab picked is kept: with a layer that hasn't got it, Layer shows, and the picked tab comes back with a layer that has it (as Blender's Properties editor does). Python: oanim.wm.properties_tab("VECTOR", "SHAPE", "ARMATURE", "BONE", ...). While the camera is picked, Properties shows just Document and Camera: the camera has no layer, masking, modifier or drawing settings. Panels are written in Python, so add-ons can add their own panels to any tab.

**Built-in MCP server.** Open Animate can be driven by AI assistants such as Claude through the Model Context Protocol, so they can look at and work in the running app. Moho has no such thing, which is why MohoMCP has to poll for requests inside a tool's draw call; here it is part of the app. The first version is deliberately thin while the API is still changing.

- **Built on what already exists.** Everything goes through the `oanim` Python API and its operators, so anything a person can do, the server can do, with the same undo steps.
- **Tools now:** run Python with the `oanim` module (as a console: names stay between calls, the last line's value, printed output and tracebacks come back); a screenshot of the window or one editor; the app's state (file, frame, playback, layers, selection, a running transform, recent Info reports); and pressing keys as if over an editor (typed numbers go into a running transform). Later, once the API settles: typed tools to read the document, layer tree, bones, actions and keyframes, get and set properties by path, run operators by name, set and delete keys, switch frames and actions, render a frame, and run several operations as one undo step.
- **Matching drawings to a reference.** `layer.shown(level=None)` gives where a layer's points and handle ends show (bones, parents and the layer's transform included) in document space, where a reference over the drawing is: as drawn (detail's handles final), or as detail level k's curve has them (its points only, that level's handles), with each point's place in the whole curve; `layer.set_detail_level(level, shapes=None)` sets shapes' edit level and `layer.detail_sync()` places the detail now, as the viewport does each frame (so edits at a level carry the finer detail along); `layer.place_point(i, (x, y))` and `layer.place_handle(curve, i, "IN" or "OUT", (x, y), keep_other=False)` put them there, the bones' bend undone, as a G drag would (keyed at the frame when the next undo step records it). `oanim.wm.render_png(path, width, height, guides=False, transparent=False)` renders the current frame to a PNG on the next frame drawn: guides=True includes guide layers (reference media) on a clear background and waits for the media's frame to be decoded, so a reference and the drawing render pixel-aligned (a document point lands at pixel (w/2 + s fx, h/2 − s fy), s = h/2, with (fx, fy) = camera.to_frame(point)). The project's match-vectors-to-reference skill builds on these.
- **Resources (later):** the `oanim` Python API reference and the operator list, so an assistant can discover what is available.
- **Transport:** JSON lines on 127.0.0.1 only (`src/wm/remote.cc`), run on the app's main loop between frames (screenshots from the frame just drawn), so no polling; a stdio bridge in plain Python using only the standard library (`tools/mcp/openanimate_mcp.py`) speaks MCP, registered for this project by its `.mcp.json`.
- **Safety:** off by default and switched on in Preferences (Remote Control); every request needs a token made afresh each time it starts (written with the port, the process id and the open file to remote/<process id>.json in the user data folder, one per running Open Animate, and to remote.json by the one holding Preferences' port, where the bridge reads them; removed when it stops, and left-over files of instances that crashed cleared). Several windows can each be reached: a second Open Animate takes a free port, and the bridge's list_projects names every open project while each tool takes `project` (a file name, path or process id), the main one otherwise; a connection that doesn't open with a request, as a browser's doesn't, is closed unread. Later: a separate switch for running Python and a status-bar indicator while an assistant is connected.
- **How it is built:** the listener in C++, the bridge in plain Python outside the app (no MCP SDK needed: MCP's stdio protocol is small), tested end to end headless (`tests/test_remote_control.py`).

**Files and output**

| Feature | From | Tier |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Save and open the native file format | Both | v1 |
| Export PNG image sequences | Both | v1 |
| Export video through FFmpeg | Both | Later |
| Import SVG artwork | Moho | Later |
| Open Flash and Animate .fla files (the binary format of Flash 5 to CS6, and the XFL that CS5 on and Animate save: a zip of XML, read even when its zip directory is damaged, or an unzipped project opened by its .xfl): File > Open and dropping one on the window convert it into a new, untitled document named after it (saving asks where; the .fla is never written). Stage size, frame rate and background come across; each Flash layer becomes a layer (stored bottom first; folders become groups, guide layers guides (shown, as on Flash's stage, inside symbols too, but left out of renders; a guide hidden with Flash's eye stays hidden), hidden layers shown (Flash publishes them), mask layers hidden masks of the layers they mask); drawings become vector layers whose fills are rebuilt from Flash's edges (each fill's loops linked, its holes real holes: one shape filling Even-Odd) and whose lines keep their width and colour; symbol instances become groups named after the library symbol, placed on the instance's transformation point (Flash's pivot, so rotating or scaling the group turns about the same point it did in Flash, and classic tweens follow the pivot's path), with the instance's transform exactly (its skew as the group's Shear, keyed through classic tweens as Flash eases it; a skewed bitmap keeps its skew too) and alpha as opacity; graphic symbols show the frame they would (First Frame, and Loop or Play Once counted from their keyframe, in step with their parent); bitmaps (JPEG, PNG, lossless) become Media layers packed into the file. On the main timeline keyframes switch by visibility keys and classic motion tweens become transform keys. What has no equivalent is listed in the Info editor: gradients come in as solid fills of their average colour, bitmap fills grey, tints and brightness are left out, shape tweens hold their first shape, text is left out in the binary format (in Animate's XFL, text fields become text layers: their runs' fonts, found by their PostScript names, sizes and colours, alignment and spacing, a fixed-width box wrapping as a text box; a font not installed is named), animated symbols on the main timeline show their frame at each keyframe only, and only the first scene comes in. The format notes are fla-decoder's (MIT) and fla-viewer's (ISC), with CS3/CS4's additions worked out here. Animate's newer motion tweens (motion objects) show their keyframes only. Python: oanim.io_fla.convert(fla, oanim), which hands XFL to oanim.io_xfl | Flash | v1 |
| Export GIF and glTF | Both | Maybe |
| Render settings: resolution, frame range, transparent background; viewport and render quality (stroke resolution: pieces drawn per curve segment) and viewport and render samples (multisampled anti-aliasing, 1 to 16 per pixel; default 4 and 16), set per document. Masks are per-sample stencil masks, so masked edges leave no seams | Both | v1 |

## Architecture sketch

The app is a small Blender: a C++20 core that holds the data and does the math, an operator and keymap layer that all editing goes through, and embedded Python for tools, panels and add-ons. We copy Blender's ideas, not its heavy infrastructure (depsgraph, multi-backend GPU module, raw-memory .blend files).

**Modules**

| Module | Responsibility | Blender equivalent |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `core/` | Math, containers, IDs, logging | `blenlib` |
| `data/` | Document, layer tree, vector drawings, skeletons, actions. Saved data kept apart from runtime caches | `makesdna`, `blenkernel` |
| `rna/` | Property reflection: name, type, range, getter/setter, update callback. Drives UI, Python and animation paths | `makesrna` |
| `anim/` | Animated channels, keyframes, interpolation, actions, action bones, drivers | `animrig`, `fcurve.cc`, `constraint.cc` |
| `eval/` | Fixed evaluation pipeline that builds an evaluated copy of the scene each frame | `depsgraph`, simplified |
| `modifiers/` | Modifier type table: mirror, noise, smooth, offset, armature deform and more | `modifiers/` |
| `ops/` | Operator registry, macros, undo flag, redo panel | `windowmanager` operators |
| `wm/` | Windows, event loop, keymaps, modal handlers, split-area screen layout | `windowmanager`, `editors/screen` |
| `editors/` | One folder per editor: viewport, timeline and dope sheet, graph, layers, properties, outliner | `editors/space_*` |
| `gpu/` | OpenGL renderer, curve tessellation, overlays for bones, handles and onion skins | `gpu`, `draw` |
| `io/` | Native file format, image and video export, SVG import, mesh import | `blenloader`, `io` |
| `python/` | Embedded CPython and the `app` module | `python/` (bpy) |
| `scripts/` | Python startup scripts: UI panels, default keymap, theme, built-in Python tools | `scripts/` |
| `mcp/` | Built-in MCP server in Python on the official SDK; maps MCP tools to operators and properties; localhost HTTP plus a stdio bridge | None |

**Evaluation order, every frame**

1. Read each animated channel at the current frame from the mainline.
2. Apply action bone actions: each driving bone's angle picks a frame in its action, and that action's values are layered on top. Then fix actions: each blends its poses' corrections by how close the pose is to each, faded toward rest by its action bone actions' shares, layered on top too.
3. Solve the bone pose: forward kinematics, IK, constraints.
4. Deform bound points and layers by their bones.
5. Run each layer's modifier stack.
6. Composite layers with masks and blend modes, then draw overlays.

**Key technical choices**

- **Window, input and tablet:** SDL3. **Graphics:** OpenGL 4.5 first; Vulkan only if needed later.
- **UI:** Dear ImGui with docking wherever it can do the job, themed with Blender's default colours, font and spacing. Custom widgets built on ImGui cover what it lacks or draws differently: Blender-style number fields (a click without dragging starts typing, a drag changes the value, Shift while dragging is precise), pie menus, tabs and icons. Panels are written in Python with a `layout.prop(...)` style API so the UI toolkit can be swapped later.
- **Python:** embedded CPython 3.12+ through pybind11, exposed as a `oanim` module shaped like `bpy`.
- **Operators:** each has `poll`, `invoke`, `modal`, `execute` and an UNDO flag, as in Blender. Extrude (E) is a macro: the data operator followed by modal move (G).
- **Keymaps:** data files of (operator, key event, properties) per editor and mode, modelled on Blender's `blender_default.py`.
- **Undo:** each undo step holds the document's settings and its layers as shared, read-only copies (each without its children, the tree's shape beside them). A push compares every layer field by field with the step before (a defaulted `operator==` on everything a layer holds, so no field can be left out) and shares those that are the same, so a step costs only what changed. Undo and redo rebuild the document from the step. Steps let go of are freed on another thread.
- **Drawing big documents:** the viewport keeps what it made of each layer while the layer stays the same. Each layer has a revision (not saved, not compared) that changes when it may have: undo steps give new ones to the layers that changed (and note when the tree changed), a point move to the layers it moves each update, and any frame that may have changed things nobody said (the frame moved or plays, a script ran, a field is dragged, the mouse is down for anything but a point move, an undo or a file opened) to every layer. The scene renderer keeps each plain drawing's geometry hash and its tessellation (a drawing moved whole draws its triangles moved), and batches its plain colour steps: they wait until anything else draws, then go out in runs of up to 32 under one stencil test from a buffer kept per run, each vertex knowing its step's place and opacity, so a rig's hundreds of drawings take a few dozen draws. The edit overlays (wires, handles, point squares) are drawn on the GPU from buffers kept per layer in layer space (instanced lines widened in the shader, instanced squares and dots), made again only when the shown drawing changes. OANIM_CHECK_REVISIONS=1 compares every layer with its last frame and reports one that changed without a new revision.
- **File format:** a .oanim file: a zip holding a JSON scene description plus binary blobs for point data, with a schema version and migration functions. A big file's drawings are parsed and built on every core: one quick scan finds each drawing's text (a big drawing's points, curves and shapes in pieces), and the rest is parsed with them left out.
- **Build:** CMake with vcpkg for dependencies, on your installed MSVC 14.51 toolchain. Windows only for now; SDL3 and OpenGL keep a later macOS or Linux port possible.

**What a Python operator looks like**

```python
import oanim

class BONE_OT_action_bone_create(oanim.types.Operator):
    bl_idname = "bone.action_bone_create"
    bl_label = "Create Action Bone"
    bl_options = {'REGISTER', 'UNDO'}

    @classmethod
    def poll(cls, ctx):
        return ctx.active_bone is not None and ctx.action is None

    def execute(self, ctx):
        bone = ctx.active_bone
        action = ctx.active_layer.actions.new(name=bone.name)
        action.action_bone = bone
        ctx.document.edit_action(action)
        return {'FINISHED'}

oanim.utils.register_class(BONE_OT_action_bone_create)
```

## Roadmap

Version 1 arrives in five milestones, and each one ends with something you can actually use. No dates yet: the pace depends on how many hours a week go into it.

1. **Foundation:** CMake build, SDL3 window, OpenGL, ImGui docking, embedded Python; operator registry, keymaps, undo, split areas, reflection registry; command-line mode for scripts and tests; interface scale setting.
   *Gate: a Python operator runs from a key press and undoes cleanly.*
2. **Drawing:** vector layers (points, curves, shapes, styles, brushes) and the curve renderer; pen, freehand, select, G R S E, curvature, fill; save and open; Layers editor, group and reference layers, masks; snapping, autosave, crash recovery, Save Incremental, pen tablet support.
   *Gate: draw, fill and save a character that matches the Moho look.*
3. **Animation:** animated channels, interpolation modes, timeline and dope sheet; switch and image layers, onion skins, PNG export; frame-by-frame drawing, auto-keying, playback controls, render settings.
   *Gate: animate a walk cycle by hand and export it as PNG frames.*
4. **Rigging:** bones, point and layer binding, IK, custom bone shapes, bone colours; actions, action bones, local armature viewport, symmetrize with _L and _R; Setup toggle for frame 0; copy, paste and paste-flipped poses.
   *Gate: an action bone head turn works on a rig with IK arms.*
5. **Version 1:** modifier stack and mirror modifier, free 3D view, layer depth; operator search, redo panel, keymap editing, Python console, MCP server; top bar menus, workspaces, Properties editor tabs, auto-generated API docs.
   *Gate: rig and animate a short scene end to end, no Moho needed.*

**After version 1:** graph editor, bone dynamics and physics, bone constraints; weight painting, motion paths, pose library, asset browser, video export, mesh import.
