Each gate is a hands-on test; a milestone is done only when its gate passes. Rendering quality is proven in milestone 2 and action bones in milestone 4, the two riskiest pieces.

## Risks, open questions and references

The biggest risks are vector rendering quality and action bones' interaction with IK; both get a prototype early, in milestone 2 and milestone 4.

**Risks**

| Risk | Why it matters | Mitigation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Vector fills and strokes look worse than Moho's | Curves must tessellate into smooth, anti-aliased triangles every frame | Prototype the curve renderer first; compare against Moho screenshots; use multisampling. Done: a drawing's triangles and stamps are kept and drawn again while nothing that makes them changes; the zoom goes into that in steps of 2^(1/8), so zooming back and forth reuses them, and once a viewport frame has spent 6 ms making new ones, a drawing seen at a new zoom is drawn from the nearest zoom it was made for and made over the next frames, so zooming a big rig (Clay10) stays at 60 fps. A Boolean's merged outlines are kept by everything that goes in, and put into the drawing as it is now (its looks and ids). The timeline reads keys without working out every point's and bone's pose. Layers drawn under the same masks go to the GPU together (up to 32 a draw; the masks changing is what sends what waits), each shape's triangles are kept by the shape, its curves and their points, so a drawing with one point dragged makes only the shapes through it again, and before each frame every drawing still to be made is made at once over the CPU's cores (brushed lines and lines ended at masks' edges, and a zoom's, are made as they are drawn). Rainbow Dash (3,156 layers): 50 ms a frame to 13. The viewport shows its last render again while nothing it shows has changed (no new revision, undo step, frame, view, setting, video frame or live physics, and that render was complete), so an idle frame or a click in a panel costs a few milliseconds (Rainbow Dash: 24 to 5). An undo step compares the layers with the last step's, and recording keys goes through them, over the CPU's cores (Rainbow Dash's release of a drag: 65 to about 30 ms); the selection isn't hashed mid-drag, and detail points are synced only on layers with a new revision. Layer runs: each viewport render takes the layers drawn (bottom first) with a signature of all each draws from (its drawing as drawn, where, how opaque, its masks and the layers they take, theirs in turn); those changed since the last render are drawn as they are and each stretch of others between them comes from a multisample image kept of it, laid over the rest sample for sample (identical to drawing everything); up to 4 drawn live, more (playback) draws everything. Runs are made only once the same layers change on two renders running (as Flutter caches a picture once it has held), so a one-off change pays for no images. Clean steps are pushed, not worked out (as Blender's depsgraph tags and Chromium's invalidation flags): while only a few layers have a new revision, a plain drawing in a kept run (no masks on it or its groups, no modifiers but the bones', not a reference) none of whose groups or own self has a new one keeps last render's signature and isn't prepared; every 30th render, and whenever many layers changed, everything is worked out again. Rainbow Dash, dragging a skirt point: 21 ms a render drawing everything, 6.5 with runs (1,132 steps, about 1,000 clean). A pan draws a margin an eighth of the view's longer side all round and shows the same image moved by whole pixels while it keeps within it (middle-dragging the view changes nothing in the document); while the zoom keeps changing the last image is shown scaled, drawn sharp once it rests 0.15 s (the new zoom's drawings made then over the cores, up to 8 ms of them a frame, the rest standing in as made for the zoom before until made, so a big drawing sharpens over a few frames; with Zoom Preview off each step of a zoom is drawn with every drawing as made for the nearest zoom, nothing made, until it rests). Under it, backdrops fill what it no longer covers when zooming out (as browsers' and maps' lower-resolution levels): an overview four times as wide and tall as the view at an eighth of its resolution, there before any zoom shows (a little out of date after an edit, it stands in), and a level twice as wide and tall at half resolution, shown while up to date; both at scales rounded to powers of two, drawn on frames with nothing else to do once nothing has changed for 0.3 s, one a frame (Rainbow Dash: 10 to 26 ms after an edit, about 110 ms after a zoom of twice or more). Rainbow Dash, dragging a skirt point: 21 to 9 ms a render. Both are options in the viewport header's Viewport Shading dropdown (beside the overlays', as Blender's), under Performance: Layer Caching (layer runs and the pan margin; off, every change draws every layer; on by default) and Zoom Preview (the scaled zoom and its backdrops; off, each step of a zoom is drawn sharp; off by default, so a zoom never shows a blurry stand-in), saved with the project; Python: oanim.wm.shading(), set_shading(name, on). The renderer is being rebuilt in stages (CPU triangulation giving way to stencil-then-cover fills, masks in depth, curves, bones and strokes on the GPU, all under MSAA); each is checked by tools/render_check/render_check.py, which renders the same cases through two builds or settings in private copies and compares them: seams (abutting shapes against the same outline whole), masks, temporal flicker, and real scenes, against a 4x supersampled truth on request. The app asks for OpenGL 4.5 (3.3 if the driver hasn't it). Stage 1, done: the scene renderer draws through a render-command layer (src/gpu/gpu_device.hh) and calls no OpenGL itself; every draw carries its whole state (shader, blending, colour writes, the stencil test and what it writes), resources are handles (meshes, textures, targets), and the OpenGL backend (gpu_device_gl.cc, which holds the shaders) sends only what differs from the last draw, a WebGPU backend able to go behind the same calls later. Renders are the same pixel for pixel (render_check against stage 0: every case, Rainbow Dash and Clay013, viewport included). Stage 2, done: the Viewport Shading dropdown's Renderer, Classic (as before) or GPU Geometry, saved with the project and used by its renders too (Python: set_shading("gpu_geometry", on)). GPU Geometry draws a colour fill of 1,024 outline points or more by stencil-then-cover instead of cutting it into triangles: a triangle from the middle of its bounds for each edge counts its winding into the stencil (one way round adding one, the other taking one), then a quad over its bounds draws it where the count isn't 0 (Even-Odd: where it is odd), each sample once, and clears the count. Smaller fills stay in the batches (two passes cost them more than cutting), and masks' shapes are always cut. The masks' stencil bits are held the other way round on the GPU (their levels in its top four bits), so the bottom four, their scratch, count winding while colour is drawn, the count cleared under each fill's bounds after masks were worked out. A fill comes out where its outline winds, as the app's own inside test says: a figure-8, which Classic's triangulation loses a lobe of, fills whole. render_check (with a new fills suite: an 800-point star, rings with holes, a twist and a curl, masked and see-through fills): Classic is unchanged from stage 1 pixel for pixel; GPU Geometry differs by a sample or two along edges (Rainbow Dash: 22 pixels), with no seams and the same masks and flicker, also with every fill drawn so (OANIM_FAN_MIN_POINTS=0). Clay013, a render at a new zoom (every drawing made again): 85 ms to 60; drawn again as kept, 31 to 32. Rainbow Dash: 308 to 262 ms made again, 34 to 42 drawn again (its 250 big fills each draw three times and break the batches, until stage 5 batches them); in the viewport, with Layer Caching, the same either way. Stage 3, done: under GPU Geometry the masks go into the depth buffer. Each masked layer's cards that cut its fills and lines alike (most) are one level, worked out in a stencil bit as Classic works one out, then kept where the levels before it hold and written there as that level's depth; a draw under K levels passes where the depth is K or more. Levels the last layer drawn shares (its groups') stay: only the new ones are worked out and pushed, and fewer than the depth holds need nothing (deeper samples still hold all of them). Cards that cut only lines or only fills, and End Lines at Edge, stay in the stencil, up to three. So masks nest as deep as they go (Classic keeps four levels), and while colour is drawn the stencil is free but for those cards. render_check (new mask cases: six groups deep, masked layers side by side in a masked group, a lines-only card with an all card, Clip to Below onto a masked base, grow, shrink and Inside the Stroke): GPU Geometry is the same as stage 2's pixel for pixel, Rainbow Dash and Clay013 included, but six groups deep, where all six now mask; Classic unchanged. As fast as stage 2 (Rainbow Dash: 45 levels worked out and 89 depth passes a render). See-through layers, done (GPU Geometry): a drawing layer less than fully opaque is faded as a whole, as a group is, so its lines don't show its fills through them and its shapes don't show each other: drawn at full strength into a multisample image of its own, then laid over sample for sample, faded, under its masks, over only what it covers (Classic fades each shape on its own; a layer whose lines and fills are masked apart still does). Each sample is faded, not each pixel averaged first, so its edges stay as exact as any; the same shapes in a faded group differ only along edges, by a sample or so. render_check: the same as stage 3 but the new fill_see_through case. About 0.1 ms a layer (40 see-through layers at 1920x1080, 16 samples: 22.4 ms against 18.4 opaque). Stage 4, started: under GPU Geometry a shape filling several loops together (Even-Odd or Nonzero, as Flash imports have) goes straight from its loops to stencil-then-cover, the stencil's count working out its fill rule, not cut into pieces on the CPU first (fill_area) and those triangulated; the same but a sample along edges (Rainbow Dash: 20 pixels), Classic unchanged. Measured, summed over the cores, a render at a new zoom (GPU Geometry): Rainbow Dash makes its drawings in about 870 ms, of which cutting fills of under 1,024 points into triangles 255, joining a drawing's steps (copying their vertices) 136, flattening outlines 89, building fans 80 and strokes 58 (its holed fills: 452 to 360 with this); Clay013 in 70, strokes 32. So flattening on the GPU would take off a fifth at most, and only once strokes needn't be made again on a zoom or a bend either; GPU flattening and bones are better done with stage 5's strokes. Then two of those costs, in both renderers: a drawing's batch goes on across its shapes, each shape keeping its own part of it for next time (from where the batch stood when it began), instead of each shape ending its steps and the steps being copied together again after; and a stencil-then-cover fill is a triangle fan a ring (its middle, then the ring), all drawn in one call, one vertex an outline point instead of three. Pixel for pixel the same in both (render_check, viewport included; a shape edited among kept ones the same as drawn fresh). Making Rainbow Dash's drawings at a new zoom: about 870 ms to 735 under GPU Geometry, 960 to 910 Classic; Clay013 70 to 64. GPU Geometry is the default renderer now: it draws the Clay files (clay015, clay14, Clay011, Clay012, Clay10, mouth rig3) pixel for pixel as Classic does and plays them as fast (viewport renders: Clay013 8.9 ms a frame, Rainbow Dash 14.5, either way), and differs only where Classic is wrong or limited (an outline crossing itself, masks more than four deep, see-through layers). Saved as the Viewport Shading's "renderer" (CLASSIC or GPU_GEOMETRY); a file without it opens in GPU Geometry (its "gpu_geometry": false was only the default then). Stage 5 (strokes, flattening and bones on the GPU) waits for a scene heavier than these to need it. Abutting shapes no longer leave a sample showing through between them where their outlines run opposite ways along one edge (render_check's strips, one sample of 16 at 14 of 255, in both renderers): an outline's samples along a segment are worked out from its nearer end (bezier_sample), so the same edge sampled either way round gives the very same points (and is cut into as many: Wang's formula's second differences are summed in an order that doesn't depend on the direction); everything else changes by a sample along an edge at most (Classic's triangulation of a figure-8 now loses its other lobe). Stage 5, planned (reports/Stage 5 GPU strokes and batching.md, from research into Skia, Vello, Rive, Blender's Grease Pencil and the 2D animation runtimes): 5.0 GPU timer queries per pass and two benchmarks (a bending rig where every point moves each frame, and zoom animated during playback); 5.1 the device layer gains storage buffers, instancing, indirect draws and compute, steps carried as records instead of the slot array; 5.2 stencil-then-cover fills batched in groups that share no samples (one stencil and one cover draw a group), then the fill threshold swept again. GPU strokes (expanded from the CPU's samples, as Grease Pencil does, keeping Classic's overlaps), compute flattening and GPU bones follow only once the benchmarks show making a drawing costing half a frame, strokes and flattening most of it: none of the 2D runtimes surveyed skins vector paths on the GPU, and playback here already fits. Stage 5.0, done: render statistics (oanim.wm.set_render_stats, render_stats; off by default): each render's CPU time, preparing, bones and modifiers, making drawings summed over the threads (outlines, cutting fills, fans, strokes, and masks' shapes), and by kind of draw (batches, fill fans, covers, mask passes, mask depth, composites, ...) the GPU's time from timer queries, the CPU's time in the calls and how many; and tools/render_bench/render_bench.py, which reads them from private copies: redraw (a frame rendered to a file again and again), bend (every bone swung by a driver, played: every weighted point moves every frame) and zoom (the top layers' scale swung, played). Found: Rainbow Dash's redraw at 1920x1080, 16 samples, is held by the GPU (about 40 ms), mostly passes over the whole image: masks' stencil passes 11 to 14 ms, GPU Geometry's mask depth passes 8 to 10, and the 48 faded groups' clears, resolves and composites about 15; the CPU then waits on whichever call comes next, which is why GPU Geometry's draw calls looked slow. Stencil fans are now indexed triangles from one middle vertex (a triangle fan drawn by MultiDrawArrays cost the driver about 40 microseconds a fill). Bending Clay013 and clay015 (107 and 113 bones driven): 22 to 30 ms of CPU a frame, of which masks' shapes, made one at a time as they are drawn, 9 to 10 ms; clay015 then plays at about 17 frames a second. Rainbow Dash scaled: 41 ms of CPU a frame, of which bones and modifiers worked out one layer after another 11.6. Strokes and flattening are 4 to 6 ms of making a bent frame. So next: masks' shapes made with the rest, before drawing and over the cores; bones and modifiers worked out over the cores; and passes over the whole image kept to what they touch. Masks' shapes are now made before drawing, with the rest, over the cores: before a render draws, every mask its steps will work out (a faded group's too) is walked through with nothing drawn, and the shapes not kept already are made as jobs (their mask's options going with each job, so any thread can make them). Pixel for pixel the same in both renderers. Bending Clay013: 28 to 19 ms of CPU a frame Classic, 22 to 16 GPU Geometry; clay015 30 to 22 and 25 to 18; the walk costs Rainbow Dash about 2 ms a frame. Working bones and modifiers out over the cores, a layer a thread, was tried and dropped: a frame needs few layers worked out (clay015 bending: 34 layers, about 3 ms in all), and Rainbow Dash's 11.6 ms is one layer, the skirt's Merged Layer with its deform modifiers (11 to 17 ms), whose own work is already split over the cores and ran on one core inside a worker (Rainbow Dash's preparing went from 21 to 37 ms). Passes over the whole image are now kept to what they touch (a scissor): a mask level is worked out, and in GPU Geometry written into the depth or let go of, only over its masked layer's region, the pixels that layer and everything inside it draw this render (its kept steps' bounds where it is drawn, three pixels more all round; the whole image where any of it isn't known before drawing, such as media or a drawing made as it is drawn), and a layer beside the last one drawn whose region lies inside where the held masks were worked out uses them as they are. A faded group is laid over sample for sample over its region, with no image resolved and nothing laid over the rest. Pixel for pixel the same in both renderers but Rainbow Dash's faded groups (2,041 pixels, by 8 of 255 at most: laid over per sample, not resolved first). Rainbow Dash's redraw at 1920x1080, 16 samples: 43 to 47 ms of GPU to 14 (GPU Geometry), 35 to 12 Classic; clay015 12 to 5 and 8.5 to 2; Rainbow Dash scaled 22 to 11 and 18.5 to 9. Bending plays as fast as before (there the GPU was already waiting on the CPU). While a zoom moves, a drawing drawn as made for another zoom (what stands in until its own is made) gives its region by what stands in, as does one whose own is still to be made, so the passes stay bounded then too: Rainbow Dash zooming at 2364x1611, 4 samples, 22 to 28 ms of GPU a frame to 7 or 8. And while only the view changes (a zoom or a pan drawn anew: nothing else the viewport checks has, the frame, revisions, undo steps, media, local view, mask preview, brushes), the viewport's renderer keeps each drawing's evaluation and what its key holds but the view (its geometry's hash, its brushes, the lines ended at masks' edges) from the render before, so a frame only finds the pixel size and looks its drawing up; not while live physics moves bones or a text is typed on the canvas. Rainbow Dash zooming with Zoom Preview off, a frame: 24 ms of CPU to 14; the frames after, making the new zoom's drawings, 39 to 28.5. A line thinner than half a pixel is drawn half a pixel wide and as much fainter by the GPU: each stroke vertex carries where it lies from the line's centre and the stroke's widest half width, and the shader moves it out along that (the CPU made a wider copy for each zoom before), so a stroke's triangles serve every zoom. A colour drawing in the viewport (not brushed, no lines ended at masks' edges) is then made for a band of zooms 16 times wide (ZOOM_BAND_OCTAVES): its curves cut finely enough for the band's nearest zoom, a stencil fill's cover reaching for its farthest, and drawn as it is across the band; masks' shapes, brushed lines and final renders keep steps of 2^(1/8). Rainbow Dash (the open skirt.oanim, Zoom Preview off), zooming 3, 6 and 12 notches in and out: 3,531 drawings remade to 233, a frame's CPU 18.7 ms to 10.2 (median), private memory after it 2.2 GB to 1.2 (the vertices are a half bigger: 1.1 GB on opening, from 1.0); making a drawing and the GPU's time a frame the same. Final renders unchanged; hairlines differ by a sample or so (Rainbow Dash: 276 pixels, 16 of 255 at most), and the viewport's curves where handles stretch them are cut a little finer. A frame whose view has just moved (a zoom, a pan past the margin) draws every layer straight into the view, with no layer run's image made of it and no signatures worked out (the next frame most likely moves the view again); the first frame at a view that holds makes the runs. A faded group's or layer's own image is cleared only within its region when it is laid back per sample there. Rainbow Dash, zooms and pans driven as a mouse makes them (no script running between frames, which gives every layer a new revision): a zoom frame's CPU 8.7 ms to 5.1 and GPU 7.0 to 4.9 (clears 2.0 to 0.2, composites 1.1 to 0.1), a pan frame's 12.6 to 5.7 and 7.3 to 4.2. With the CPU and the GPU about even, replaying the last frame's draw list (no walk of the layers) would save CPU time but no frame time, so it wasn't built. A vertex is packed in 20 bytes as the GPU reads it, its colour a byte a channel and a stroke's offset and widest half width 16-bit floats (36 bytes before; a batch's vertex 40 to 20). Rainbow Dash: private memory on opening 1,103 MB to 997, after zooming 1,213 to 1,064; 8 pixels differ, by 3 of 255 at most (the colour rounded to bytes). render_check gains a Boolean case |
| Action bones fight IK and constraints | Moho samples actions after posing; order mistakes cause jitter | Follow the evaluation order in the architecture section; write regression tests from small rigs |
| UI work balloons | Blender's UI toolkit is huge | Start with Dear ImGui docking; keep panels in Python so the toolkit can change later |
| Python binding grows hard to maintain | Hand-written bindings for every class add up | Generate bindings from the reflection registry, as Blender's RNA does |
| Scope creep | 63 features is years of work | Ship each milestone; move features between tiers instead of adding them to v1 |
| Reference layers lose their link or gain stray keys | Advanced masking depends on references staying in sync; Moho users report both bugs | Store a per-channel override flag; test sync, override and break on nested groups in milestone 2 |

**Open questions**

- Your Moho pros list was cut off after "all the tools are o…". What was the rest?
- Should version 1 open Moho files? Moho's format is not documented, so the spec assumes no.

**Where to look in the reference material**

| Topic | Blender source (`blender-main/blender/source/blender/`) | Moho material (`Moho/`) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Action bones | `blenkernel/intern/constraint.cc` (`actcon_evaluate`, the Action constraint) | Smart-bone snippet in `MohoDocs/mohoscripting_text.txt`; `lm_select_bone.lua` |
| Operators, G and E | `windowmanager/WM_types.hh`; `editors/transform/transform_ops.cc`; `editors/grease_pencil/intern/grease_pencil_edit.cc` | Tool lifecycle on the script-structure page |
| Keymaps | `scripts/presets/keyconfig/keymap_data/blender_default.py` (outside `source/`) | Modifier keys listed in each tool's description |
| Split views | `editors/screen/screen_edit.cc`, `screen_geometry.cc`, `screen_ops.cc` | — |
| Drawings and mirror modifier | `makesdna/DNA_grease_pencil_types.h`; `modifiers/intern/MOD_grease_pencil_mirror.cc` | `M_Mesh`, `M_Point`, `M_Curve`, `M_Shape` pages; `sz_auto_mirror` |
| Bones and custom shapes | `makesdna/DNA_armature_types.h`; `draw/engines/overlay/overlay_armature.cc` | `M_Bone`, `M_Skeleton` pages; `sz_symmetrize_bones` |
| Bone deform | `blenkernel/intern/armature_deform.cc` | Flexi-binding methods on `MohoLayer` |
| Keyframes and interpolation | `blenkernel/intern/fcurve.cc` (key storage, handle types, Bezier evaluation, about lines 2000–2440); `editors/space_graph/`; `editors/space_action/` | `InterpSetting` page and `INTERP_*` constants (the modes, hold, interval, stagger); `AnimChannel` pages |
| Python API | `python/intern/bpy_rna.cc`; `scripts/modules/_bpy_types.py` | Lua tool scripts in `moho ai/` and `sz_*` |
| Theme colours | `release/datafiles/userdef/userdef_default_theme.c` (outside `source/`) | — |
